# Changelog of the libretro core

What changed in the libretro core of this fork, newest first. Changes that come with a merge of upstream RPCS3 are in upstream's own history; a merge is noted here with the upstream build it brought in.

## Unreleased

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): "LOD/Texture Detail" stretches the model distances in steps instead of always drawing the most detailed version.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** on "Highest" a far character was drawn with parts missing that the game's own choice shows (the user's two screenshots of the same view, patch on and off). The patch made the LOD pick return the most detailed level at any distance, and that version of a model is evidently not made to be drawn from far away. Why its parts go missing there has not been found.
  - **What changed:** the setting now has four values: "Game Default", "High", "Very High" and "Highest". The three above the default halve the distance the pick is given once, twice or three times, so each version of a model is kept 2, 4 or 8 times as far and the far ones are still used beyond that. The water at full resolution is unchanged and comes with all three. The setting is still one instruction; its low two bits are the water's shift and the rest the number of halvings.
    - Not known until it is run: how far "Highest" can go before the same parts go missing; that is what the two lower steps are for.
  - **Tests:** the patch applied to the decrypted executable with three of the settings and the changed code read back through a disassembler; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): Unlock FPS's three movement numbers follow each frame's time.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** the pull down a slope, ground friction and the momentum kept in the air are applied once a frame, and Unlock FPS set them once, for 60 frames a second. At 120 (VBlank frequency 120 Hz) they were applied twice as often as they were set for: a roll or a walk off a ledge fell short again, and slopes and friction were twice as strong.
  - **What changed:** "Unlock FPS" (version 1.6) writes the three each frame from the frame's real time, beside the stuck test's numbers: the slope's pull times the frame time in 30ths of a second, and 0.65 and 0.95 to that power. The three fixed values are gone from the patch.
  - **Tests:** the patch applied to the decrypted executable (the three words keep the game's values until the first frame) and the new code read back through a disassembler; the series used is within 0.001 of the real power up to a 50 ms frame; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): no more slow run after touching a wall at high frame rates (Unlock FPS).** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** at 120 frames a second (VBlank frequency 120 Hz), running into a wall and away from it left the character moving slowly until the stick was let go. The movement code has a test for "stuck" (0x004855F0): a frame's move times 30 is the speed, and under 1 the character's speed factor is multiplied by 0.8 a frame down to 0.5; otherwise by 1.2 up to 1. The 30 is the game's frame rate. Against a wall the factor drops to 0.5; away from it, at 120 frames a second a frame's move at half speed times 30 is still under 1, so the test keeps failing and the factor never comes back. The frame rate where that starts depends on the run's speed (about 95 for a run of about 6.3 units a second). The PC game has the same bug, fixed the same way by the PC frame rate mods ("graze check").
  - **What changed:** "Unlock FPS" (version 1.5) works the three numbers out each frame from the frame's real time (1 / the frame time, and 0.8 and 1.2 to the power of the frame time in 30ths of a second, so slowing and recovery take as long as at 30) and the test loads them in place of its constants. Until the first frame has set them it uses the game's own.
  - **Tests:** the patch applied to the decrypted executable, the three changed loads and the new code read back through a disassembler; the power series used for 0.8^x and 1.2^x is within 0.0005 of the real value up to x = 1.5; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): "Skip Intro Logos" patch.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** a new patch in the Patch Manager, like Demon's Souls' "Skip Intro Videos". The three logos at startup are one dialog with a small state machine (fade in, hold, fade out, next logo); after the third it closes itself, which lets the title screen follow. The patch makes its step go straight to that closing code (one branch at 0x00AA2814).
    - Not known until it is run: whether a logo shows for a frame before the dialog is gone.
  - **Tests:** the patch applied to the decrypted executable and the branch read back; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): "LOD/Texture Detail" on "Highest" also draws the water's reflection and surface at full resolution.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What the game does:** the scene mirrored in water is drawn into a target a quarter of the screen's width and height (256x180 at the game's 1024x720), with a depth buffer of that size; only the mirror pass (0x003755BC) draws into them and only the water reads the result (0x0036D7D0). The water's surface is drawn into a 512x360 target of its own, over the half-size depth buffer the effects also use, which it clears first.
  - **What changed on "Highest":** the mirror's colour target and depth buffer are made at the screen's size (the shift by 2 where they are made is by 0), and the surface's target is 1024x720 (its size is doubled where the game copies it at startup, so the target and the size its shader is told both follow). The surface's pass needs a depth buffer as big as its target, so it takes the mirror's, full-size too now, in place of the effects' half-size one; both passes clear it before they draw. "Game Default" leaves all of it as it was. The setting is one instruction (`li r0, N`) used at each place, so the two values differ in nothing else.
    - Not known until it is run: that the passes take their viewport from the target they are given (nothing in either pass sets a size of its own, as far as read), that the mirror pass clears its depth buffer itself, and that the video memory has room for the three bigger buffers (about 8 MB more).
  - **Tests:** the patch applied to the decrypted executable with each setting, every changed word and all three pieces of new code read back through a disassembler; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): "LOD/Texture Detail" patch, with characters and objects always at their most detailed model on "Highest".** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Why:** the game swaps characters and objects to simpler versions of their model as they get further from the camera, which shows as detail dropping in and out.
  - **What changed:** a new patch in the Patch Manager with one setting, "Game Default" (nothing changes) or "Highest". The model's LOD pick (0x001DDFF0) compares the distance with the area's LOD_BANK row (two distances, each with a margin so the level does not flicker) and returns level 0, 1 or 2; on "Highest" it returns 0 at once.
    - Not in it yet: the water. Its surface is drawn into a 512x360 target (made at 0x00378734 from two numbers in the game's data) and the mirrored scene into a quarter-size one, each over a depth buffer of its own small size, so the size cannot be raised by changing those numbers alone.
  - **Tests:** the patch applied to the decrypted executable with each setting and read back; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): the bonfire softlock is caught by what it is, inside "Unlock FPS".** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** with "Unlock FPS", after Reverse Hollowing the character stays seated every time the bonfire menu is left with circle. The "Bonfire Softlock Watchdog" patch never stepped in. A reading of the game's state in the softlock (taken on the user's machine) shows why: the bonfire script's seated byte is already 0 and no menu is open, so the script has ended normally and the watchdog, which waited for "seated byte set, no menu", had nothing to see. It was also a separate switch that was off.
  - **What changed:** the separate patch is gone and "Unlock FPS" (version 1.4) carries the check. Each frame it reads the player's loop animation id (the word the script's `PlayLoopAnimation` writes at 0x003EF8A8 and `StopLoopAnimation` sets to -1 at 0x003EF778; the player is found as that native finds it). One of the three seated loops (7701, 7711, 7721, the ids the PC fixes look for) still set while the script's seated byte is 0 is the softlock. When that has lasted a second, the character's own stop of its loop animation (0x003EF684) is called, the function `StopLoopAnimation` calls. No menu words and no prompt timing are read any more.
    - Not known until it is run: that the loop id is what is left set in the softlock (the PC fixes clear the same id there, and the seated byte reading fits it, but it was not read on this build), and whether a warp from a bonfire can meet the condition for a second before the screen is dark (the character would stand up early).
  - **Tests:** the patch applied to the decrypted executable and read back, every word of the new code checked against a disassembler; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **A refused save state says in the log what the game's threads were doing.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Why:** in Dark Souls, with SPU Compatible Mode on, Save State is refused every time with "Game saving is in progress". RPCS3 shows that for any system call that holds its savestate guard, not only a game save, and nothing said which one. Reading the code did not find a call that keeps the guard.
  - **What changed:** when a state is refused, the line in the frontend's log now gives the guard's count and, for each of the game's PPU threads, the system call it is in (or was last in). Only on that path; nothing is added while the game runs.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes.

- **Core option: Save States: SPU Compatible Mode.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** Save State in Dark Souls failed with "Failed to lock SPU state, enabling SPU-Compatible mode may fix it", and nothing in the core could enable it: RPCS3's Compatible Savestate Mode was not among the core options.
  - **What changed:** a core option for it, off by default as in RPCS3 (it costs some SPU speed). It takes effect at the next content load, and SPU code is compiled again for it.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes. Whether a state can then be made in Dark Souls has not been seen.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): a bonfire watchdog in place of 30 FPS at the bonfire.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Why:** dropping to 30 FPS while seated was a workaround, and not a sure one: the softlock (the bonfire menu does not come back, the character stays seated) is in the game at any frame rate, only rarer at 30; Remastered has it at its native 60. What the PC fixes do (FPSFix, FPSFix+, DS1-FPS-Unlock) is watch for it and stand the character up.
  - **What changed:** "Unlock FPS" stays at 60 FPS at the bonfire again, and its three movement numbers are plain data patches now. A new patch, "Bonfire Softlock Watchdog", looks each frame at the game's own state: the talk script's seated byte, and the menu manager's words for the bonfire menu, the yes/no prompt and the bonfire's sub-menus (the ones the game's `IsMenuOpen` reads; the same eight the PC fixes watch, at the same offsets). Seated with none of them open for 4 seconds, and at least 9 seconds after a Kindle or Reverse Hollowing prompt, it does what the script's own leave does: calls the event script's `Lua_BonfireLoopAnimEnd` and clears the seated byte. It hooks the instruction after the frame time's load, so it works with Unlock FPS on or off.
    - Not known until it is run: whether the seated byte is still set in the softlock (if the script cleared it, the watchdog never fires), and whether a warp from the bonfire, seated with no menu while the next area loads, can trip it.
  - **Tests:** both patches applied together to the decrypted executable and read back (the two hooks chain: the frame-time cave returns into the watchdog's); `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): falls and slides at 60 FPS as at 30 with Unlock FPS.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** with Unlock FPS a roll or a walk off a ledge fell short - the timed roll onto the pillar on the way back to the Undead Asylum could not be made. The PC version had the same fault with an unlocked frame rate. The movement code multiplies the momentum kept in the air by 0.95 once a frame, whatever the frame takes, so at 60 frames a second it faded twice as fast. Ground friction (0.65) and the pull down a slope (1.0) are applied once a frame the same way.
  - **What changed:** on this console build all three numbers are in the game's data (0x01886C30, 0x019F48C4, 0x019F48C0), so the patch's per-frame code sets them with the flip mode: 0.95 ^ 0.5, 0.65 ^ 0.5 and 0.5 at 60 FPS, the game's own at the bonfire's 30. Found by following the PC fix in zofrea1's DS1-FPS-Unlock (its air damping, slide friction and slide gravity sites) into this executable.
    - Not covered: the other per-frame effects that project lists for the PC version (camera smoothing, gauge fill speed, some timers); and frames slower than 60 FPS still use the 60 FPS numbers.
  - **Tests:** applied to the decrypted executable with the core's patch parser and read back; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: two audio latency patches in the Patch Manager.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What they are:** Dark Souls uses the same sound engine as Demon's Souls (MagicOrchestra over FMOD), but its built-in setup text has none of the mixer keys Demon's Souls' "Reduce Audio Latency" changes, and its output ring is already the smallest (8 blocks).
    - "Double Sound Update Rate" (all Dark Souls serials the text is found in): the engine's `MainThreadUpdateTime` goes from 33333 to 16666 microseconds, rewritten in the setup text as the executable loads, so it does not depend on the build of the game.
    - "Reduce Audio Latency" (BLES01765, the executable `PPU-d764d35c...`): FMOD's PS3 output mixes 7 blocks (37 ms) ahead of the block being played, the most the ring allows. A "Blocks Ahead" value sets that: 7 (the game's), 6, 5 (the default), 4 or 3. It is one instruction at 0x000B3550. Too few blocks will break the sound up; that is what the choice is for.
  - **Tests:** both applied to the decrypted executable and read back. `test_game_text_patch` has the case of a key rewritten as itself; `test_patch_options` and `test_game_patches` pass.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): 30 FPS at the bonfire with Unlock FPS.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** with Unlock FPS (which runs, confirmed on the user's machine) the bonfire menu could close after kindling with the character left sitting, unable to get up, sound and picture carrying on. The PC version has the same fault with unlocked frame rates (its "bonfire input glitch"), and does not have it at 30.
  - **What changed:** the patch's per-frame code now also sets the flip mode: the retail 30 FPS mode while the bonfire's talk script has the character seated, 60FPS(NO SKIP) otherwise. It reads the game's own flag for that, a byte the talk script's StartBonfireAnimLoop command sets (0x006FD1C0) and its EndBonfireKindleAnimLoop clears (0x006FD230), named in Smithbox's catalogue of DS1 talk commands. The frame time already follows the real time between frames, so nothing else changes speed.
    - Not known: whether every way of leaving a bonfire (a warp, say) clears that flag. If one does not, the game would stay at 30 until the next bonfire is left normally.
  - **Tests:** applied to the decrypted executable with the core's patch parser and read back; `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition (EU): Unlock FPS for the disc's executable.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** the Patch Manager offered "Unlock FPS" for BLES01765 and turning it on did nothing. RPCS3's patch is for one build of the game (`PPU-03679186...`); the executable here is another (`PPU-d764d35c...`), where the patched addresses hold other code, so the patch engine rightly did not apply it.
  - **What changed:** the core's own patch file has the patch for this executable, under the same name, so the option already shown (and already on) now applies. The same two changes, found again in this build: the game's 60FPS(NO SKIP) flip mode set where its object is made (0x00149CDC), and the fixed 1/30 s step at 0x00145A18 replaced by the time since the last frame, kept between 4 ms and 50 ms. Both in caves the patch engine allocates; the last frame's time is kept in the unused end of the executable's data.
  - **Tests:** applied to the decrypted executable with the core's patch parser and the code read back: both hooks branch to their caves and the caves disassemble as written. `test_game_patches` passes.

- **PPU analyser: a function's blocks and a module's segments in sorted arrays; analysis about a quarter faster.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** every PPU function kept its basic blocks in a `std::map<u32, u32>` (address to size), and a module its segments in another. A game has tens of thousands of functions, most with one block: a tree node allocated for each. They are in `util/addr_sizes.c` now (C89): the pairs sorted in one array, the first two inside the list itself, so most functions allocate nothing. A module's memory is found through its segment list on every instruction the analyser reads; that lookup is a search of a few words now, not a tree walk.
    - The analyser's large working maps (`fmap`, `addr_heap`) are left: thousands of entries added in no order is what a sorted array is wrong for.
  - **Measured:** analysing Demon's Souls' executable takes about 1.3 s, from about 1.7 s (six runs each, alternated), and finds the same functions and blocks (same count, same crc32 of every function's address, TOC, size and blocks).
  - **Tests:** `test_addr_sizes` checks the list against a model over 200,000 random steps. `test_llvm_jit` runs the PPU analyser on an executable named in `PPU_ANALYSE_ELF` (none is in the tree) and prints what it found and how long it took.

- **SPU LLVM recompiler: function chunks found by address, block edges in a small list.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the recompiler's function chunks (`m_functions`) were a `std::unordered_map` by address and each block carried another one for its edge blocks (`block_edges`), constructed and destroyed with every block though most blocks have none. The chunks are a `pc_map` now (`util/pc_slots.c`) and the edges a `util/pair_list.c` list, which allocates nothing until a block has more than two.
    - The blocks themselves (`m_blocks`) are left as they were: the order that map is walked in decides the order register stores are moved between blocks, so changing it changes the code that comes out (same size, different bytes). That is a change to make on its own, with a game run.
  - **Tests:** `test_llvm_jit` passes; the 200 plain and 900 branching programs compile to the same bytes as before.

- **Closing content no longer waits for good on a stop that cannot finish.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What was wrong:** closing content after a game had crashed could hang RetroArch. The backtrace shows the frontend's thread in `stop_emulation`, waiting for the emulator to become "stopped", with every emulation thread already gone. That state is set at the end of the thread that finishes a stop ("Emulation Join Thread"); if that thread ends on a fatal error part way, nothing ever sets it. Which error it ends on after a crash is not known yet - the hang left no log.
  - **What changed:** a fatal error on that thread now lets go of whoever waits for the stop, with a line in the log saying the stop did not finish. Every fatal error in the frontend's log now also names the thread it came from (`RPCS3: [thread name] ...`), so the next log says what the stop thread ended on.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes. Checked against the user's DLL that the hung wait is this one (the return address in `stop_emulation` is the loop that reads the emulator's state).

- **Build warnings: five gone.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** no change in behaviour. `SPUThread.cpp` no longer declares `__movsb` again under MinGW, whose own headers define it. `lv2_socket.cpp` loses a counter nothing read. Four casts now go by way of `void*` and stop tripping `-Wstrict-aliasing`: the sleep queue link store in `sys_sync.h`, and one each in `JITASM.cpp`, `HLE_PATCHES.cpp` and `cellVdec.cpp`.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes with no warnings under GCC on Linux. The MinGW `__movsb` change was checked with a small MinGW compile, not a full MinGW build.

- **SPU analyser: its working tables found by address too; analysis about 30% faster.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the pattern search in `spu_recompiler_base::analyse` kept seven more `std::map`s for the length of one analysis: each block's register state (`infos`), the atomic, channel-read and reduced-loop findings, the GETLLAR starts, and two maps of `bool` used as "seen this block" marks. The first five are `pc_map` now (`util/pc_slots.c`, as the blocks and functions are); the two marks are plain bit arrays. The table's first slab is small now, so one that holds a few entries takes little.
  - **Measured:** 3000 analyses of branching programs take about 700 ms, from about 1000 ms (three runs each, alternated).
  - **Tests:** `test_llvm_jit`'s checks of the analyser's dump pass, and the 900 branching programs compile to the same bytes as before (the test now prints the pack's size and crc32 after them; same before and after).

- **SPU analyser: basic blocks, functions and patterns found by address, no `std::map`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the analyser's blocks (`m_bbs`), functions (`m_funcs`) and patterns (`m_patterns`) were each a `std::map` by address: a tree node allocated per entry, every lookup a walk down the tree, all of it freed after each function. They are in `util/pc_slots.c` now (C89): an entry is found by its address in 256-entry leaves, and the entries are cut from slabs that are kept between functions. Order is the same as the map's (rising address), and an entry never moves, so references held across insertions stay good as they did.
  - **Measured:** no speed difference. 3000 analyses of branching programs take about 1040 ms before and after (five runs each, alternated); the time goes elsewhere in the analyser. What this removes is the allocations and the STL containers.
  - **Tests:** `test_pc_slots` checks the table against a model. `test_llvm_jit` now also analyses 300 programs with branches, calls and mid-function returns at each block size and checks the analyser's own dump against checksums taken from the code before either change; all 300 compile at each size.

- **SPU analyser: branch targets and predecessors in plain per-address lists.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** analysing an SPU function keeps, for each address, the list of addresses it branches to (`m_targets`) and the list it is reached from (`m_preds`). Both were a `std::unordered_map` of `std::vector`: a node and a vector allocated per address, all of it freed and allocated again for the next function. They are in `util/pc_lists.c` now (C89): a list per SPU address, found by index in 256-entry leaves, with no hashing. Clearing between functions keeps every leaf and every list's memory, so a compiler thread stops allocating for these once it has seen a few functions.
    - The lists are walked in rising address order now, where the hash map's order was arbitrary. The one loop that depends on order reaches the same result either way.
  - **Tests:** `test_pc_lists` checks the table against a model over 300,000 random steps. `test_llvm_jit` compiles its 200 SPU functions to the same bytes as before (same size, same crc32).

- **Renderer upload buffers call their allocator directly, with no `std::function`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** `rsx::io_buffer` (`Common/io_buffer.h`) is the buffer a texture, a set of transform constants or an instancing table is written into, its memory taken from a ring buffer when it is first asked for. The allocator was held as a `std::function`: built from the caller's lambda and copied again for every buffer, and allocated on the heap when the lambda captures more than two things - the texture upload's captures four, so every texture level uploaded paid two allocations and frees. The buffer now keeps a pointer to the caller's own lambda and calls it through a plain function: nothing built, copied or allocated.
    - The lambda has to outlive the buffer, which it does at every use. Passing a temporary no longer compiles; four places that did (the Vulkan and OpenGL instancing tables) name their lambdas first.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes.

- **Texture cache: a frame's views of composed textures in a plain table.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** a texture put together from several surfaces gets an image view made for it, kept for the rest of the frame in a `std::unordered_multimap` by address. Each kept view copied the whole request (its list of copies included, another allocation), a node was allocated for it, and all of it was freed at the end of every frame. The views are in `util/view_cache.c` now (C89): one open-addressed table of plain entries by address and properties, holding only what a later lookup reads (the view, its channel remap, the memory it was made from). The end of the frame empties it and keeps its memory, so a frame allocates nothing.
    - A view taken out in the frame (its surface changed) is marked dead where it sits; a table worn down that way is rebuilt at the same size.
    - Measured alone, with every view asked for four times a frame: about 23 to 27 ns a request in the multimap, 5 to 8 ns here, for 8, 64 and 256 views.
    - The lookup reads what it needs from an entry before updating a view's contents, which can add entries and move them.
  - **Tests:** `test_view_cache` covers finding by address and properties together, growth, taking out and keeping again, a worn table rebuilt without growing or losing any, emptying with the memory kept, and freeing. It builds as strict C89 with MinGW. A grep in the test Makefile keeps the multimap out.

- **Z-cull reports: locked pages found by address, not in hash maps.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** each page a pending occlusion report is to be written to is locked, and counted, in a `std::unordered_map` for its memory location - looked up for every report queued and again for every report done. A page now has a 32-bit word found by its address in two loads (`util/page_words.c`, C89: leaves of 1024 pages, allocated when first used). The word says whether the page is locked for reports, whether it is protected, and how many reports are still to be written.
    - Measured alone, queueing a report's page goes from about 6 ns to 2.5 ns, for 2, 8 or 64 pages alike.
    - A sorted array was tried first and was slower than the hash map from 8 pages up, so it was dropped.
    - The count other threads read with no lock is stored when a page comes or goes, no longer on every report.
    - The set of addresses in `copy_reports_to` is a sorted array (`util/addr_map.c`), no hash set.
  - **Tests:** `test_page_words` covers pages found and not found, a page's every address, neighbours, both ends of the address space, words staying put as leaves are added, and freeing. It builds as strict C89 with MinGW. A grep in the test Makefile keeps hash maps out of `RSXZCULL`.

- **Surface cache: surfaces taken out of use are kept in a list with its own nodes.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the render target cache kept the surfaces taken out of use in a `std::list`: a node allocated for each one and freed when it was reused or dropped. They are in `util/node_list.c` now (C89), a doubly linked list whose nodes come from the list's own blocks and are used again.
    - It keeps what the code around it relies on, exactly as the `std::list` did: the order of adding, and elements that stay where they are while others are added or erased. That is why it is a linked list and not an array: the code holds a reference to the last one while it goes on working, and an array would move it. (The render target bug of 0631 was an entry that moved.)
    - A surface put in and taken out again measures about 21 ns in the `std::list` and 6 ns here.
  - **Tests:** `test_node_list` covers the order both ways, nodes staying put through adds and erases, erasing at the front, middle and end, erased nodes used again before a new block, erasing while walking, and freeing. It builds as strict C89 with MinGW. A grep in the test Makefile keeps the `std::list` out.

- **Frame statistics: framebuffer sizes in six slots, not a hash map.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the largest framebuffer seen for each antialiasing mode was kept in a `std::unordered_map` (`Core/RSXDisplay.h`): hashed on every framebuffer bound, and its nodes allocated again when each frame's statistics were copied for the flip. The modes are the values 0, 3, 4 and 5, so it is an array of six with a bit for each one in use; the statistics are plain data again, copied without allocating.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes.

- **The renderers' memory-mapping blocks are found by address, not in a hash map.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the Vulkan and OpenGL renderers keep a block for each 64 KiB stretch of guest memory they have mapped for transfers (`VKDMA.cpp`, `GLDMA.cpp`), in a `std::unordered_map` keyed by the block's address. Each is now a table with one slot for each of the 65,536 blocks of the guest's 4 GiB, indexed by the address: a shift and a load.
    - OpenGL looked its block up in the map on every mapping. Measured alone, that lookup goes from about 5.4 ns to 0.6 ns.
    - Vulkan already answers repeat mappings from its table of views without the map; this is its first-use path and its unmapping, and the map's node allocations there.
    - The Vulkan checks that compared the map's size with the memory allocated use a count of the slots in use.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes. A grep in the test Makefile keeps the hash maps out.

- **Demon's Souls: a "Shadow Map Size" patch in the Patch Manager.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the game's shadow map size setting (2048 as shipped) can be set as the game starts: 4096, 3072, 2048 or 1024. It is the value the debug menu's GAME REND > Shadow > "ShadwoMap Size" shows, which the game reads only where an area's shadow camera is made - so the menu could not change it for the area already loaded.
    - The shadow filter is worked out from this number. Whether the shadow buffer itself grows past 2048 with it is not known yet: the patch's notes say so, and to go back to 2048 if shadows look wrong.
  - **Tests:** applied to the game's executable, the setting reads 4096. `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Demon's Souls "In-Game Debug Menu": the shadow map size entry goes up to 4096.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** GAME REND > Shadow > "ShadwoMap Size" stopped at 2048, which is also the game's own setting. Its highest value is now 4096 (one instruction at 0x0021CA8C). The game reads the size when an area's shadow camera is made, so a change shows after the next area load. Whether the game's shadow buffer follows the number above 2048 is not known yet: that is what trying it will show. Patch version 1.2.
  - **Tests:** `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Demon's Souls "Fixed Frame Pacing" counts vblanks, so a slow frame no longer slows the game.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** each game frame advanced a fixed 1/60 or 1/120 s. When the machine could not finish that many frames a second the game ran in slow motion, with the frame counter still at 120 (it counts frames presented, not frames the game finished) and the audio unaffected. Each frame now advances by the vblanks that passed since the last one: a frame that misses a vblank advances two vblanks' time, and the game keeps its speed.
    - The count is the game's own: its vblank handler (0x009F7018) adds one to a counter (0x01B50434) every emulated vblank. Unlock FPS's frame time routine at 0x016C7C30 is written again to read it in place of the host clock (21 instructions, the same space), keeping Unlock FPS's 50 ms a frame limit. Time still comes from the emulated vblank alone: the same every run.
    - The frame rate chosen must be the VBlank Frequency: "Auto" sees to that.
    - Patch version 1.1.
  - **Tests:** applied with the upstream "Unlock FPS" to the game's executable and disassembled: the routine, its two constants and Unlock FPS's hook are in place. `test_game_patches` passes.

- **VBlank Frequency has an "Auto" value, the default: it follows the Patch Manager.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** running a game at 120 FPS took two settings that had to agree: the patch's frame rate and the VBlank Frequency core option. "Auto" is 60 Hz, or the frame rate a patch that is on is set to when that is higher: the picked choice of a patch value named for it ("Frame Rate", "FPS"), by the number in its label ("120 FPS") or its value. Demon's Souls "Fixed Frame Pacing" at 120 FPS now needs nothing else set.
    - It is read as the game loads, like the patches themselves: a patch changed in play applies, with its rate, at the next start.
    - The fixed values (50 to 240 Hz) and "From List" work as before.
    - `patch_options_frame_rate` (`libretro_patch_options.c`, C89) does the reading.
  - **Tests:** `test_patch_options` covers it: no patch on, a label's number, a value without one, the higher of two, and values not named for a frame rate left alone.

- **Demon's Souls "In-Game Debug Menu": the debug memory zone is 8 MB, as in gibbed's patch.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the patch gave the debug menu a root node but left the game's debug memory zone at the retail 2 MB. gibbed's DemonsSoulsDebug raises it to 8 MB (three instructions at 0x0003A1FC, 0x0003A244 and 0x0003A2B0), and this patch had left that out. With a root node, each area load adds its debug pages from that zone: the reload after a death is the likely place it ran out, the game then using memory it never got ("The PS3 application has likely crashed"). The three instructions are now part of the patch (version 1.1).
  - **Tests:** `test_game_patches` passes.

- **Fatal errors reach the frontend's log.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** RPCS3's fatal messages went only to its own log file, which exists only with "Detailed Log" on. A game stopping with "The PS3 application has likely crashed", or a stop that hangs on closing content, left nothing to go by. Every fatal message now also goes to the frontend's log as an error. For a guest access violation, the faulting thread's state goes with it: its registers, the address and the call stack. Fatal messages are rare, so play costs nothing.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes.

- **A boot that fails while starting the renderer no longer hangs.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** while a game's emulator objects and renderer are made, new threads wait before running (`g_launch_retainer` in `init_fxo_for_exec`). When the renderer's start threw, that wait was never let go. The failed boot then stopped the emulator, whose stopping thread waited at its first line for good, and the frontend hung on opening content. The wait is now let go however the start ends, so the failure reaches the frontend's log ("RPCS3: booting ... threw: ...") and content loading fails cleanly.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes.

- **Texture cache: a flush-always range added twice keeps one entry.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** when the flush-always sections moved into a short list, adding a range that was already there made a second entry. The hash map it replaced put the new section in place of the old one. With two entries, removing the range left the old section in the list, and every draw then reprotected it even after it was gone. Adding a range now replaces its section if the range is there.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes.

- **Render targets: a surface is found again after its block is split.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** since render targets moved into short lists per 4 MB block (`util/pair_list.c`), an entry can move when its block gains or loses one. Binding a render target held a reference to its old entry while splitting the surfaces it overlaps, and that split adds to and takes from the same block. The entry was then read, released and erased at its old place: the wrong surface could be freed while still in use.
    - This is the likely cause of "Verification failed (object: 0x0)" stopping the emulation (a render target pointer that no longer points at a render target), and of the crash in `vk::surface_cache::destroy` on exit.
    - `bind_surface_address` (`Common/surface_store.h`) now looks the surface up again after each split, in both the primary and the other store.
  - **Tests:** the full build passes.

- **Patch Manager: Demon's Souls' in-game debug menu.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** a fourth Demon's Souls (US 1.00) patch in the core's own patch file, `libretro_patch.yml`: **In-Game Debug Menu**.
    - Select opens and closes the developers' debug menu in game. The retail game builds all its pages at boot but never draws them or reads input for them: HG MAN (graphics), MEM MAN, PERF MAN, Param Man, the character and camera pages and more.
    - While it is open, the game does not take the pad.
    - It is a port of gibbed's DemonsSoulsDebug runtime menu into two code caves the patch engine allocates:
      - **Building the menu:** one cave takes the place of the call at `0x00020F44` that builds the menu, and gives it its root node.
      - **Each frame:** the other takes the place of the empty function at `0x00015A50`, a lone `blr` the game calls every frame. It draws the menu and reads its input.
    - **Changes from gibbed's code:**
      - Each cave keeps a proper stack frame of its own. gibbed's wrapper wrote over its stack back-chain.
      - Calls into the game are absolute (`bla`), so they hold wherever the engine puts the cave.
  - **How it was checked:** the core's patch parser applied it alongside the other Demon's Souls patches and RPCS3's Unlock FPS, with no overlap. The caves were disassembled, and every branch inside them was checked to stay inside. Every call target was checked to be a function's start, and the menu's root label ("ROOT") and menu pointer were read from the executable.
  - **Tests:** `test_game_patches` reads every patch in the core's file, this one included. The full build and suites pass.


- **Patch Manager: the core's own game patches, starting with Demon's Souls.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A patch file of the core's own** (`rpcs3/libretro/libretro_patch.yml`) is compiled in, by both the Makefile and the CMake build. Like RPCS3's `patch.yml`, it is written to `patches/libretro_patch.yml` in the config folder when missing or different. The patch engine loads it after `patch.yml` (`append_global_patches`), and the Patch Manager lists its patches with the rest.
    - **Demon's Souls (US disc 1.00, BLUS30443)** gets three new patches:
      - **Title Debug Menu:** the title screen builds the developers' menu: Model Viewer, Map Select, FaceGen, SFX Test, Title List and two Title entries.
        - The address comes from gibbed's DemonsSoulsDebug.
        - The labels are rewritten in English and moved clear of the space where RPCS3's "Unlock FPS" keeps its timing code. With both on, three labels would otherwise have been overwritten with code.
      - **Disable Dynamic Exposure:** the debug menu's ToneMap switch is on from the start, so tone mapping keeps a fixed exposure instead of adapting each frame. This stops exposure flicker.
      - **Fixed Frame Pacing:** an add-on to "Unlock FPS"; on its own it changes nothing.
        - The game advances a fixed time per frame (60 or 120 FPS, a choice in the Patch Manager) instead of the measured host clock time, and waits one vblank per frame.
        - Pacing comes from the emulated vblank; a frame that runs late slows the game rather than skipping time.
        - That the game's wait value counts vblanks (2 at retail 30 FPS) is inferred, not confirmed.
  - **How the patches were checked:** they were applied to the decrypted executable with this core's patch parser (`Utilities/patch_db.c`), together with RPCS3's current "Unlock FPS". Nothing overlapped. The patched code was disassembled and the menu's labels read back. They have not run in the game.
  - **Tests:** `test_game_patches` now loads the core's file, checks every patch in it is read, and checks Demon's Souls (US) is offered Title Debug Menu and the Frame Rate choice. Leaving the file out of the Patch Manager's list fails it. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks of the changed files, the full build and suites pass.


- **Texture cache: flush-always sections in a short list, not a hash map.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The flush-always sections** are framebuffers the CPU reads back every frame, normally a few. They were kept in an `std::unordered_map` by address range, a node allocated for each.
    - **They are now in `util/key_list.c`:** the range's start and end are packed into one 64-bit key, and up to four are kept in the list itself with no allocation.
      - The per-frame update walks the pointers in one array.
      - Invalidation takes a section out by key: the last one moves into its place.
      - `key_list_remove` is new.
  - **Speed (this machine):**
    - **With 4 sections:** a lookup takes 3.0–3.3 ns, where the map took 5.0–5.3 ns. Taking one out and adding it back takes 3.9–4.7 ns, against 31–34 ns. A walk takes about the same, 1.5 ns.
    - **With 16:** walks take 5.4 ns against 10 ns, and taking out and adding back is 20 ns against 25 ns. A lookup is slower, 14 ns against 4.5 ns.
    - **With 64:** walks take 32–40 ns against 104–110 ns, and taking out and adding back is about even (28–33 ns against 20–25 ns). A lookup is slower, 24–26 ns against 5 ns.
    - An open-addressed table (`util/ptr_table.c`) was tried first. Its lookups were faster with many sections, but its walk took 10 ns against 1.3 ns with four.
  - **Smaller code:** 6.7 KB less over the Vulkan and OpenGL objects that build the texture cache.
  - **Tests:** `test_key_list` now takes entries out by key, from the middle and the end, and checks a missing key leaves the list alone. Dropping the move into the gap or the count decrement fails it. The flush-always sections themselves need a running renderer, which the tests do not have. The full build, the suites and the Windows (MinGW) syntax checks pass.


- **Texture cache: blocks in use kept as bits, not a hash set.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The texture cache** splits guest memory into 4096 blocks of 1 MB. It kept the blocks holding a texture section in an `std::unordered_set` of pointers. A block goes in when its first section is made and comes out when its last is destroyed.
    - **A new header-only C89 set** (`util/block_bits.h`) replaces it: one bit per block, plus a summary word with a bit for every 64 blocks that has any set.
      - Adding or taking out a block is a store or two, where the set hashed and allocated or freed a node each time.
      - The walks (purging unreleased or unlocked sections, trimming protection) go through the summary, so they only visit 64-block words with a bit set, in address order.
  - **Speed (this machine):**
    - **Adding and taking out a block:** 4.3–5.5 ns, where the set took 36–52 ns.
    - **A walk** with 126 blocks in use takes 140–160 ns against 215 ns; with 908, 750 ns against 1900 ns.
    - **With only 16 in use**, a walk is slower: 17–35 ns against 13 ns.
  - **Smaller code:** 2.8 KB less over the texture cache and renderer objects.
  - **Tests:** `test_block_bits` sets, clears and tests indices, including two in one word and the first and last. It checks the summary bit stays while any bit of its word is set and goes with the last. It walks the set as the texture cache does, checking every index comes out once in rising order, over 20,000 random steps. A summary left set, dropped too soon or never set, and a wrong clear mask each fail it. The full build, the suites and the Windows (MinGW) syntax checks of the Vulkan and OpenGL texture caches pass.


- **Vulkan and OpenGL: two per-draw hash lookups made array indexes.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Vulkan format properties** (`render_device::get_format_properties`): a draw asks this for each texture (whether its format can be filtered linearly), and render targets ask it when made. It used an `std::unordered_map`, filled on first use.
      - Every core format's properties (185 of them) are now asked of the driver once, when the device is made, into an array indexed by format.
      - The array is only read afterwards, so any thread may read it safely. The map was grown from whichever thread asked first, with nothing guarding it.
      - The renderer uses no extension format. One would be asked of the driver each time.
    - **OpenGL null textures** (the 1x1 textures bound in place of a missing one): looked up in an `std::unordered_map` by GL target for each unbound texture of a draw. They are now an array of four indexed by the texture's dimension, so the step that turned the dimension into a GL target is gone too.
  - **Speed:** a format lookup takes 0.4–0.5 ns, where the map took 10.4 ns (this machine, loop included).
  - **Smaller code:** 3.6 KB less over `device.o`, `GLGSRender.o` and `GLDraw.o`.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; both need a running Vulkan or OpenGL device, which the tests do not have. The guard here fails if either map comes back. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks of the four changed files, the full build and suites pass.


- **RSX: render target lookups faster at every block size up to 8.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Why:** with 0624, a 4 MB block holding 6 or 8 render targets was slower to search than the old hash map: 6.8 and 8.1 ns, against 5.0 and 5.2 ns.
  - **What changed** (`util/pair_list.c`):
    - **The keys** are kept in an array of their own, with the surfaces in a second array in the same allocation.
    - **The search** compares keys four at a time (SSE2 on x86, NEON on ARM, plain C elsewhere) into one bit mask. Bits past the count are masked off, then the first set bit gives the index.
      - Up to 8 keys this takes two loads, up to 16 four, and nothing branches on where the key is. Longer lists go 16 keys at a time.
      - The two kept inside the list itself are checked the same way.
    - **The heap holds at least 8 keys**, then a multiple of 16, so every load stays inside its own allocation.
  - **Speed (this machine, 1024 blocks, random hits and misses; earlier ones measured with keys that strayed into the next block, now fixed):**
    - **1 to 8 per block:** 3.0–4.2 ns (once 5.7 ns, at 2), where the map took 5.7–13 ns.
    - **6 per block:** 4.0 ns against 6.1 ns.
    - **8 per block:** 4.1 ns against 5.7 ns.
    - **One block searched over and over:** 2.7 ns against 4.5 ns at 4 to 8 per block, and 3.3 ns against 4.3–5.0 ns at 10 to 16.
    - **10 to 16 per block, spread over all blocks:** 6.5 ns against 5.7 ns. Past 16 it is slower: 12 ns at 24 against 5.7 ns. A 4 MB block holds that many only with many small targets.
  - **Tests:** `test_pair_list` now:
    - grows a list one key at a time to 70, checking every key is found and every other is not;
    - at every length to 40, checks that a key just taken off the end, still in memory past the count, is not found.
    - A missing mask on the 8-key, 16-key or long path, a wrong shift, or a pass of the wrong length each fail it.
    - It passed on ARM (NEON, under qemu) and on the plain C path, as well as SSE2. The full build, the suites and the Windows (MinGW) syntax checks pass.


- **RSX: render targets found without a hash map per 4 MB block.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The render target and depth stores** (`Common/ranged_map.hpp`, used by `surface_store.h` on every draw) split guest memory into 1024 blocks of 4 MB. Each block kept the surfaces starting in it in its own `std::unordered_map`. Each is now a short C89 list (`util/pair_list.c`).
      - The first two entries are kept in the list itself; more go on the heap.
      - A key is found by selects, with no branch on where it is, so its place does not have to be guessed.
      - Each surface's `unique_ptr` is kept in its entry and moved with it, with a compile-time check that it is pointer-sized.
    - **The iterator** now hands out the address and a reference to the surface, as the map's pairs did, so `surface_store.h` is unchanged. Taking an entry out moves the last one into its place, which the iterator looks at next, so every surface is still visited once.
    - **`ranged_map.hpp`** no longer uses `std::min` or `std::for_each`, so it no longer depends on `<algorithm>` coming in from elsewhere (the MinGW break 0623 fixed).
  - **Speed (this machine, finding a surface among 1024 blocks, random hits and misses):**
    - **1 surface per block:** 2.3–2.7 ns, where the map took 12–15 ns.
    - **2 per block:** 5.2–5.4 ns against 8.1–9.3 ns.
    - **3 to 4 per block:** about even (5–9 ns each).
    - **6 or 8 per block:** slower, 6.8 and 8.1 ns against 5.0 and 5.2 ns. A 4 MB block holds that many only with many small targets.
    - **Going through every block:** about the same or faster.
  - **Smaller code:** the ten Vulkan and OpenGL objects using the store are 12.9 KB smaller together.
  - **Tests:** `test_pair_list` covers two entries inline and more on the heap. It checks each entry's contents survive moves and that a reused slot comes back zeroed, then runs 20,000 random adds and removes against a plain array. Unmasked old slots, a lost move and an unzeroed slot each fail it. The surfaces themselves need a running renderer, which the tests do not have. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks of the Vulkan and OpenGL render target code, the full build and suites pass.


- **Build: no more `std::copy_n` or `std::fill_n`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Why:** a newer MinGW GCC stopped at `Utilities/StrUtil.h` with "'copy_n' is not a member of 'std'". `<algorithm>` was not included there, and that GCC no longer pulls it in through other headers. All 15 uses in the core are replaced.
  - **What took their place:**
    - **`strcpy_trunc`** (`StrUtil.h`, used for every fixed-size string the core fills in) now zeroes the whole array, then copies the text over it. For a 32-byte array that is two vector stores and an inline copy, with no call. It used to call `memmove` and then zero the rest by a length known only at run time.
    - **Copies of the same plain type** now use `memcpy`, where `copy_n` called `memmove`. This covers the texture upload fast path for unswizzled formats (`TextureUtils.cpp`), the syscall argument history, and `sys_event_queue_tryreceive`'s events.
    - **Fills of a big-endian word** (patch NOP fills in `bin_patch.cpp`, SPU image fill segments in `sys_spu.cpp`) now swap the value once, then store it. `fill_n` converted it on every store.
    - **The rest** became plain loops, or one `insert` into the vector in place of a `back_inserter` (`cellDmuxPamf.cpp`).
  - **Tests:** no new unit test; the build is the test. This guard fails if either function comes back. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks of each changed file, the full build and suites pass.


- **Guest memory: a block's allocations in a sorted C89 array, not an `std::map`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A new C89 module** (`util/addr_map.c`) keeps ranges sorted by start address:
      - The addresses are in an array of their own, searched by halving with no branch on the comparison.
      - Room is kept at both ends, so adding or taking out a range moves whichever side of it is shorter.
      - It allocates only when it grows, where the map allocated a node for every range.
    - **Each guest memory block** (`vm::block_t`) kept its allocations, with their sizes and shared memory handles, in an `std::map` behind a type map. They are now in the new module.
      - Each handle is kept in its entry and moved with it, so it needs no allocation of its own. A static check makes sure a handle is two pointers.
      - **Finding an address** (`block_t::peek`) now uses the new search. It runs when an SPU reads its event status with a reservation in mapped memory, and in `sys_mmapper`.
      - **Tearing down a block** used to count every handle into an `std::unordered_map`. It now counts only for a handle that is still shared, and only that handle.
  - **Speed (this machine, 16, 128 and 1024 ranges):**
    - **Finding an address:** 7, 11 and 16 ns, where the map took 28, 47 and 52 ns.
    - **Adding and then removing a range:** 43 and 67 ns at 16 and 128 ranges, where the map took 57 and 73 ns. At 1024 ranges it is slower, 172 ns against 97 ns. A block rarely holds that many.
  - **Size:** `vm.o` is 2.2 KB smaller, about what the new module adds (2.1 KB).
  - **Tests:** `test_addr_map` adds out of order and refuses a second range at the same address. It finds ranges exactly and at or below an address, and takes them out at the front, middle and back. It then runs 20,000 random steps against a plain array, checking each range's data moved with it. Wrong shifts, a missed gap when growing, and an off-by-one search each fail it. The full build, the suites and the Windows (MinGW) syntax check pass.


- **Vulkan and OpenGL: overlay programs, fonts and images, no hash maps.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A new C89 table** (`util/ptr_table.c`): pointers by a 64-bit key, open-addressed with linear probing, entries taken out by shifting the ones after back, grown to stay at most half full. Keys are mixed before use, so addresses a fixed stride apart do not bunch up.
    - **The overlay renderers** kept their resource images, fonts and the images an overlay draws (icons in a save list, for example) in five `std::unordered_map`s each, the image and its view apart. Each image and its view now sit together.
      - The resource images are an array by resource id, found by an index, not a hash.
      - The fonts and drawn images are in the new table. An overlay's images are taken out as they were, by owner, now without collecting keys into a vector first.
    - **The Vulkan overlay passes' programs** (one a render pass) are in the table too.
  - **Speed:** a lookup takes 2.7 to 2.9 ns at 2 to 64 entries, where the map took 5.7 to 5.9 ns. A short list (`util/key_list.c`) was tried first, but it lost to the map past about 8 entries.
  - **Smaller code:** `VKOverlays.o` is 6.0 KB smaller and `GLOverlays.o` 5.2 KB, against 1.6 KB for the table.
  - **Tests:** `test_ptr_table` adds past several growths and takes out by key and by slot. It checks that entries spread through the table and runs 20,000 random steps against a plain array. A wrong shift-back rule or a table that stops spreading as it grows fails it. The renderers themselves need a running GPU, which the tests do not have. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **OpenGL: resolve helpers and overlay passes, no hash maps.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The colour resolve and unresolve helpers** were found by format in `std::unordered_map`s. Only a few formats are ever resolved, so they are now short lists (`util/key_list.c`).
    - **The depth and stencil resolvers** were found by aspect: depth 2, stencil 4, both 6. They are now arrays of eight, indexed by aspect and checked. `clear_resolve_helpers` deletes them all as the maps' `unique_ptr`s did.
    - **The overlay passes** (`gl::get_overlay_pass<T>()`) looked their type's index up in an `std::unordered_map` on each use. As Vulkan's do since 0617, each type now has a static slot of its own, kept in `util/slot_registry.c` for `destroy_overlay_passes`.
    - **Smaller code:** the seven OpenGL objects using them together 1.4 KB less.
  - **Tests:** the two modules have their own (`test_key_list`, `test_slot_registry`). The helpers themselves need a running OpenGL renderer, which the tests do not have. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Vulkan: render pass keys build no vectors, and the resolve helpers are a short list.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **`vk::get_renderpass_key`** took its images and input attachment ids as `std::vector`s. With programmable blending on, the per-draw `invalidate_render_pass` and the framebuffer setup each built a vector of the ids 0 ... n−1, one allocation each time. The depth and stencil resolves, and the partial stencil clear, each built a vector holding one image.
    - It now takes `std::span`s. The ids are a slice of a constant list (`vk::renderpass_attachment_ids`), and a single image is a one-element array, so nothing is built. The callers passing the framebuffer's own vector are unchanged.
    - **The colour resolve and unresolve helpers** were found in `std::unordered_map`s by format. Only a few formats are ever resolved, so they are now short lists (`util/key_list.c`). `clear_resolve_helpers` destroys and deletes them as before.
    - **Smaller code:** VKGSRender, VKDraw, VKResolveHelper and VKRenderPass together 3.9 KB less.
  - **Tests:** `test_shader_corpus`: the ids for 0, 4 and 8 draw buffers are empty, 0 to 3, and 0 to 7, from one constant list. The keys and resolves themselves need a running renderer's images. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Vulkan: scratch resources in arrays and a short list, not three hash maps.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The scratch buffer** (`get_scratch_buffer`, asked for on every compute conversion and texture upload conversion) was found in an `std::unordered_map` by queue family. The device's queue families are small indices, so it is now an array of 16, checked.
    - **The null textures** (`null_image_view`, bound where a texture is missing) were found by view type. There are five types, so it is now an array indexed by type.
    - **The typeless helpers** (`get_typeless_helper`, the images format conversions copy through) were found by format and class. Only a few are in use, so they are now a short list (`util/key_list.c`, from 0616). One grown in size is swapped in place with the new `key_list_replace`, and the old one goes to the GC as before.
    - `clear_scratch_resources` frees all three as the maps did.
    - **Smaller code:** scratch.o 2.6 KB less.
  - **Tests:** `test_key_list` now also covers `key_list_replace`: a key's pointer swapped with the old one given back, both inline and on the heap, and a key not there replaced as none with nothing added. The scratch resources themselves need a running renderer's device, which the tests do not have. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Vulkan: compute tasks and overlay passes are found in a slot of their own, not a hash map.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - `vk::get_compute_task<T>()` and `vk::get_overlay_pass<T>()` find the renderer's compute shaders and overlay passes by type. That happens on each use: format conversions, resolves, overlays and the like, at 26 sites. Each looked the type's index up in an `std::unordered_map`, hashed every time, and the maps had a few dozen entries made once each.
    - Each type now has a static slot of its own, so finding one is a single load. The slots are kept in a new C89 module (`rpcs3/util/slot_registry.c`) so the renderer's reset and teardown can still go through them. Resetting frees each overlay pass's resources. Teardown deletes each task, and destroys then deletes each pass, then empties the slots, so a renderer made again makes them again.
    - **Smaller code:** the ten Vulkan objects using them together 11.4 KB less.
  - **Tests:** `test_slot_registry`: gone through in the order filled with an emptied slot skipped; cleared the last filled first, every slot emptied and the registry usable again; a full registry refusing more. Mutants fail it: clearing without emptying the slots, and taking one past full. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Vulkan: an image's views are a short C89 list, not a hash map.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - A texture's view is asked of its image on each bind (`viewable_image::get_view`), by its channel remap and aspect. An image view's per-format subviews (`image_view::as`) work the same way. Both were kept in `std::unordered_map`s of `unique_ptr`s, hashed on each look, with a node allocated per view. An image has only a few views.
    - They are now a short list searched in turn, in a new C89 module (`rpcs3/util/key_list.c`). The first four are kept in the list itself, so nothing is allocated, and more go on the heap as it grows.
    - A small owner, `vk::view_list` in `vkutils/image.h`, deletes the views as the maps' `unique_ptr`s did. It hands them to the GC when the image's component layout changes, and moves them with the image when it is cloned.
    - **Measured:** a look at 1 to 4 views takes 3.7 to 4.7 ns, where the map took 7.1 to 7.3 ns.
    - **Smaller code:** image.o 287 bytes less; the module is 957 bytes.
  - **Tests:** `test_key_list`: found by key (a 0 key, and keys differing only in their top half, kept apart); four kept in the list with nothing allocated, more on the heap and all still found after it grows twice; moved, with the old list empty and usable; freed and usable again. Mutants fail it: growth copying only half the keys, and a move leaving the source as it was. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Vulkan: the sampler pool is C89, in a fixed table.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - `vk::sampler_pool_t` holds the renderer's samplers by their key. It is looked up on each texture bind that changes its sampler, and a sampler is made on a key not yet seen. It kept them in two `std::unordered_map`s of `unique_ptr`s, one for samplers with no custom border colour and one for the rest. Both were hashed on each lookup, and each new sampler allocated a node.
    - They are now one fixed table in a new C89 module (`rpcs3/Emu/RSX/VK/vkutils/vk_sampler_table.c`), made once with the renderer and never grown. It holds 8192 slots, open-addressed by both halves of the key with linear probing. A sampler taken out has the run after it shifted back, so no slot is left marked deleted.
    - The resource manager trims the samplers no texture holds once there are more than 2048 (`VKResourceManager.cpp`), so the table holds four times what it keeps. The pool owns the samplers it holds and deletes them on `clear` and as it goes, as the `unique_ptr`s did.
    - The trim's predicate captured nothing, so it is a plain function (it was a `std::function`).
    - **Measured:** a find among 300 samplers takes 3.8 to 4.8 ns, where the map took 4.6 to 5.2 ns, and a new sampler no longer allocates a node.
  - **Tests:** `test_vk_sampler_table`:
    - Found by both halves of the key, and a key there already is not replaced.
    - Three quarters full with every other sampler taken out: every one left is still found, and none taken out is.
    - A run of 40 colliding keys that wraps past the table's end, taken out every other one, the rest still found.
    - A full table keeps no more, and collecting with no pick takes them all.
    - Mutants fail it: shifting every sampler back on a removal, and shifting none back.
    - The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **OpenGL: the driver state tracker is C89, in fixed tables.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - `gl::driver_state` remembers what the renderer last set of the driver's state, so a GL call setting the same again is not made. That covers caps, depth, stencil and blend state, indexed caps, and the texture each unit has bound, behind every draw's state setup. It kept these in `std::unordered_map`s: two for properties, and one per texture unit (48 of them). Each call hashed into one, and grew it on a first key.
    - They are now fixed tables in a new C89 module (`rpcs3/Emu/RSX/GL/gl_state_table.c`), made once with the renderer and never grown, so there is no allocation per call. Properties are open-addressed by their GLenum (128 slots), indexed properties likewise (32 slots, four values each), and each unit's bound textures are a short list by target (eight per unit).
    - What it does is unchanged: a key first seen reads as changed and is kept, binding none first reads as set (the map's default), and unbinding a deleted texture clears the first unit found with it. A table full keeps no more, so the call is made each time, as one not cached. The renderer uses far fewer keys than the tables hold.
    - **Measured:** `test_and_set`, on 40 keys as the renderer uses them, takes 2.8 to 2.9 ns a call, where the map took 5.8 to 7.5 ns.
    - **Smaller code:** GLGSRender.o and GLDraw.o together 9.5 KB less; the module is 1.4 KB.
  - **Tests:** `test_gl_state_table`: set, changed and first-seen values, keys that collide in the hash, an indexed property's other values, a full table reading new keys as changed every time while kept keys still read set, and texture units (unbinding clears only the first unit found). Mutants fail it: a probe stopping at the first empty slot, and an unbind clearing every unit. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **sys_io: no lock.** The game's `sys_config_start` and `sys_config_stop` make and take down the config event queue and its handler thread with no lock, through a new C89 module (`rpcs3/util/build_word.c`). Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - The first start made the queue and the thread, and the last stop took them down. Both happened behind `mtx`, a `shared_mutex` the game's threads waited on one at a time (`lock_lv2_mutex_alike`, putting the PPU to sleep while another held it).
    - It is now one word: the starts not yet stopped (bits 0-31), whether the queue is made (bit 32), and whether a call is making or taking it down (bit 33, the builder). A call changes the count, and one finding no builder becomes it. The builder makes or takes down the queue until that matches the count, then stops being the builder unless the count changed meanwhile. A call finding a builder only changes the count, for the builder to see, and returns: no call waits for another.
    - A game starting and stopping on one thread, as games do, is always its own builder, so it makes and takes down the queue in the call as before. Only when another thread's call is already building does a call return before the queue is made or taken down.
    - The savestate keeps the count as before. On load it is made where the count is not 0, with no builder.
    - The one `shared_mutex` left in the core is now `g_submit_mutex`. It covers Vulkan submits, and only for a frontend that lends no queue lock; RetroArch lends one.
  - **Tests:** `test_build_word` (plain and under TSan):
    - On one thread, as a game calls them: the start makes it, a second start and its stop do nothing, the last stop takes it down, a stop with no start is refused, and a restored count is made where it is not 0.
    - On four threads each starting and stopping 50000 times at once: it is made and taken down only in turn, never by two builders at once, and at the end it is made exactly when the count is not 0, with no builder left.
    - Mutants fail it: a start always taking the builder (10 of 10 runs), and the builder stepping down without looking at the count (9 of 10).
    - `test_sys_io_gate` now starts from the word, and its case of a send while the game's call holds things up is a builder at work (it was the mutex held). The send still goes through.
    - The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **The last timed sleeps are gone.** Each now waits on the event it was waiting for, or on the guest's time where the delay is the game's. Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The PAMF demuxer** delayed a new stream by sleeping 1.5 ms of the host's time, so White Knight Chronicles II finishes setting up FMV playback first. It now waits 1.5 ms of the guest's time (`lv2_obj::wait_timeout`), which goes at `retro_run`'s pace, woken by each frame of it.
    - **SPU stop code 0x001** slept a millisecond of the host's ("hack"). It is now a millisecond of the guest's, the same way.
    - **An SPU handing its SPURS slot on** slept 300 µs so another thread could take it. It now waits for the running count to change, that is for one to take it, or for its own state to change, at most the same 300 µs.
    - **An SPU backing off** when there is more SPU work than host threads slept 200 µs, then tried its reservation again. It now waits on that reservation's notifier, at most the same 200 µs, as the wait just below it does.
    - **The access-violation handler, while the emulator stops,** retried mapping a page every millisecond. It now waits for the memory map to change (a writer leaving, as a thread unmapping frees room) or for the thread's state to change, then tries again.
    - **The HLE SPURS kernel's idle loop** slept a millisecond at a time, for good. It reads a local-store copy that nothing refreshes, so only a stop can end it: it now waits on the thread's state. The HLE kernel is not reached anyway: its entry is not registered.
  - **Flagged:**
    - In the access-violation retry, the memory map's epoch is read after the failed try, because that try's own map attempt took the writer lock. A free landing between the failure and the read is only seen at the next writer to leave, of which there are many while the emulator stops.
    - The two guest-time waits wake on each frame of the guest's time, so they look for a stop at that pace, not on the thread's state.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: these run in SPU, SPURS and demuxer threads of a running game. The waits they use have their own tests (`test_guest_clock` for `wait_timeout`, `test_vm_range_locks` for the writer epoch). The guard keeps timed sleeps out of the emulator. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Emulator callbacks: plain functions, not 47 std::functions.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - `emu_callbacks` is the table the emulator calls the core through: dialogs, the renderer's frame, audio, input handlers, paths, localized strings and the rest. It held 47 `std::function`s. Every one the core sets is a lambda that captures nothing.
    - They are now plain function pointers, null where unset. Each call goes straight to the function rather than through a `std::function`, and every call site reads the same.
    - `call_from_main_thread` and `try_to_quit` still take a `std::function` argument: the work handed over, owned by whoever runs it.
    - Smaller code: libretro_core.o, System.o, VFS.o and localized_string.o together 29.9 KB less.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits`' two stand-ins for `call_from_main_thread` are now plain functions as well, reaching their state through statics. The blocking call still returns only after the call has run, and the shader loading dialog still closes after its last queued call. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **suspend_all: no lock.** The `s_cpu_lock` shared_mutex is replaced by a lock-free guard in a new C89 module (`rpcs3/Emu/CPU/cpu_guard.c`). Not yet run on a game.
  - **What the lock did, and what does it now:**
    - **One suspend at a time.** The owner held the lock for the whole of a suspend. The guard keeps an epoch, odd while a suspend runs. The next owner waits for it to be even, then makes it odd with a compare-and-swap, and makes it even again on the way out, by any path.
    - **A thread entering the list** (`cpu_counter::add`, as a thread starts and as one comes back from waiting) took the lock and blocked for the whole of a suspend. It now publishes itself, then waits out a suspend running then (`cpu_guard_wait_suspend`). That suspend either saw it, already marked waiting, or began before it was there and must not have it run meanwhile. A suspend beginning after sees it. Each side stores before it loads, with a full fence between, so the one or the other sees the other.
    - **A thread leaving** locked and unlocked it at exit, so a suspend reading it before could finish. After taking itself out it now waits out the suspend running then, and the walkers in the list then (`cpu_guard_retire`).
    - **The preempt path**, setting yield on every thread, took it shared. It now counts itself in as a walker (`cpu_guard_read_begin`, `_end`), which a thread leaving waits for.
    - **A push from outside a CPU thread**, finding work queued already, locked and unlocked it to wait out the suspend. It now waits on the epoch.
    - Every wait is on one of the guard's words changing, woken by whoever changes it where someone waits: no spin, no clock.
    - A thread can now take a slot freed during a suspend (the lock had stopped it). It enters marked waiting, so the suspend takes it as waiting, and clearing its pause on the way out is harmless.
    - Code size: CPUThread.o 620 bytes less, and the guard 876.
    - With these two gone, no `std::lock_guard` is left in the core. The ratchet on them, `LOCK_GUARD_MAX` in the tests' makefile, is now 0.
  - **Flagged:** a thread leaving waits until no walker is in the list. Walkers coming one after another without a gap could keep it waiting, where the lock let it in ahead of new readers. Walkers come only on a scheduler preempt and are short, so this has not been seen.
  - **Tests:** `test_cpu_guard` (plain and under TSan):
    - Three threads suspend 60000 times and never overlap.
    - Four threads enter and leave the list meanwhile, and none runs in a suspend that did not take it.
    - An object is swapped out 20000 times under two walkers and the suspends, and is never seen after it is freed.
    - Mutants fail it: entering without waiting (10 of 10 runs), retiring without waiting for walkers (9 of 10 on this two-core sandbox), suspends not excluding each other (5 of 5).
    - The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **RSX audio: the period timer no longer calls a static std::function holding the first data thread's `this`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The data thread's period wait** (`rsxaudio_periodic_tmr::wait`) took a `std::function` to call when a period was up. The data thread kept the one it passed in a function-local `static`: `[this]() { extract_audio_data(); }`, made once, with the first data thread's `this`. A data thread made again later, as on a second boot, would have had the timer extract audio through the first one, already freed.
    - `wait` now returns `SUCCESS` when a period is up, and the data thread calls its own `extract_audio_data`. The callback was the last thing `wait` did, so the order is unchanged.
    - **The hardware-parameter update** (`update_hw_param`, a change run on the HLE thread before it returns) took a `std::function` of each caller's lambda. It now takes the lambda by reference, as a plain function and the lambda's address. A thin template forwards to the one body (`update_hw_param_by`), so the body is not made again for each caller.
    - Smaller code: sys_rsxaudio.o 778 bytes less.
  - **Tests:** `test_guest_clock`'s period test now counts the periods `wait` returns as up: one for each period of guest time, none while `retro_run` does not come. A second data thread can't be made in the tests, so the stale `this` itself is not tested. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **Index buffers: whether to expand a draw's indices is a bool, not a std::function.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - `write_index_array_data_to_buffer`, behind every indexed draw on both renderers, took a `std::function<bool(primitive_type)>` asking whether to expand the indices: quads, fans and polygons to triangles, line loops closed. Both renderers passed a lambda that captured nothing and asked `is_primitive_native` of the one primitive the call was for. So a `std::function` was built per draw, to be asked once.
    - The renderer now asks that itself and passes the answer as a bool. Smaller code: VKVertexBuffers.o 459 bytes less, GLVertexBuffers.o 307, BufferUtils.o 216.
  - **Tests:** `test_shader_corpus`: four quad indices are left as four, or made six when told to expand, and a line loop is closed with its first index again. One that ignored the bool fails it. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Audio: the backend's write callback, called on every pull, is a plain call, not a std::function.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The write callback**, which the libretro backend calls on every pull of `retro_run`'s audio, and **the state callback**, were `std::function`s. `sys_rsxaudio` and cellAudio set them with `std::bind` of a member function. The bound object (the member pointer and `this`, 24 bytes) is more than `std::function` keeps inline, so setting one allocated it on the heap. Each call then went through the `std::function` and the bind to reach the member.
    - They are now `audio_write_cb` and `audio_state_cb`: a plain function and the object it is called for. `audio_write_cb::of<&T::member>(obj)` makes one from a member function, called straight through. Nothing is allocated, and there is no thunk between.
    - The libretro backend's three lock-free callback slots hold these as they held the `std::function`s, so setting one is now a plain copy.
    - Smaller code: sys_rsxaudio.o 1039 bytes less, libretro_audio.o 723, cellAudio.o 600, AudioBackend.o 320.
  - **Tests:** `test_audio_frontend`: a member function bound as a write and a state callback is called on that object with what it was given, and an empty callback reads as none. Its pull tests now set the producer as an `audio_write_cb`. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Pipeline compilers: a deferred compile's callbacks are moved into its job, not copied.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - A deferred compile hands a worker its callbacks: Vulkan's completion callback and, from the shader interpreter, its create-info function; OpenGL's post-create and completion callbacks. These are `std::function`s owning what they captured, and they stay so. But each was copied on the way in: into the work queue's push, then again into the job's members.
