A capture too big to keep inline (the interpreter's, the program cache's cloned programs) was allocated again at each copy.
    - They are now moved at the push and into the job. The inline path's create-info function is taken by reference rather than copied.
    - The program buffers' own callback is still copied into the compile, because they call it again after the compile returns.
    - Smaller code: VKPipelineCompiler 608 bytes less, GLPipelineCompiler 304.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: a move in place of a copy changes no behaviour, and a deferred compile needs a running renderer's compiler threads. The guard keeps the moves. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **OpenGL pipeline compiler: no std::function for its contexts and post-link.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The worker contexts:** each compiler thread made, bound and let go of its context through three `std::function`s. Each wrapped a lambda calling the frame. The compiler now keeps the frame itself (`GSFrameBase*`, none where the shaders compile inline) and calls its `make_context`, `set_current` and `delete_context`.
    - **The post-link step** (`program::link`'s `init_func`, and the job's `post_link_func`): the one given, binding the texture units, captures nothing, so it is a plain function pointer. Before, a `std::function` was copied into every compile job and its call.
    - **Kept as std::function:** the post-create and completion callbacks. A deferred job owns them, with what they captured, until a worker runs it. A hand-made pointer-and-context in their place would still allocate per compile, so there would be nothing to gain.
    - Smaller code: GLGSRender 1598 bytes less, GLPipelineCompiler 1438, program.o 16; GLShaderInterpreter 238 more, from different inlining.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: the compiler needs a running OpenGL renderer and context, which the tests do not have. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Shader cache loading: the workers are no longer std::functions.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - Loading the shader cache, as a game boots, runs two worker lambdas (`load_shaders`, `compile_shaders`) on worker threads or on this one through `await_workers`. Each was put in a `std::function`. The load worker captures five references, more than `std::function` keeps inline, so that also made a heap allocation per load.
    - The workers now go to `await_workers` as a reference: the lambda's address and a plain function that calls it (`worker_ref`, made by `make_worker_ref`). Nothing is allocated, and there is no `std::function` thunk.
    - Making `await_workers` a template on the lambda instead was measured and rejected: it copied the workers into every call and made the renderers' code 1.1 to 1.6 KB bigger. The reference makes it smaller: VKGSRender 1215 bytes less, GLGSRender 948.
  - **Tests:** `test_shader_corpus`: a worker capturing five references, called twice through its reference, runs with the stop points given, and the reference points at the lambda. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Vulkan descriptor pools: a subpool's release no longer allocates a std::function.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A descriptor pool moving to its next subpool** (`descriptor_pool::next_subpool`, during gameplay whenever one fills up) handed the old subpool's reset to the GC. It did so as a heap-made `gc_callback_t` holding a `std::function` of a lambda.
    - Each subpool now keeps its device. The GC is handed the subpool's own address with a plain function that resets it and gives it back (`descriptor_pool::release_subpool`, through the new `vk::disposable_t::call`), so nothing is allocated. `gc_callback_t` and the pool's `reset`, now unused, are gone.
    - **The GC's exit callbacks** (`add_exit_callback`, the two shared pools let go of as the device goes) are plain function pointers rather than `std::function`, as neither captures anything.
    - Smaller code: descriptors.o 551 bytes less, VKResourceManager.o 270, sync.o 266.
  - **Tests:** `test_shader_corpus`: a `disposable_t::call` makes its call once, as it goes, on the pointer it was given, and not when the GC takes it over (`take`). The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Guest memory faults reach the renderer through an atomic pointer, not a std::function.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - The fault handler (`handle_access_violation`, Utilities/Thread.cpp) handed a fault on memory the renderer may hold to `rsx::g_access_violation_handler`. That was a global `std::function` the renderer assigned as it started and cleared as it stopped. Any thread's fault handler read it with no lock while it was being assigned, which is a data race on an object that is not atomic. Each call also went through the `std::function`'s thunk and a lambda before the renderer's virtual `on_access_violation`.
    - It is now an atomic pointer to the renderer (`retro_atomic_ptr_t`), stored with release as the renderer starts and stops. The handler reads it with acquire through `rsx::has_access_violation_target` and `rsx::access_violation`, which call `on_access_violation` directly.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits`: with no renderer running there is no target, and a fault is handed back as not handled. A renderer can't be made in the test, so the hand-over to one is not tested. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Texture cache: no std::function through invalidate and flush calls.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - `invalidate_address`, `invalidate_range`, `invalidate_range_impl_base`, `flush_all` and `flush_set` each took an `on_data_transfer_completed` callback as a `std::function` by value. They are behind every guest write to protected memory and every deferred flush.
    - No caller ever gave one: the renderers called without it, and the texture cache's own calls passed `{}`. So an empty `std::function` was built at the outer call, copied into each layer, and tested before its call. The parameter, the `{}` arguments and the call are gone; nothing else changes.
    - Smaller code: GLGSRender 1294 bytes less, GLPresent 1383, VKGSRender 1189, VKTextureCache 861 (VKPresent 53 more, from different inlining).
  - **Tests:** none of their own: nothing ran through the callback, so there is no behaviour to test. The guard keeps the parameter out. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **On Linux, AMD processors no longer sleep 50 ms on load to measure the TSC, where the kernel will say it.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Where CPUID does not state the TSC's frequency** (AMD), it is now taken from the kernel before measuring, through a new C89 module (`rpcs3/util/tsc_perf.c`). The module opens a perf event on its own thread, maps the event's page, and reads the kernel's TSC-to-ns conversion, `time_mult` and `time_shift`, under the page's sequence count. The frequency is 10^9 << shift / mult, rounded. The event and the page are let go of at once.
    - The kernel fills the page in only where it reads time from the TSC itself (`cap_user_time`: a native, stable sched_clock). It gives the page only where `perf_event_paranoid` lets a thread watch itself, which some distributions forbid. Otherwise it measures as before.
    - Not on Android: its app filter kills a process making a system call it does not allow, rather than failing the call.
    - **Flagged:** this is untested live here. This sandbox is a VM, where the kernel leaves the page empty, so it still measures. AMD on Windows still measures with the sleep: Windows has no such page.
  - **Tests:** `test_tsc_perf`: the kernel's mult for 2.1 and 3.7 GHz at a shift of 31, 4.012345 GHz at 30, 1 GHz at 10 and the widest shift that fits all give the frequency to the hertz; a mult of 0 or a shift too wide gives none; read here it gives none, or a frequency between 100 MHz and 10 GHz. A mutant without the rounding fails it. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Skylake and Kaby Lake desktop parts, and Denverton, no longer sleep 50 ms on load to measure the TSC.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - These CPUs give the TSC's ratio to the crystal in leaf 0x15 but leave the crystal's frequency at 0, so they still measured with the sleep. The crystal is now taken as Linux takes it (`native_calibrate_tsc`). Denverton (family 6, model 0x5F) gets 25 MHz. Others derive it from leaf 0x16's base frequency: an i7-6700K at 4000 MHz with a ratio of 334/2 gives a 23952 kHz crystal and 3999984 kHz.
    - The arithmetic is in kHz, as Linux's is, and leaf 0x15 is read on Intel only, also as Linux.
    - **Flagged:** AMD processors, which state neither leaf, and this sandbox under KVM still measure with the sleep.
  - **Tests:** `test_tsc_cpuid` now also covers Denverton's 25 MHz (not on another family), the i7-6700K from its base frequency, none without leaf 0x16 or its base, and none from a vendor not Intel. Mutants without the model check, the vendor check or the leaf 0x16 bound fail it. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Loading the core no longer sleeps 50 ms to measure the TSC, where the processor states its frequency.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The TSC's frequency** (`utils::get_tsc_freq`, behind the line-watch wait bounds and other cycle timeouts) was measured during static initialisation, so while the core loads. It read the TSC and the wall clock, slept 50 ms, and read both again. This is the pre-existing sleep and wall clock flagged earlier.
    - It is now read from CPUID first, by a new C89 module (`rpcs3/util/tsc_cpuid.c`), and nothing is measured where the processor states it. Under a hypervisor that has it, that is leaf 0x40000010, the TSC in kHz (VMware, and KVM where enabled). It comes first, because a guest's TSC may be scaled from the host's. Otherwise it is leaf 0x15 (Intel, Goldmont and Skylake on), the crystal's frequency times the TSC's ratio to it, where the leaf names the crystal.
    - **Flagged:** processors that state neither still measure as before, with the sleep. That covers AMD before Zen 4's leaf 0x15, Skylake desktop parts that leave the crystal unnamed, and this sandbox (KVM hides both leaves). A Skylake crystal table, as Linux keeps, would cover those desktop parts.
  - **Tests:** `test_tsc_cpuid`: an Alder Lake's leaf 0x15 gives 3.6096 GHz; a leaf 0x15 with no crystal, a ratio of 0, or beyond the processor's last leaf gives none; a hypervisor's leaf comes before leaf 0x15, and is not used with the hypervisor bit clear or a leaf 0x40000000 out of range. The Windows (MinGW) syntax checks, the full build and suites pass.


- **Vulkan: a texture readback no longer spins on its copy's event.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A texture-cache readback** (`cached_texture_section::imp_flush`) waits for the DMA copy that its section queued. The copy signals a VkEvent, and Vulkan has no host wait on an event, so `vk::wait_for_event` polled it on a pause until it was set, for up to 2 seconds.
    - Each command buffer now counts its submits (`command_buffer::submit_count`). When the copy signals its event, the section records the buffer and which submit carries the signal. The flush waits in the driver on that submit's fence (`command_buffer::wait_last_submit`), so the thread sleeps until the GPU is done. A later submit already made means that one waited for it first.
    - The event check after the wait stays, and it ends at once. It still polls only if the submit has not been made: the renderer submits its queue before it flushes, so that should not happen.
    - **Flagged:** the fence is left to whoever sees it done. A flush on a thread other than the renderer's could, rarely, wait on a fence the renderer has just reset for its next use. The wait is then bounded at 2 seconds, as the poll was, and the event check ends it.
  - **Tests:** `test_shader_corpus` (a real Vulkan device, lavapipe here) holds a submit on an event the host sets: the wait for it times out while held, ends once set, and the copy's event is set by then. A wait that does not wait fails it. It submits directly, as the tests have no renderer for `vk::queue_submit`; the guard keeps the submit count. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **OpenGL: waiting for the GPU no longer spins forever.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **`gl::fence::wait_for_signal`**, behind the ring buffers' waits and texture readbacks, polled the fence's status with `glGetSynciv` in a loop with no end and no pause. It now makes one `glClientWaitSync` with no time limit, so the driver sleeps the thread until the GPU passes the fence. The old poll also returned "not signalled" when it had seen the fence pass; that is fixed as well.
    - **The host label drain** (`RSXDMAWriter::drain_label_queue`, on a texture-read semaphore release) polled the counter the GPU writes, on OpenGL only; Vulkan already waited on its command buffers. OpenGL now first waits in the driver on the ring buffer's newest fence, the one after the last label write (`scratch_ring_buffer::wait_last_barrier`, through the new `gl::fence::client_wait`, which keeps the sync for the ring's own later use).
    - The counter check after the wait stays. It ends at once unless a write came after the newest fence, or the mapping, which is not coherent, has not shown the write yet.
    - **Flagged, left as it is:** Vulkan's `vk::wait_for_event`, behind texture-cache readbacks, still polls the VkEvent, because Vulkan has no host wait on an event. Making it block means waiting on the fence of the command buffer that signals it.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: the waits need a running OpenGL renderer, and the tests' surfaceless EGL contexts drive only the frame hand-over. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **SPU byte locks and event reads: waiting for another thread no longer spins forever.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **With accurate DMA on**, a transfer locks the bytes it writes in a 128-bit mask beside the reservation line. A transfer finding some of them held spun on `busy_wait(500)` for as long as they stayed held: a forced `true ||` had cut out the old wait branch, which was left half-written. After 100 tries it now waits until the 32-bit word holding the first held byte changes, through `utils::spin_on_cacheline_once`, bounded at 50 µs. The dead branch is gone.
    - **Reading SPU events** (`get_events`) while `push_snr` holds the event channel retried on `busy_wait(100)` with no end. After 100 tries it now waits the same way on the word holding the lock count.
    - **Flagged:** both locks are freed with a plain compare-and-swap that wakes no one. Where the processor cannot watch the line (this sandbox's Xeon) the wait ends on its bound, a timed wait where it spun. The 100 tries first keep the usual short waits as they were.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: both waits need running SPU threads. `spin_on_cacheline_once` has its own tests in `test_emu_waits`. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **Writer locks and suspend_all: waiting for other threads no longer spins forever.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A writer taking a reservation line** (`vm::writer_lock`) waits for readers holding that line, then for each PPU holding a passive lock to reach a wait. Both spun on a pause for as long as it took. **Suspending all threads** (`cpu_thread::suspend_work::push`, behind `suspend_all`) spun on a pause until every thread had set its wait flag.
    - After 100 pauses they now wait until the word changes, the reader's slot or the first running thread's state, through `utils::spin_on_cacheline_once`. UMWAIT, MWAITX or WFE wake on the other thread's store, with a 50 µs bound.
    - **Flagged:** a reader's release and a thread setting its wait flag are plain stores (the wait flag also from the recompiled code), so nothing wakes the word. Where the processor cannot watch the line (this sandbox's Xeon) the wait ends on its bound, a timed wait where it spun. The 100 pauses first keep the usual short waits as they were.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: the waits need running guest threads. `spin_on_cacheline_once` has its own tests in `test_emu_waits`. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **Memory-layout changes: waiting for readers no longer spins forever.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A change to guest memory's layout** (allocating, freeing or protecting pages, `_lock_main_range_lock`) waits for the readers holding the range to leave. It spun on a pause for as long as they stayed. After 100 pauses it now waits until the first busy reader's slot changes, through `utils::spin_on_cacheline_once`: UMWAIT, MWAITX or WFE wake on the reader's release, with a 50 µs bound.
    - **`vm::try_access`** (the debugger's and HLE's guarded memory access) spun 200 cycles at a time while such a change held its range. After 100 spins it waits on that lock's slot in the same way.
    - **Flagged:** readers release with a plain store on their hot path (every guest memory access under a range lock), and nothing wakes the slot. Where the processor cannot watch the line (this sandbox's Xeon) the wait ends on its bound, a timed wait where it spun. A wake there would put a fence on every read lock's release. Layout changes are rare next to reads.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: the waits need guest threads holding range locks. `spin_on_cacheline_once` has its own tests in `test_emu_waits`. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **Memory reads and PPU thread exit: waiting for writers no longer spins forever.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A thread reading guest memory that a writer holds** (`vm::range_lock_internal`) and **a PPU thread exiting** (`_sys_ppu_thread_exit`, which waits for the writers it saw) spun 200 cycles at a time for as long as the writers stayed. After 100 such spins they now sleep on the writers' wake word (`vm::wait_for_writers`) until a writer leaves; the reader also wakes on its own thread's state, as `passive_lock` does.
    - **The 100 spins before the sleep stay, measured:** on this sandbox (2 vCPUs, virtualised), one writer handing a line to one reader, against sleeping at once. With the writer holding 0.5 or 2 µs, spinning first sees the release in 0.5 µs and costs the writer 0.15-0.18 µs to release; sleeping at once takes 0.8 µs and 0.37-0.51 µs. Held 10 µs, the spin sees it in 0.8 µs and sleeping at once in 12.8 µs, as the wake costs the writer 7.9 µs. At 50 µs both sleep and match (14.7 µs). Dropping the spins would slow the common short hold, so they stay; past them nothing spins.
    - **Flagged:** a memory-layout change waiting for readers to leave (`_lock_main_range_lock`) still spins with no end, and `vm::try_access` spins on it while it is held. Readers leave with a plain store on their hot path, so waking a sleeper there would put a fence on every read lock's release.
    - **Also:** the check added with the writer-lock change never looked for a yield: make took a `$` in its pattern, and awk failed on what was left. It now reads the code as meant.
  - **Tests:** the wake itself is tested in `test_vm_range_locks` (it fails without the wake). These two callers need running guest threads. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **Reservation locks and the HLE SPU-signal wait: no more yields.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Waiting for a locked reservation** (`vm::reservation_lock_internal`, `vm::reservation_shared_lock_internal`, the SPU's GET of a locked line, GETLLAR's retry, and the unconditional put's two waits in `SPUThread.cpp`) yielded the host's thread in a loop after its short spins. It now waits until the reservation word changes, through `utils::spin_on_cacheline_once`, the line watch RSX's FIFO already uses on these words: UMWAIT or MWAITX on x86 and WFE on ARM wake on the unlock's store, with a 50 µs bound. Without them it is a timed wait of 50 µs on the word.
    - **The Sonic HLE patch** (`WaitForSPUsToEmptySNRs`) yielded between looks at the SPUs' signal channels. It now waits on the first busy channel's word in the same way, with a 100 µs bound.
    - **This was the last `std::this_thread::yield` in the emulator.** A check keeps it that way.
    - **Flagged:** these unlocks and signal reads are plain stores from the recompiled code, so nothing wakes the word. Where the processor cannot watch the line (no WAITPKG or MWAITX, as on this sandbox's Xeon), the waits end on their bound, a timed wait where it yielded. Waking them would take a notify in the JIT's unlock path, its hot path. The short spins before the waits stay.
  - **Tests:** no new test: these waits need running SPU threads. `spin_on_cacheline_once` has its own tests in `test_emu_waits`. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **Memory locks: waiting for a writer to leave sleeps, no yields.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A thread taking the memory lock while a writer holds part of memory** (`vm::passive_lock`, and a new writer in `vm::writer_lock`) spun 100 short pauses, then yielded the host's thread in a loop until the writers left. After the spin it now sleeps on a wake word (`vm_writer_wake_t` in `vm_range_locks.c`, C89) until the writers' bits change, or, in `passive_lock`, until its own thread's state does (a pause or exit still reaches it).
    - **A writer leaving** (`~writer_lock`) wakes the sleepers only when there are some: a fence and a load of the waiter count when no one waits, a bump and a wake when someone does. A sleeper counts itself before it looks at the bits again, so a writer leaving between the two is seen either way.
    - **Flagged:** the 100 short spins before the sleep stay, unmeasured on a game. The reservation locks' yields (`vm.cpp`, `SPUThread.cpp`) and the HLE SPU-signal wait remain: their release is a store from the recompiled code, which would need a wake added in the JIT.
  - **Tests:** `test_vm_range_locks` gains a round-trip test, 3000 rounds of a waiter sleeping on the word while a writer clears its bit and wakes it, each with a 1 s bound. It fails when the wake is left out. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass.


- **Savestates: locking the SPUs waits on their states, no yields, sleeps or host clock.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Bringing the SPUs to a point where they can be saved** (`try_lock_spu_threads_in_a_state_compatible_with_savestates`, before a savestate is written) waits, when no SPU is savable yet or the suspend that would stop them failed, on the emulator's state and the first seven SPUs' states for up to 1 ms (50 ms when the SPUs could not be collected), where it yielded the host's thread in a loop, slept 1 ms after every 150 ms of the host's clock, and slept 50 ms. It gives up after about 2.25 s of such waits, as before: a wait that runs out counts its bound, one a change ends counts 10 µs.
    - **Flagged:** an SPU leaving the code it cannot be saved in (an MFC command, a channel read, a state check) is not an event: its `unsavable` byte is a plain store from the SPU recompiler. A wake on it would put a locked operation on every such exit, the SPU's hot path; the 1 ms bound is what ends most of these waits. It runs only while a savestate is being made.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: the lock needs running SPU threads. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a yield, sleep or host clock comes back to the lock.


- **RSX FIFO: waits for put and for locked lines by watching their words, no yields or busy-waits.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **An empty FIFO** (the guest has not moved put since the RSX caught up), **a command waiting for its argument** (`inc_get`) and **a semaphore wait's fallback** wait for the guest's put pointer to change with `FIFO_control::wait_for_put`: the processor watches its cache line (UMWAIT, MWAITX or WFE), else a timed wait of 100 µs, as the semaphore acquire already waits on the semaphore. They yielded the host's thread and came straight back. The guest writes put with a plain store, so a line watch is the only event there is for it.
    - **Fetching FIFO commands over a cache line someone is writing** (its reservation locked) waits for that line's reservation word to change, watched the same way (the writer's unlock is a store), else for 50 µs, where it spun 200-cycle busy-waits for 50 µs of the host's clock and then yielded. The host clock reads and the idle time they added to the performance counters go with it.
  - **Tests:** none fail on these waits coming back: they need an RSX thread and a guest FIFO. The line watch's timed fallback is tested in test_emu_waits (the semaphore wait). The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a yield or busy-wait comes back to the FIFO.


- **util: no host yields: a line watch falls back to a timed wait; TSC calibration without yields.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **`utils::spin_on_cacheline_once`** (the RSX semaphore acquire's wait) waits, where the processor cannot watch the cache line (no UMWAIT/WAITPKG, no MWAITX, not ARM), with one timed wait of the given bound on the word itself through libretro-common's `retro_waitaddr` (the low half of an 8-byte word), where it yielded the host's thread and came straight back. A writer that wakes the word ends it at once; a guest's plain store is seen when it times out, and the caller looks again. Intel before Alder Lake has neither instruction; AMD has MWAITX and ARM has WFE.
    - **The TSC frequency calibration** (once, at load) keeps the tightest of up to 1024 paired TSC and clock reads without the host yields it took every 128 reads. Flagged, not changed: it still sleeps 50 ms between its two samples.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits' semaphore wait (a value stored after 20 ms) now also checks it looked fewer than 5000 times: 102 to 120 here, on a processor with no line watch, where it looked 102169 times with the yield. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a host yield comes back to asm.hpp or sysinfo.cpp.


- **SPU: a polled decrementer no longer yields the host's thread.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **An SPU reading its decrementer while it counts down** (SPU Loop Detection on, more than a scheduler jiffy left: the guest is using it as a timer) is marked as waiting so a suspend need not wait for it, and the recompilers' read checks for a stop, as before; the host yield each read made between the two goes, in the interpreter (`read_channel`), the LLVM recompiler and the ASMJIT recompiler.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: the yield changed nothing the guest sees, only how the host's thread spent the read. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a host yield comes back to the decrementer read.


- **lv2: no host yields or pauses in sleeps; usleep(0) yields to the guest's threads.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A game's sleep** (`lv2_obj::wait_timeout`) waits for what is left with one timed wait on the thread's state and the guest clock, on every host and at every Sleep Timers Accuracy. libretro-common's timed waits end when they should (1 µs timer slack on Linux, a high-resolution waitable timer on Windows, which its eventcount already uses), so there is no host quantum to finish by hand. Before, the accurate settings finished the last 10 µs (500 µs on Windows) with a WAITPKG/MWAITX pause or, on CPUs without them (Intel before Alder Lake), a yield loop, and Windows waited in 500 µs steps. This takes back the last patch's Windows exception: its premise, that Windows timed waits round up to 1 ms, was wrong for these waits. Sleep Timers Accuracy still decides whether a usleep marks the thread asleep for the scheduler.
    - **A sleep of 0** (`sys_timer_usleep(0)`, which games call to give way) yields to the guest's other threads through `lv2_obj::yield`, as `sys_ppu_thread_yield` does (the lv2 scheduler puts this thread behind the others ready to run), where it yielded the host's thread.
  - **Tests:** none of their own: the waits end on time either way, and a guest yield needs running PPU threads. test_guest_clock's timed waits pass. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a host yield or timed pause comes back to lv2's sleeps (it replaces the last patch's check for the Windows yield).


- **lv2 sleeps: the last few microseconds as a timed wait, not a yield loop, off Windows.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A game's sleep with Sleep Timers Accuracy set to usleep or all timers** (`lv2_obj::wait_timeout`; on Linux and macOS the default is As Host, which already only waited) ends, once 10 µs or less are left and the CPU has no WAITPKG/MWAITX timed pause, with one timed wait on the thread's state and the guest clock, where it yielded in a loop until the time had passed. On Linux a timed wait is precise to the microsecond (the core sets 1 µs timer slack).
    - **Windows keeps the yield loop:** its timed waits round up to the timer period (1 ms with the core's raised resolution), far past a sleep of a few microseconds; it is the default there (usleep accuracy). Flagged: an Intel CPU without WAITPKG (before Alder Lake) spins in it for a game's short usleeps.
  - **Tests:** none of its own: the yield and the wait both end on time, and the change is in how the waiting thread spends those microseconds. test_guest_clock's timed waits pass. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the yield comes back off Windows.


- **SPU channels and mailboxes: straight to the wait, no busy-waits first.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A channel's pop and push waits** (`spu_channel::pop_wait`/`push_wait`: the SPU reading a signal or the outbound mailbox, writing one that is full) and **the inbound mailbox's** (`spu_channel_4_t::pop_wait`) wait on the channel's word at once, where each spun ten 3000-cycle busy-waits (about 10 µs) first. The other side already clears the wait bit and wakes the word, so nothing else changes; the pending lv2 wakes are still flushed before the wait.
    - **A stopped SPU whose pop had a value pushed to it** waits on the channel word for the pusher to finish, where it spun 500-cycle busy-waits with no bound.
    - **Measured here:** a hand-off between two threads (one stores and wakes, the other waits, and back) took 0.6 to 0.75 µs a round trip waiting at once, and 1.7 µs with the ten busy-waits first: a busy-wait only looks again every ~1 µs, later than the reply comes. That was a two-thread microbenchmark on this 2-CPU machine, not the channels in a game.
    - **From the council's review:** the correctness and accuracy seats rated these safe to convert (the waits were already event-driven); the performance seat asked for a measurement first, which is the one above.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits' channel and mailbox cases (a push wakes a waiter marked as pop_wait marks it) pass; the waits themselves need an SPU thread, so none fails on the spins coming back. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a busy-wait comes back to the channel waits.


- **Waits: their records made at retro_init, so the fault handler's waits never allocate; libretro-common resynced.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **libretro-common** is RetroArch master's again: `retro_waitaddr` with `retro_waitaddr_reserve` (a wait looks through the records already made before it makes more), and `file_path`'s `path_resolve_realpath_weak` building where realpath or Windows Vista's calls are missing.
    - **retro_init makes the wait records for 1024 threads waiting at once** (`retro_waitaddr_reserve(1024)`, about 100 KiB, out of a pool of 4096). A wait reached from the fault handler (a guest access violation pausing the emulator, a page mapped on demand) used to be able to call calloc for a new chunk of records, which a signal handler must not do; with the records made first, it takes one already there. A thread past the 1024 still gets one, outside a fault handler. This is the council's prerequisite for the waits that follow.
  - **Tests:** the reservation is tested in RetroArch (samples/rthreads/retro_waitaddr_reserve_test: 32 threads waiting at once allocate nothing after it). The core's call has none of its own: the tests do not run retro_init. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if retro_init stops making the reservation.


- **suspend_all: a paused thread sleeps until the work is done, no spin or 10 µs timer.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A PPU or SPU thread paused by `cpu_thread::suspend_all`** (savestates, the SPU and PPU recompilers' patching, the debugger, and every other suspend in the core) waits on two words: the suspend counter and its own state. It leaves when the counter has moved on to the next suspend (the owner's last increment, after which it wakes the counter's waiters, as it already did) or when its pause has been taken off. Before, it spun 300 cycles at a time for its first 20 looks and for as long as the counter was odd (the whole time the owner ran the work), then woke every 10 µs (marked upstream as a workaround to fix). The owner clears every pause before it wakes the counter, so no wake is missed.
  - **From the council's review** (performance, correctness and PS3 accuracy, reading the code): this was the first site all three agreed is safe to make event-driven; it was the biggest one, as every paused thread spun for the whole of each suspend.
  - **Tests:** none of their own; the wait needs running PPU or SPU threads inside a suspend, which no test here makes. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the 10 µs timer comes back.


- **No std::regex left: wildcards through libretro-common's rl_fnmatch.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A version string** (`utils::compare_versions`: firmware, patches) is valid when it holds only digits and dots, checked by `find_first_not_of`, where a std::regex (`[0-9.]*`) checked it.
    - **The DNS switch list** (RPCN's host redirects, `host=ip`, where `*` stands for any run of characters) matches names with `rl_fnmatch` (libretro-common's fnmatch, compat_fnmatch.c, now built into the core), the pattern and the name both in lower case, where each pattern was turned into a case-insensitive std::regex. Only `*` was ever a wildcard there; a `?` or `[` in a pattern is now one too.
    - **A log level set for a name with a wildcard** (the configuration's Log section) sets every channel the name matches with `rl_fnmatch` (`*`, `?`, `[...]`), where a name holding any regex character was taken as a std::regex: `sys_*` works as before written as `sys_.*` did; a name that used other regex syntax (`|`, `+`, `(...)`) now matches only as wildcards.
  - **Tests:** test_vfs_positional sets a level for `TE?T`, `T*` and `X*` and checks the TEST channel took the first two and not the third; it fails if a `?` or `*` name is looked up as it is. test_emu_waits compares version strings by part and checks one with a letter is refused; it fails on a check that lets letters through. The DNS hook has none: it needs the RPCN configuration. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::regex comes back to the core.


- **ISO dates: days counted with libretro-common's rtime_days_from_civil.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A file's date in an ISO** is turned into a UNIX time with `rtime_days_from_civil` (libretro-common's rtime.h, from RetroArch), where it used the core's own copy of the same count (`iso_days_from_civil` in ISO.h, with three static assertions), which goes. The two are the same algorithm, so the times are the same.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits checks the count on 1970-01-01, 1969-12-31, 2000-03-01, 2006-11-11 and 2055-12-31; it fails on a count that does not take January and February with the year before. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the core's own copy comes back.


- **PKG installer: its path check on libretro-common, no std::filesystem left in the core.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Every path a package installs to** is resolved with `path_resolve_realpath_weak` (libretro-common, from RetroArch): the part that exists with its links resolved, the rest by its text, as std::filesystem's weakly_canonical resolved it. A file is installed only if its resolved path lies in the resolved install directory by whole components (`pkg_path_is_inside`: `/a/b` is in `/a`, `/ab` is not), as std::filesystem's component-wise comparison checked. Directories are still resolved once each, the files in them joined to that.
    - **A path that cannot be resolved** (too long, or a `..` above the root) is refused, where it fell back to its text alone: a name with `.` or `..` was already refused before it got there.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits makes an install directory with a link inside it that leads to /tmp, and a directory beside it whose name begins with its: a new file under it is inside, one through the link is not, one in the other directory is not. It fails on a check by prefix alone, and on one that does not resolve links. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::filesystem comes back to the core.


- **libretro-common resynced with RetroArch master.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Twelve files** are RetroArch master's again: `file_path` (path_resolve_realpath_weak, for the PKG installer next), `rtime` (the Gregorian calendar), `features_cpu` (Android ARM64 timer frequencies), `rjpeg_encode` and `formats/image.h` (a block at a time), `rslc` (C89 sprintf names) and `crypto/x509.c`. None of the core's own code changes with them.
  - **Tests:** none of their own here; path_resolve_realpath_weak is tested in RetroArch (samples/file/file_path) and in the core with the PKG installer next. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if libretro-common falls behind without path_resolve_realpath_weak.


- **Shaders: GLSL built with fmt::text_writer, no string streams left in the core.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **`fmt::text_writer`** (StrFmt.h) is a `std::string` appended to with `<<`, as the decompilers used streams for: it writes what a std::ostringstream with its default flags writes (characters as themselves, bool as 1 or 0, integers and unscoped enums in decimal, strings as they are) with no stream buffer, locale or sentry per `<<`. Floating point and pointers other than strings will not compile with it, so no value can be formatted otherwise without being seen: none of the decompilers wrote one, so the shader text is the same byte for byte.
    - **Every GLSL source the core makes** goes through it in place of std::stringstream: the vertex and fragment program decompilers (RSX Program), their OpenGL and Vulkan back ends, both shader interpreters and the shared GLSL helpers (GLSLCommon); `std::endl` there is a newline (nothing was flushed).
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits writes every type the decompilers write (u32, u8, s8, bool, negative and 64-bit integers, an unscoped enum, strings) to a text_writer and a std::ostringstream and checks the two texts are the same; it fails on a writer that writes bool as `true`. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a string stream comes back to the core.


- **No string streams in Thread.cpp and the PKG reader.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The stack trace logged on an unhandled host exception** (local builds with a C++23 stacktrace only) is `std::to_string` of it, where it went through a std::ostringstream.
    - **A PKG's digests in hex** (logged while it installs) are written two digits a byte into a string sized once, where they went through a std::stringstream with setw and setfill per byte; unpkg.h no longer includes <sstream> or <iomanip>.
    - **Not changed here:** the shader decompilers (RSX Program, GL and VK) still build their GLSL with std::stringstream, about 90 uses passed between files; that is a sweep of its own.
  - **Tests:** none of their own; the hex is private to the PKG reader, and the stack trace is only in local builds. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a string stream comes back to those files.


- **No std::thread left in the core: thread ids and the CPU count from libretro-common.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The host's logical CPU count** (`utils::get_thread_count`, taken once: the thread pools, the SPU thread count and the CPU usage overlay size by it) is `cpu_features_get_core_amount`, libretro-common's, where it asked the host itself (GetNativeSystemInfo, sysconf). The AArch64 CPU name reads each core's MIDR up to that count, where it asked std::thread::hardware_concurrency.
    - **The OpenGL renderer's trace lines** (compiled out unless their trace switch is on) tag themselves with `sthread_get_current_thread_id`, where they hashed std::this_thread's id.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits checks the CPU count is the host's (sysconf); it fails on a count of 1 on a host with more. The AArch64 file was syntax-checked with the aarch64 cross compiler (its only errors are the x86-configured LLVM's). The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::thread comes back to the core.


- **Detached helper threads on libretro-common's rthreads: thread_ctrl::spawn_detached.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **`thread_ctrl::spawn_detached(callable)`** (Thread.h) runs a callable on a new detached thread made with `sthread_create` and let go with `sthread_detach`: the callable is moved to the heap and the thread frees it when it returns. It fails as a std::thread did if no thread can be made.
    - **Its three users** were detached std::threads: closing the home menu after a savestate loads (it waits 120 of the game's frames), killing the emulator when the game does not answer an exit request in time, and the trophy list's RPCN trophy sync.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits hands spawn_detached a move-only callable and checks it ran and was freed; it fails on a thread that does not free it. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a std::thread comes back to those files.


- **RPCN: its three threads on libretro-common's rthreads, not std::thread.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The RPCN client's threads** (the main one, the socket reader and the socket writer) are made with `sthread_create` and joined with `sthread_join` (libretro-common's rthreads), where they were std::threads; their bodies are unchanged. A thread that failed to start is none to join.
  - **Tests:** none of their own; the client needs the RPCN server. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::thread comes back to the RPCN client.


- **Logs: the file writer on libretro-common's rthreads, not std::thread.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The thread that writes the log file** is made with `sthread_create` and joined with `sthread_join` (libretro-common's rthreads), where it was a std::thread; its body is a member function of the writer, unchanged.
  - **Tests:** test_vfs_positional's log file case (four threads logging at once, every line there in order) goes through the writer; with no writer thread it never finishes. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::thread comes back to logs.cpp.


- **No C++ file streams: /proc and sysfs read with libretro-common's filestream, the fallback copy through fs::file.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The host's memory use** (/proc/meminfo, for the performance overlay), **the CPU's per-core use** (/proc/stat) and **the kernel's clock sources** (sysfs, read once to decide whether the TSC can be trusted) are each read whole with `filestream_read_file`, libretro-common's whole-file read (it reads to the end when, as here, the file gives no size), and looked at in place: split into lines as views, numbers with `strtoull`. They went through std::ifstream, with getline into a string per line, or a stringstream copied out once more (/proc/stat, twice a second while the overlay shows it).
    - **fs::copy_file on a host with no native copy** (not Windows, Linux, macOS or Solaris) copies through fs::file in 1 MiB blocks, as a copy to or from the frontend's VFS already did, where it went through std::ifstream and std::ofstream.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits reads the memory use and checks it has a total and some available; it fails if /proc/meminfo is not read. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a C++ file stream comes back to the core.


- **Random numbers from libretro-common's entropy source: no std::random_device, mt19937 or rand().** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A new configuration's console PSID** is 128 bits of `crypto_random_bytes` (libretro-common's entropy source: getentropy or /dev/urandom, BCrypt on Windows), where each 32 bits came from an mt19937 seeded by std::random_device.
    - **A new RPCN NPID** (`RPCS3_` and ten letters or digits) takes its ten from `crypto_random_bytes`, where it was `rand()` seeded by the wall clock: two made in the same second were the same.
    - **cellSearch's random content** (a random-order search) is picked with one draw of `crypto_random_bytes`, where it was std::sample with an mt19937 seeded by std::random_device; **cellMusic's reshuffled playlist** (shuffle with repeat all, at the end of the list) seeds its engine from `crypto_random_bytes`. std::random_device keeps 5 KiB of state on the stack.
    - If no entropy source answers, the PSID is 0, the NPID letters are all `0`, the content is the first and the shuffle is the same each time.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits draws two PSIDs and checks they are not 0 and differ; it fails on a PSID not drawn. The NPID is private to the RPCN configuration and has none. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::random_device, mt19937 or rand() comes back to the core.


- **JIT: the allocator's macOS lock removed.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **jit_runtime::alloc** (code and data memory for the PPU, SPU and RSX recompilers) took a `std::mutex` on macOS only. It goes: the allocation is a compare-and-swap on one word (jit_bump), and an allocation past the committed top commits its own range (mprotect) before it returns, so none hands out memory another thread is still committing; committing a range twice is harmless. Windows, which also commits as it goes, never took the lock. Not built or run on macOS here.
  - **Tests:** test_jit_bump's two threads now also check that every block lies inside the range its taker commits or saw committed; it fails on an allocator that does not report a new top to commit. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a lock comes back to JITASM.cpp. The core's std::lock_guard count goes to 2.


- **Threads: join waits for the thread to finish, no 1 ms wake-ups.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **thread_base::join** waits on the thread's sync word until the thread has set its result (finished or errored), which wakes that word's waiters, as each notify does; it looks again on every change. When it was called from a destructor while the emulator was stopped, it woke every 1 ms instead (an upstream workaround for threads slow to finish, marked to be removed) and, after 16 wake-ups and at each power of two, logged how long it had waited, read from the TSC. It went on waiting either way, so only the wake-ups and the log line go.
  - **Tests:** none of their own; nothing is left to see but the wait, and every suite joins threads through it. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the 1 ms wake-ups' log or a TSC read comes back to Thread.cpp.


- **SPU captures: a taken name gets a number, no one-second sleep.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Saving an SPU's local storage as an ELF** (`spu_progs/<title>_<name>_<time>_capture.elf`) when a capture already has that second's name adds a number of its own to the name (`_<base57>`, from `fs::unique_id`), where the SPU thread slept a whole second for the timestamp to change. The timestamp in the name is the wall clock on purpose: it names the file and paces nothing.
    - **Flagged, not changed:** the SPU still sleeps 1 ms on a stop-and-signal 0x001 (an upstream hack), and waits 300 µs and 200 µs on purpose to let other SPU threads take over SPURS work.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; a capture needs a running SPU. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if the one-second sleep comes back.


- **Access violations: a paused thread waits on the emulator's state, no timer or yield loop.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Every change of the emulator's state wakes its waiters:** `set_state` and a successful `state_cas` wake whoever waits on the state word, where only Run and Kill did. `Emu.wait_state_change(old)` waits until the state is no longer `old`; WaitReady is that wait on ready.
    - **A thread whose access violation pauses the emulator** (the handler waits while it is paused or ready, then pauses it and waits while paused) waits on the state word and, for a guest thread, on its own state, where it woke every 1 ms (a host thread) or yielded in a loop until the emulator updated its state (a guest thread not yet paused). Each change of either wakes it; it looks again.
    - **On Windows,** a fault outside guest memory is handed on to the next handler at once, where it was handed on after a 1 ms sleep.
    - **Flagged, not changed:** when the emulator is stopping, the handler still retries mapping the faulting page every 1 ms until it can, so the thread can see its exit flag; nothing signals that memory has been freed.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits waits on the state word while the emulator is stopped and checks the change to running wakes it; it fails if a state change wakes no one. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if a yield or sleep comes back to Thread.cpp.


- **Temporary file names without the TSC.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The number in a temporary file's name** (the file replaced in one step by fs, a VFS temporary path, the trophy loader's temporary copy, and the prefix of a virtual device) comes from `fs::unique_id` in File.h: one past the last number given, counting from 64 bits of `crypto_random_bytes`, libretro-common's entropy source, taken on the first call. It was `utils::get_unique_tsc`, which read the CPU's timestamp counter on each call; that goes from tsc.hpp. Names stay unique within a run (a counter, a compare-and-swap only the first time) and differ between runs (the random start). If no entropy source answers, the count starts at 1.
  - **Tests:** test_vfs_positional takes two numbers and checks they are one apart and not 0; it fails on a number that does not count. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if get_unique_tsc comes back.


- **std::chrono out of the core: types.hpp no longer includes it.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **types.hpp** (included by every file) no longer includes `<chrono>` or brings `steady_clock` into the global namespace. Nothing in the core uses std::chrono any more: its times are microsecond counts, on libretro-common's clock where they are real time.
    - **Five files had leaned on what <chrono> pulled in:** cctype.hpp includes `<limits>`, ISO.cpp `<ctime>` and serialization_ext.cpp `<span>` themselves; the pad handler's minimum output interval (300 ms, unused) goes.
    - **The random system name** (`RPCS3-` and three digits, for a new configuration) takes its number from `crypto_random_bytes`, libretro-common's entropy source, where it was `rand()` seeded with the wall clock.
  - **Tests:** none of their own. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to types.hpp or anywhere in the core's sources.


- **Network (Emu/NP): its times on libretro-common's clock, off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Every time in Emu/NP** is a microsecond reading of `cpu_features_get_time_usec`, libretro-common's monotonic clock, where it was a std::chrono steady-clock time point or duration: RPCN's UDP pings to the signaling server (every 5 s, every 500 ms until answered) and its TLS handshake's 10 s bound; the signaling manager's packet queue (each packet's wake-up time, its resend delays of 200 and 500 ms, the 10 s ping and the 60 s timeout of a silent peer) and the timestamps it sends for round-trip times; the clan server's 30 s request bound; and the local side of the network time handed to games. These are the protocols' own timers, real time on purpose.
    - **A silent peer's 60 s timeout** compares the last message's time plus 60 s with now, so a peer whose time was taken after the manager's `now` is not seen as timed out (as before, with the unsigned times).
    - **A clan request** waits on its transfer (`net_http_wait`) for the time left, where it woke every 100 ms to read the clock again: `net_http_wait` returns as soon as the transfer can go on.
    - **Flagged, not changed:** RPCN's TLS handshake still polls its socket in 50 ms steps so it can see a termination.
  - **Tests:** none of their own; these need the RPCN and clan servers or a peer. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to Emu/NP.


- **RB3 MIDI drums: hit and combo times on libretro-common's clock, off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A drum hit's expiry** (each hit is held for the pulse width, 30 ms by default, so the game sees it) and **a combo's** (the window, 2 s by default, to play the notes that mean start, select or hold kick) are microsecond readings of `cpu_features_get_time_usec`, libretro-common's monotonic clock, where they were std::chrono steady-clock time points. The pulse width and window are microsecond counts from the same settings. The times are real time: a hit is held as long whatever the frame rate.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the device needs a host MIDI port (rtmidi). The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to the drums.


- **Loader: ISO dates and the IRD check's times off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A file's date in an ISO** (ECMA-119: the recorder's local date and time, and its offset from GMT in 15-minute steps) is turned into a UNIX time by counting days from 1970 with `iso_days_from_civil` (H. Hinnant's days_from_civil, the proleptic Gregorian calendar, in ISO.h) and adding the seconds, where it went through std::chrono's sys_days and durations. The result is the same; the host's time zone still never enters it.
    - **The IRD check's closing log line** (milliseconds reading the IRD and listing the game, and hashing it) reads `cpu_features_get_time_usec`, libretro-common's monotonic clock, where it read std::chrono's steady clock. It times the check for the log, and paces nothing.
  - **Tests:** three static assertions in ISO.h check days_from_civil on 1970-01-01, 2000-03-01 and 1969-12-31; the build fails on a count that does not move January and February to the year before. The IRD timing has none: it is a log line. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to either loader.


- **Savestates: their creation date without std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The date a savestate records** (local time, `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS`) is `time()` formatted by `date_time::fmt_time`, the same strftime format, where it was std::chrono's system clock formatted through a formatter of its own in Utilities/date_time.cpp. That file goes: it held only that formatter. The date is the wall clock on purpose: it says when the state was made, and paces nothing.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; writing a savestate needs a running game. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to System.cpp or date_time.cpp comes back.


- **Utilities/sync.h removed.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The futex wrapper** (a Linux futex syscall, emulated elsewhere with a mutex and a condition variable per bucket, its timeout a std::chrono duration) goes: no file included it any more; every wait in the core is retro_waitaddr's.
  - **Tests:** none; nothing used it. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if the file or an include of it comes back.


- **Pads: two unused clock fields removed.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **PadDevice's last output and last reconnect times** (`steady_clock` time points, 16 bytes a device) go: nothing read or wrote them since the host pad handlers left for the libretro one, which takes its pads from retro_run's input.
  - **Tests:** none; nothing used them. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to the pad handler.


- **cellGame: the new game data's temporary directory named without the clock.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **cellGameCreateGameData's temporary directory** (`/dev_hdd0/game/_GDATA_<number>`, where the game writes its new data before cellGameContentPermit moves it) is numbered with 64 bits from `crypto_random_bytes`, libretro-common's entropy source, where the number was the steady clock's reading. It is as many digits at most (20), so the paths handed back to the game fit as before; a number already taken fails the directory's creation, as a clash did before.
    - **Flagged, not changed:** cellGame waits on purpose in 20 places (`lv2_sleep`, 300 µs to 300 ms), standing in for the time the firmware's calls take; this one waits 50 ms before making the directory. These are the guest's timing, and stay for now.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the call needs a running game's HLE thread. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to cellGame.cpp.


- **Media: the shuffled playlist seeded from libretro-common's entropy, off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A game's music played shuffled** (cellSearch's shuffle option, through the audio decoder) is shuffled once with an engine seeded by `crypto_random_bytes`, libretro-common's platform entropy source (getentropy or /dev/urandom, BCrypt on Windows), where it took the steady clock's reading as the seed. If no source answers, the seed is 0 and the order is the same each time. std::random_device stays out: its state is 5 KiB on the stack.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the path needs a game's music search context and the audio decoder. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to media_utils.cpp.


- **Logs: message stamps on libretro-common's clock, off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **A log message's stamp** (microseconds since the first message) reads `cpu_features_get_time_usec`, libretro-common's monotonic microsecond clock, on every host. It read std::chrono's steady clock, or on Windows QueryPerformanceCounter with its own arithmetic (the same arithmetic libretro-common's does). The stamp is one subtraction from the first reading, kept in a static.
  - **Tests:** test_vfs_positional logs two messages 2 ms apart by that clock and checks their stamps are 2000 to 500000 apart; it fails on stamps in nanoseconds. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono or a clock of its own comes back to logs.cpp.


- **Overlays: their stopwatch and button auto-repeat on libretro-common's clock, off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Utilities/Timer.h** (the stopwatch the overlays use: the performance overlay's update and frame times, and the dialogs' input loop) reads `cpu_features_get_time_usec`, libretro-common's monotonic microsecond clock, where it read std::chrono's steady clock. Its times are plain microsecond counts; `Timer::now_us()` gives a reading. The nanosecond getter goes: nothing used it.
    - **A dialog's button auto-repeat** (a held button repeats after 500 ms, then at the dialog's interval) keeps its per-pad press times as microsecond readings where they were steady-clock time points. The input loop is still woken once a frame; the repeat goes by real time, as before.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits checks that a stopped timer keeps its time and that the milliseconds since a reading 5 ms before it started are 5; it fails on a timer that gives microseconds there. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to Timer.h or the overlays' input loop.


- **Shader cache loading: its dialog paced by frontend frames, off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **With one worker** (the cache loaded on the calling thread, ten entries at a time), the loading dialog is updated when the frontend's frame count (`g_libretro_events`) has moved since the last update, and after the last entry. Before, it read the steady clock after each ten entries and updated when 16 ms had passed. With several workers it already went by the frame count.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the path needs a shader cache on disk and the dialog. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono comes back to rsx_cache.h.


- **Threads: wait_for_accurate off std::chrono.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **thread_ctrl::wait_for_accurate** (the SPU wake-up delay's wait) is one timed wait on every host, as it already was on Linux. On Windows and macOS it was a loop that waited in 500 µs steps, then yielded, then busy-waited 100 cycles at a time, reading the steady clock after each step until the time was up.
    - **The wait itself stays**: it is the SPU Wake-Up Delay setting's, off by default (0 µs), a per-game workaround that delays an SPU on purpose. It is flagged here, not removed. A delay that is shorter than the host's scheduler step may now run long on Windows, where it was spun out exactly.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the delay is only reached with the setting on, and the changed path is not Linux's. The Windows (MinGW) syntax check, the full build and suites pass. A check fails if std::chrono or a busy-wait comes back to Thread.cpp.


- **util/atomic.hpp and util/atomic.cpp removed.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The two files are gone,** with their 18 includes, the forward declaration of atomic_t in types.hpp and their build lines in Makefile.common and the CMake list. Nothing in the core used atomic_t, atomic_storage, the atomic_wait engine or its fences any more: every word is a retro_atomic one, every wait a retro_waitaddr one.
    - **The TSC stamp for unique file names** (`utils::get_unique_tsc`, used for temporary file names in fs, VFS and the trophy loader) moves to tsc.hpp, with its last-given stamp a `retro_atomic_64_t`: a compare-and-swap when the stamp is past it, a fetch-and-add of one when not, as before. It is a clock read, flagged for the std::chrono pass; it names files, it paces nothing.
    - **lv2's thread wake** changes its thread's flags with a plain lambda where it used the header's AOFN macro, its last user. The Vulkan sampler pool includes `<functional>` itself, which it had through the header.
  - **Tests:** test_vfs_positional's test of atomic_t's wait and notify goes with it (retro_waitaddr is tested in RetroArch). The old ring the rsxaudio test compares against keeps its two pointers plain where they were a 16-byte atomic_t; the test uses it from one thread, and its benchmark baseline no longer pays a 16-byte compare-and-swap per call. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if either file, an include of it or atomic_t's declaration comes back.


- **The SPU's reservation waits on retro_waitaddr, off the atomic_wait engine.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **vm::reservation_notifier_wait** waits on the waiter's flag with `retro_waitaddr_wait`, its bound in microseconds where it was nanoseconds (the four SPU waits keep theirs: 50, 100, 100 and 200 µs).
    - **The SPU's lost-reservation check** (GETLLAR's wait for its line to change) is passed to the wait as a condition, where it was a thread-local one-time callback the engine called: asked before each wait with the waits so far, the wait goes on until it says to stop or the flag changes. Each wait is still 100 µs long, so a thread whose reservation nobody notifies wakes every 100 µs to check it; that was so before, and is flagged here, not changed.
    - **Two notifies** (lv2's postponed wakes, and the one the SPU LLVM recompiler emits after PUTLLC16) call `retro_waitaddr_notify_all` themselves, where they went through the engine's, which did the same.
    - **A thread's exit log line** no longer gives its time waited and spurious wakes (`Wait:[…]`): they were counted by a callback the engine called on its own waits, which nothing makes any more; the TSC read at each wait goes with it.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits waits with a condition that says stop after its fifth ask, and checks it was asked five times; it fails on a wait that asks once. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if atomic_wait comes back outside util/atomic.


- **The last atomic_storage calls on retro_atomic.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Thirteen loads, stores and exchanges** of plain fields in nine files are retro_atomic's of the same width: the scheduler's and lv2's look at another thread's reservation address and the CPU thread's pc (acquire loads), a PPU module export's address and the PPU thread's TOC and cia (acquire loads, and an exchange marking a TOC done), cellMusic's valid flag and sys_prx's loaded flags (the new 8-bit word), the raw SPU's entry pc (a release store), and the TSC frequency set at start (a release store where it was a seq_cst one; it is set once before anything reads it).
    - **The PPU interpreter's dispatch** loads the next function with `retro_atomic_load_acquire_ptr`, or `retro_atomic_load_relaxed_ptr` where it used atomic_storage's observe: a plain load either way. PPUInterpreter.o is 68 bytes smaller, with as many locked instructions as before (119).
  - **Tests:** none of their own; they read and write fields of running threads. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if atomic_storage comes back outside util/atomic.


- **vm's page flags on retro_atomic, off atomic_t: the last atomic_t in the core.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The page table** (`vm::g_pages`, a byte of flags per 4 KB page of the 4 GB guest space) is an array of `retro_atomic_8_t`, the new 8-bit word, where it was `atomic_t<u8>`. Its 22 uses in vm.h and vm.cpp say what they do: `retro_atomic_load_acquire_8` where atomic_t read it implicitly or with load(), `retro_atomic_store_release_8` for its release(), and `retro_atomic_exchange_8` where a page is mapped. The savestate writes the byte loaded, and the table is cleared through a void pointer as before. Each is still one byte access: a plain mov on x86, ldarb or stlrb on aarch64.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the table is guest memory's, which the tests do not map. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if atomic_t comes back to vm.h or vm.cpp.


- **vm::try_access's narrow guest writes on retro_atomic.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **An aligned write of 1, 2, 4, 8 or 16 bytes** to guest memory through `vm::try_access` (the debugger's and patches' way in) is a retro_atomic release store of that width: the new 8- and 16-bit words for a byte or halfword, the 32- and 64-bit words, and the 128-bit word for 16 bytes. Each is one access of its width, as atomic_storage's release was; on x86 the code is the same (plain moves, movdqa for 16 bytes, no locked instruction).
  - **Tests:** none of its own; try_access needs guest memory mapped. The 8- and 16-bit words are tested in RetroArch's retro_atomic_test (a byte store leaves its neighbours as they were, under four threads too). The full build and suites pass. A check fails if atomic_storage comes back to vm.cpp.


- **libretro-common resynced from RetroArch master again: retro_atomic.h.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **retro_atomic.h** is RetroArch's again, with its new 8- and 16-bit words (`retro_atomic_8_t`, `retro_atomic_16_t`: acquire load, release store, relaxed load and store, exchange, each one access of its width), for vm.cpp's narrow guest writes and the page flags. The core builds against it as it is.
  - **Tests:** the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the copy's retro_atomic.h falls behind the 8- and 16-bit words again.


- **SPUCommonRecompiler off atomic_storage.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The patch points the SPU recompiler rewrites in place** while other threads may be running the code (the dispatcher's jump back to the dispatcher on a failed check, the branch patcher, and the reverse-branch patch) land whole through `spu_patch_store_64` on x86, a `retro_atomic_store_release_64`, and `spu_patch_store_128` on aarch64, a `retro_atomic_store_release_128`. On x86 that is the same plain 8-byte store; on aarch64 it is one LDXP/STLXP loop, as atomic_storage's was where the CPU has no LSE2 (where it has, atomic_storage used a dmb and an stp).
    - **The profiler's read** of each SPU's block hash is a `retro_atomic` acquire load.
  - **Tests:** none of their own; the rewrites happen as SPU code runs on its threads. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if atomic_storage comes back to SPUCommonRecompiler.cpp.


- **libretro-common resynced from RetroArch master again.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Three files** are RetroArch's again: retro_atomic.h, whose 128-bit release store, acquire load and exchange are now one exclusive-pair loop each on aarch64 (no compare-and-swap loop), rmp4.c and stdstring.c. The core builds against them as it is.
  - **Tests:** the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the copy's retro_atomic.h falls behind the aarch64 128-bit store again.


- **SPUThread off atomic_storage.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **PUTLLC's 16-byte write**, when only one 16-byte part of the line differs, is a compare-and-swap of that part. It goes through `spu_cas_16`, `retro_atomic_cas_seen_128` on the line, which like `atomic_storage<u128>::compare_exchange` gives the caller what was there when it fails. It is the same lock cmpxchg16b: 49 in SPUThread.o before and after.
    - **The block statistics' reads** (the block counter and hash shown in the debugger's thread text), the SPURS thread-limit hack's exchange of `max_run`, and the scheduler's look at another thread's reservation address are `retro_atomic` acquire loads and an exchange on the same fields. A load of a local copy of the hash is a plain read.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the 16-byte path needs a reservation on an SPU thread with accurate reservations off, and the rest is debugger text and a game hack. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if atomic_storage comes back to SPUThread.cpp.


- **The lv2 sleep queues' links on retro_atomic.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The nine release stores** of a sleep queue's head and next links (unqueue, schedule and emplace in sys_sync.h) go through `lv2_obj::release_link`, a `retro_atomic_store_release_ptr` of the plain pointer slot, in place of `atomic_storage<T*>::release`. The queues are changed under their lock but read by other threads without it, so the stores stay release stores; the code is the same plain store on x86.
  - **Tests:** test_lv2_events takes the head, a middle and the last node out of a four-node queue, the last without clearing its link, and checks that a node not on it is not found. A mutant `release_link` that stores nothing fails it. A check fails if atomic_storage comes back to sys_sync.h.


- **The last fences on retro_atomic.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The remaining 15 `atomic_fence_*` calls** are retro_atomic's fences, one for one: the PPU interpreter's (sync, eieio, the reservation), the SPU interpreter's and threads' (sync, dsync, the channels and the reservation), the raw SPU's MMIO, the PPU thread's, the RSX FIFO's and the NV47 sync and NV406E semaphore writes, cellSpurs' two and sys_lwmutex's two. The seq_cst ones are a locked or on x86 as before (no mfence in PPUInterpreter.o, SPUInterpreter.o, SPUThread.o, PPUThread.o or RSXFIFO.o); the acquire and acq_rel ones are compiler barriers on x86 as before. Nothing calls util/atomic.hpp's fences now.
  - **Tests:** none of their own; they order the guest's memory, which the tests do not run on threads that race. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if an atomic_fence_* comes back.


- **libretro-common resynced from RetroArch master again.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **Eight files** are RetroArch's again: retro_atomic.h, whose seq_cst fence is now a locked or on x86 with Clang as with GCC (no mfence), encoding_utf and utf.h, file_path and file_path.h, audio_transfer, and rmp4 and rmp4.h. The core builds against them as it is.
  - **Tests:** the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the copy's retro_atomic.h falls behind the locked-or fence again.


- **cellSync's fences on retro_atomic.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The seven acquire-release fences** (after a mutex is taken, around the barrier and rwm calls, after a queue's or rwm's fields are written and after an LFQueue is found already set up) are `retro_atomic_thread_fence_acq_rel` in place of util/atomic.hpp's `atomic_fence_acq_rel`. On x86 both are a compiler barrier only (no mfence in cellSync.o); on aarch64 both are one dmb ish.
  - **Tests:** none of its own; the fences order the guest's memory around the calls, which the tests do not run. The full build and suites pass. A check fails if atomic_fence_* comes back to cellSync.


- **libretro-common resynced from RetroArch master.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **42 files** that had fallen behind RetroArch's are RetroArch's again, among them retro_atomic.h (with the new `retro_atomic_thread_fence_acq_rel`), file_stream, the VFS, net_http and the SSL socket, rjson, rmp4, rflac, the image and audio formats, crypto, rthreads and its eventcount, rtime, features_cpu and libretro.h. Six headers they now include come with them: retro_assert.h, retro_horizon.h, formats/rlpcm.h, formats/rmodtracker.h, formats/video_audio_track.h and gfx/shaders/spirv_words.h.
    - **The core needed no change** to build against them.
  - **Tests:** the full build and suites pass. A check fails if the copy's retro_atomic.h falls behind again (no acq_rel fence).


- **test_emu_waits: the libretro_wait_on tests no longer race their own waiter.** Test only.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The two tests that check a retro_run wakes `libretro_wait_on`** fired one event as soon as the waiter's count moved, and failed now and then: the waiter takes the event count as it goes back into its wait, so an event fired before then is not one it saw, and it slept on. It showed in a full suite run after the libretro-common resync, whose eventcount changes move the timing. They now fire events until the waiter returns, with a bound.
  - **Tests:** ten runs of test_emu_waits in a row pass. A mutant `libretro_wait_on` that does not wait on the events at all still fails it (the run hangs until it is killed). A check fails if a test goes back to firing one event and expecting it seen.


- **The standalone waits on retro_waitaddr, off atomic_wait::list.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **`thread_ctrl::wait_list(list, count, usec)`** waits on a `retro_waitaddr_t` array: untimed, until one of its words is not what it was, through spurious wakes; timed, once. `wait_on_custom` goes through it too.
    - **Moved to it, or to a plain retro_waitaddr loop on one word:** libmixer's, cellGcmSys's (the FIFO get), the shader-loading dialog's and the RSX cache's waits, the RSX thread's two start-up waits, thread_ctrl::wait_for, a named thread's join, a task's done wait, the launch retainer, the lockless queue's consumer wait, the bitset's wait and the SPU function's compile wait. They wait for the same words with the same old values as before.
    - **Flagged, not changed:** a join from a destructor after a stop still waits 1 ms at a time and logs a "too sleepy" line from the CPU's timestamp counter, as it did. The SPU reservation wait stays on atomic_wait::list for now: it needs the wait engine's one-time callback, and goes with the SPU code.
  - **Tests:** test_emu_waits and test_held_queues run through these waits, and the full build and suites pass. A mutant `wait_list` that waits on only the first word of its list fails test_emu_waits: a named thread waiting for a frame is told to end through a later word of its list, is not woken, and the check that its join ends fails. A check fails if an atomic_wait::list comes back outside the SPU reservation wait.


- **thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom on retro_waitaddr, off atomic_wait::list.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:**
    - **The list** a named thread waits on (the caller's words, then the thread's own sync word and its task queue) is a `retro_waitaddr_t` array, waited on with `retro_waitaddr_wait`, where it was an `atomic_wait::list` going through atomic_t's wait engine to the same retro_waitaddr. A setter gets the array and fills its entries: `{address, old}` for a raw word, `thread_ctrl::waitaddr(word, old)` for a typed 32-bit one (a cpu state's flags, a queue's change count), `{x.wait_word(), 0}` for an atomic_ptr. 45 setters in 26 files are moved, as are `wait_on`, `wait_on_word` and the waits built on them.
    - **The steps are the same:** an untimed wait sleeps until one of the words is not what it was, through spurious wakes; a timed one waits once.
