Two threads now stay for 20000 rounds and meet before each install, as the NP transaction test's threads do.
  - **Checked:** the test now fails with the install's compare-and-swap made a plain store. The NP transaction claim test was checked the same way, with the claim made a load and a store, and it already failed. The 0456 entry's note on the test's threads is corrected: they were not held to one CPU, they just did not overlap.


- **PPU thread and SPU thread group priorities are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** each priority (its level, its enqueue order and, for a PPU thread, the bit kept for a savestate's load) is one `retro_atomic_64_t`. `get_prio()` reads it whole, `set_prio()` writes it, and `prio_op()` changes it with a compare-and-swap loop, as `atomic_op` did. The scheduler's queue code (sys_sync.h), lv2's priority changes, the sync primitives' priority look-ups, sys_ppu_thread and sys_spu use them.
  - **SPU threads:** an SPU thread's priority is its group's. The little `priority_t` proxy that stood in for it is replaced by `get_prio()` and `prio_op()` on the SPU thread itself.
  - **Savestates:** a PPU thread's priority is written and read through the `u64` it was, and an SPU group's as the `u64` it was, so the format is unchanged.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` makes an SPU thread group and checks that its priority is the one it was made with. Two threads, each held to a CPU of its own, then change the order 200000 times each with none lost, and the level stays as it was. It was shown to fail with the compare-and-swap made a plain store. Left to the scheduler, the two threads did not overlap enough to show a lost change; the race only showed once each was put on a CPU of its own. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the priorities.


- **The PPU and SPU threads' debugger mode is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the mode that picks how a thread's registers are dumped is a `retro_atomic_int_t` on both threads. Nothing in the core sets it any more (the debugger is not in the core), so it stays at its default, as before.
  - **Tests:** none: nothing changes at runtime. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to it.


- **A PPU thread's joiner status is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the status is a `retro_atomic_int_t`. It holds joinable, detached, zombie or exited, or the id of the thread joining it. `get_joiner()` reads it. `joiner_op()` changes it with a compare-and-swap loop and gives back what `atomic_op` or `fetch_op` gave. sys_ppu_thread's exit, join and detach use them. An exiting thread that waits as a zombie for its joiner waits through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`.
  - **Savestates:** the status is read through `ar.pop<ppu_join_status>()` and written as the `ppu_join_status` it was, so the format is unchanged.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: a PPU thread cannot be made without a game. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the joiner.


- **A PPU thread's command and start gate words are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** five words are now `retro_atomic_int_t`:
    - the command notify word, which interrupt threads wait on and which sys_interrupt, sys_ppu_thread and the RSX set;
    - the start gate's caller, the caller waiting in sys_ppu_thread_start for the new thread to reach its entry command;
    - the start gate's open flag;
    - the entry-reached flag;
    - the scheduler watch word, which every scheduler edge put to the thread moves.
  - Every wait on them goes through `set_raw` and `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`, as before on the same words. Nothing saved in savestates changes.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: they are moved only by a game's PPU threads and syscalls, which the test machine cannot run. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to these words.


- **PPUThread.cpp's own atomics are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game. `ppu_thread`'s fields, in PPUThread.h, are next.
  - **What changed:** the precompiler's file counter and its memory budget are `retro_atomic`. A file takes its share of the budget with a compare-and-swap loop, where `fetch_op` was, and a worker with none left waits through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`. Also converted: the LLVM workers' job and done counters and thread index, the breakpoint table's address range, the pause-all-on-breakpoint flag, the precompile-disable flag, the savestate's saved and loaded tags, and the flag a PPU thread waits on until the first one is initialised. That last wait also goes through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`. Nothing saved in savestates changes.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: these need a game's modules compiled or a PPU thread run. The existing tests that boot the core run through the precompiler's set-up. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to PPUThread.cpp.


- **Logging is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the log file writer's buffer and written-byte counters are `retro_atomic_64_t`s, each on its own 128-byte line as before. A line takes its room with a compare-and-swap loop, where `fetch_op` was. The listener list's links are `retro_atomic_ptr_t`, and a new listener is added at the end with a compare-and-swap. The started flag is a `retro_atomic_int_t`. A channel's enabled level is a `retro_atomic_int_t`, read with a relaxed load as `observe()` did, so the check on every log line costs the same.
  - **Channels are 32 bytes:** a message finds its channel and its level from its own address (the address rounded down, and its low 3 bits). The level used to be one byte that filled a 16-byte channel exactly. The 4-byte word does not fit, so a channel is now 32 bytes, 32-aligned, with the seven messages at offsets 25 to 31. The lookup rounds to 32, and static asserts hold the layout.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks that each of a channel's seven messages finds the channel and its own level, and that the enabled level lets through what it should and nothing else. It was shown to fail with the lookup still rounding to 16. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to logging.


- **The video provider is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the recording's active flag is a `retro_atomic_int_t`, and its start time a `retro_atomic_64_t`. The start time is still set once, by whichever of the first frame or the first samples comes first, with a compare-and-swap from "unset".
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: the provider needs a recording's video sink and encoder. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to it.


- **The compressed savestate writers are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** for the deflate writer, the pending byte count is a `retro_atomic_64_t` and the pending wake and signal words are `retro_atomic_int_t`s. The count's "add or mark waiting" update is a compare-and-swap loop, and a writer over the memory bound waits on the wake word with `retro_waitaddr`. For the zstd writer, the error flag, the taken word that the compression and writer threads wait on, and the output and thread buffer indexes are `retro_atomic`. The writer thread keeps its own index and publishes it for `finalize()`. Two zstd fields nothing used are gone. The bytes written are unchanged.
  - **Tests:** `test_savestate_memory` writes and reads back two zstd savestates through the writer's threads. It was shown to hang (timed out) with the taken word's wake taken out. The deflate writer's wait needs 64 MB pending to be reached, and has no runtime test. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to serialization_ext.


- **NV4097's log counts are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the count of times each unknown render mode has been seen (logged as an error the first 10 times, then as a trace) is a `retro_atomic_int_t`, raised by a compare-and-swap loop that stops at 10, as `try_inc` did. The report and notify writes into the game's memory are left for the guest memory work.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: the count only moves as a game sets an unknown render mode, and it only picks a log level. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to it.


- **The RSX IO map is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the two 4096-entry tables (the effective address each IO megabyte maps to, and the IO address each effective megabyte is mapped at) are `retro_atomic_int_t`s. They are read and set through `get_ea()`, `get_io()`, `set_ea()` and `set_io()`, which sys_rsx, cellGcmSys, the FIFO and the RSX thread use.
  - **Savestates:** the first 512 entries are written and read through a `u32` array, the bytes `atomic_t<u32>` made, so the format is unchanged. On a load, the reverse map is filled in from them as before.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks that the map's savestate is byte for byte the 512 `u32`s, and that reading it back fills in the reverse map and nothing past the saved entries. It was shown to fail with the reverse map left unfilled. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the IO map.


- **The RSX's atomic bitmask is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** `rsx::atomic_bitmask_t` (the RSX thread's async flip and interrupt masks, the overlays' status flags, and the Vulkan renderer's flush queue status and runtime state) keeps its bits in a `retro_atomic_int_t`, or a `retro_atomic_64_t` for a 64-bit mask. Set, clear and test are `retro_atomic` fetch-or and fetch-and. A wait on the mask, used by the Vulkan renderer's flush queue, is a `retro_waitaddr` wait, and is offered only for 32-bit masks. bitfield.hpp no longer includes util/atomic.hpp. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks a 32-bit and a 64-bit mask, each with its top bit set, and that `clear()` gives back the bits left. It also checks that a waiter wakes as the mask changes, 300 times. It was shown to fail with `clear()` giving back the bits before, and with the wake taken out. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to bitfield.hpp.


- **The program state cache's shader id counter is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the counter that numbers each vertex and fragment program as it is compiled is a `retro_atomic_64_t`, taken with `retro_atomic_fetch_add_64`. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: the counter is taken only as a renderer compiles a program. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the program state cache.


- **The game-window-focused flag is gone.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** `is_input_allowed()` read an `atomic_t<bool>` that nothing in the core ever set. In the core the frontend owns the window and its focus, so the flag was always false. The function now reads the background input setting alone, which is what it already came to. Behaviour is unchanged.
  - **Tests:** none: nothing changes at runtime. A check fails if the flag comes back.


- **The shader cache's loading workers are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the count the workers take shaders by as they load and compile the on-disk cache is a `retro_atomic_int_t`, taken with `retro_atomic_fetch_add_int`. The thread updating the loading dialog waits on it with `set_raw`, woken as a worker finishes or once a frontend frame. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: the workers need a renderer's pipeline storage. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the shader cache.


- **The RSX context's unsent events are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the event bits left unsent when the RSX or VBLANK thread stops (sent again on a savestate's load) are a `retro_atomic_64_t`. sys_rsx adds to them with `retro_atomic_fetch_or_64`, and the renderer takes them whole with an exchange.
  - **Savestates:** written and read through a `u64` temporary, the 8 bytes `atomic_t<u64>` made, so the format is unchanged.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: they are set as sys_rsx fails to send an event to a stopped thread, and saved with the whole RSX thread, which the test machine cannot run. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back or the field is saved directly again.


- **ZCULL's own fields are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the two report pages' accessed flags and the count of critical reports in flight are `retro_atomic_int_t`. The report written into the game's memory through an `atomic_t` view is left for the guest memory work. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: they are set as the renderer writes ZCULL reports and the game's memory is accessed, which needs a running RSX. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to these two fields.


- **The RSX DMA offloader is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** its enqueued and processed job counts are `retro_atomic_64_t`, and its memory fault flag is a `retro_atomic_int_t`. A sync off the renderer thread waited on the 64-bit processed count. It now waits on a 32-bit word that the offloader moves each time it has caught up and as it ends, through `retro_waitaddr`. The sync on the renderer thread is unchanged (`wait_attending`). Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: the offloader's thread calls into the current renderer as it catches up, and the test machine runs none. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the offloader.


- **The rest of the Vulkan renderer is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** a fence's flushed flag (which `wait_flush` waits on, now with `retro_waitaddr`), the descriptor set's storage cache id, the unique resource id counter, the DMA pool's allocated size and the libretro image generation are `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`. No Vulkan file includes util/atomic.hpp any more. The texture cache's own flag is left for the texture cache work. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: a fence and everything else here needs a Vulkan device. A check fails if `atomic_t` or util/atomic.hpp comes back to the Vulkan renderer outside the texture cache.


- **The Vulkan renderer's flags and counters are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the samplers-dirty flag, a flush request's pending flag, the command buffer ring's index, the cached render pass key and the pipeline compiler's thread index are `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: they need a Vulkan device. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to these files.


- **The Vulkan async compute scheduler is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** its submit count and its event and semaphore pool counters are `retro_atomic_64_t`, and its sync-required flag is a `retro_atomic_int_t`. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: the scheduler needs a Vulkan device and compute queues. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to it.


- **The Vulkan driver manager and event counters are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the driver manager's last requested and last completed event ids are `retro_atomic_64_t`, and the two words its thread and a drain wait on are `retro_atomic_int_t`. Both waits go through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`, so they also wake as their thread is told to end. The resource manager's event counter and last completed event are `retro_atomic_64_t`. The two "raise to at least" updates are compare-and-swap loops that never lower the value. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` runs the driver manager's thread and checks, over 200 rounds with events raised out of order, that each drain returns because the thread woke it, before its own thread is joined. It was shown to fail with the driver thread's wake taken out. The resource manager's counters have no runtime test of their own, because they need a Vulkan device. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back.


- **The GL renderer is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the samplers-dirty flag, a shader's compile state, the pipeline compiler's context-ready flag and its thread index counter are `retro_atomic_int_t`. A thread that finds a shader being compiled by another waits on its compile state with `retro_waitaddr`, woken as the other is done. glutils/program.h no longer includes util/atomic.hpp. Nothing is saved in savestates.
  - **Tests:** none at runtime: everything here needs a GL context, which the test machine does not have. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the GL renderer.


- **PPUModule is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the liblv2 address range (which PPUThread reads to skip lwmutex notifications) and the libusbd flag (which sys_usbd reads) are `retro_atomic_int_t`. The PRX name table, read with no lock by the thread that names a crashing address, holds `retro_atomic_ptr_t`s. The library lock map is touched only on the HLE thread (`hle_call`), so its flags are plain `bool`s with no atomic at all. Nothing is saved in savestates.

  - **What changed:** the three flags a game's content check sets (whether it may create data, whether the data exists, and the check mode) are `retro_atomic_int_t`. Nothing is saved in savestates.

- **cellDmuxPamf's queue is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.

That word is now a `retro_atomic_int_t`, the same 4 bytes, so savestates (the game's memory) are unchanged. A waiter on an empty queue waits through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`, so it wakes on an entry or as its thread is told to end.

- **sys_uart is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the AV UART's receive and send claim words and its three ring counters (what the AV thread, a blocking send and a blocking receive wait on) are `retro_atomic_int_t`. Their waits keep the same words, now through `set_raw` and `retro_waitaddr`. The AV thread's own wait keeps the game's timeout, a guest-clock wait. sys_uart.h no longer includes util/atomic.hpp. Nothing is saved in savestates.

A port's volume step (its two floats) is one `retro_atomic_64_t`, set and read whole. The period counter that cellRec waits on, the initialized flag and the device-changed flag are `retro_atomic` too. cellRec's video thread waits on the period counter through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`, so it also wakes as it is told to end.
  - **Savestates:** ports are still saved bitwise, and `static_assert`s hold their size and the offsets of the state and the volume step to what `atomic_t` made. The initialized flag is written and read as the `u8` it was. The format is unchanged.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks a port's bytes: its state as a `u32` and its volume step as two floats in order. It also checks that its copy keeps both, and that the state's compare-and-swap gives back the state it was. It was shown to fail with the two floats packed the other way round. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to cellAudio.

A net_http caller and a thread waiting for another's IPv6 check wait on their word with `retro_waitaddr`. The signaling thread waits through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`, so it wakes on a packet or call, at the next packet resend (a network timeout), or as it is told to end. Nothing is saved in savestates.

It was shown to fail with the wake taken out. The signaling thread, UPnP and the IPv6 check have no runtime test, because they need the network. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to Emu/NP.

A transaction's waiter and the QuickMatch timeout worker wait through `thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`, so they wake on the word, on their deadline, or as their thread is told to end. The QuickMatch wait keeps the game's own timeout, a network timeout. Nothing is saved in savestates.

The QuickMatch worker has no runtime test, because it needs an RPCN server. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the NP contexts.

The send message dialog's confirmation flags, the friends list's dirty flag and its current and last page, the trophy list's dirty flag and sync status, the trophy notification queue's two counters, the overlay manager's id counter and its input thread's abort and interrupt flags, the home menu's screenshot and recording requests, and the shader loading dialog's count of queued main thread calls are `retro_atomic` too. Nothing is saved in savestates.

It was shown to fail with a wait that returned while one call was left. The dialogs' done flags and the other fields have no runtime test, because they need a running overlay and its input. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the overlays.

- **cellSysutil is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.

The emulator-available flag and the pause mark are `retro_atomic_int_t` too. `System.cpp` has no `atomic_t` left.

- **The RPCN client is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the connected, logged-in, wanted and stopping flags, the connection state, the counts of connections and logins tried that their waiters wait on, the reader's and writer's busy flags, the event count the reader moves, the two request counters and the signaling addresses and port are `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`, and their waits are on `retro_waitaddr`. `num_failures`, which nothing used, is gone.
  - **Waits kept:** the client is made once, the first time a thread asks for it. A second thread asking meanwhile waits until it is made, since making it starts its threads and its connection, and a second one made and thrown away would connect twice. The 10 seconds given to the server for the signaling address and port, and the TLS handshake's deadline, are network timeouts, which are kept exceptions.
  - **Tests:** none of its own. The client starts its network threads as it is made, and these words have no logic apart from them. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back.

That is about 420 uses across sceNp, sceNp2, sceNpCommerce2, sceNpTus, cellNetCtl and the friends list. The matching 2 request ids keep their 16-bit wrap.
  - **The clan ticket:** the wait for the server's answer is on `retro_waitaddr`, with the same 60 seconds. It is a network timeout, one of the kept exceptions, not pacing.
  - **Savestates:** the flags are written and read through a `bool` array, so the format is unchanged.
  - **Tests:** none of its own. The NP handler needs its network threads to be made, and these flags have no logic of their own to test. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back, or if the savestate stops writing the flags as bools.


- **cellCamera is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the camera thread's state that the game's threads read before their calls reach it (initialised, open, attached, streaming, the read mode, the frame count and the bytes read, the frame time, the buffer being written and the two buffers' locked flags) is `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`. So is what it shares with cellGem (the frame's time, width, height, size and format). The rest stays the camera thread's alone, as before.
  - **Savestates:** written and read through the types the fields were (`u8`, `bool`, `u32`, `u64`, `s32` and the format), so the format is unchanged.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks that both savestates are byte for byte what the `atomic_t` fields made, and read back. It was shown to fail with one flag saved as an `s32`. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to cellCamera.


- **Demon's Souls: Reduce Audio Latency, in the Patch Manager.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What it does:** the game's sound engine (MagicOrchestra2 v2.6.18) mixes into a ring of `PS3MixerBlockSize` blocks of 256 samples, 16 by default (85 ms at 48 kHz), and refills it every `PS3MixerUpdateFrewuency` blocks (sic), 4 by default (21 ms). With the option on, they are 8 and 2: a new sound waits on half as much of the ring before it plays. It is the counterpart of Bloodborne's FMOD buffer going from 4 blocks to 2 in parallel-ps4.
  - **Where the values are:** found in BLUS30443's EBOOT.BIN. The engine reads its setup from a text built into the executable: it hands the text to a memory stream with its length, 0x450. `PS3_GAME/USRDIR/sound/magicorchestra2.ini` on the disc is not that text, and its name is nowhere in the executable. The two keys are read with defaults of 16 and 4 and are in neither text. The block count goes to a counter that MultiStream's per-block callback ticks, and the engine mixes 256 samples a block.
  - **How:** as the executable loads, the core finds the text by its bytes, not by an address, so every version that carries it is patched. Two lines the engine does not read, `PS3MixerPriority` and `PS3MultiStreamSpuPriority`, become the two keys, padded with spaces to their old lengths: the text keeps its size and every other byte. On the BLUS30443 executable, 41 bytes change, all in those two lines, and finding them takes 7.5 ms once. Not done on a savestate's load, whose memory is as it was saved. `game_text_patch.c` (C89) is a new kind of Patch Manager entry, the core's own, listed for the games' serials (BLUS30443, BLES00932, BCJS30022, BCAS20071, BLUD80018) and off by default.
  - **Patches before any boot:** the Patch Manager's choices are now written right after the Patch Manager is made. With the null and software renderers the game booted before they were written, so a change took effect one boot later.
  - **Unconfirmed:** whether the emulated audio output keeps up with a ring of 8 refilled every 2 without repeated blocks. parallel-ps4 needed an output hold for FMOD's 2 blocks; this may need the same. Only BLUS30443's executable was looked at; the other versions are patched if they carry the same text, and left as they are if not.
  - **Tests:** `test_game_text_patch` (C89) runs the edits on BLUS30443's built-in text inside other strings. It checks both lines at their old lengths, every other byte unchanged, nothing left to change on a second run, only the text that holds the anchor touched, and short or look-alike lines left alone. It was shown to fail with the fit check taken out, and with the search let past the anchor's own text. A check fails if the Patch Manager's choices are written after the game may boot.
- **The on-screen keyboard dialog's own state is off `atomic_t`, the second half of cellOskDialog.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the dialog's state (unloaded, open, abort, closed), its continuous mode, current input device, the pad, mouse and keyboard input switches, whether device events are ignored, and the input field's result are `retro_atomic_int_t`. `OskDialogBase` has `get_state()`, `set_state()` and `try_abort()`. The abort, which was an `atomic_op`, is a compare-and-swap loop that moves any state but unloaded to abort. The overlay's switch of input device is an exchange, as before.
  - **Waits:** the three words the OSK waits on while the main thread runs a call for it (the PS button's check, the confirm callback, and the overlay's input thread being attached) are `retro_atomic_int_t`. For them `System.h` gains a `libretro_wait_on` for a `retro_atomic_int_t` word, woken as the word is set and notified, or on a retro_run.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks that an abort leaves an unloaded dialog unloaded and turns an open one into abort, 2000 times against another thread closing it. It was shown to fail with an abort that ignores the unloaded state. It also checks that the new `libretro_wait_on` returns as its word is set and on a retro_run, and not before. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the OSK's files.


- **The on-screen keyboard's settings (`osk_info`) are off `atomic_t`, the first half of cellOskDialog.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** what the game sets with `cellOskDialogSet*` and the dialog reads as it loads is now `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`. That covers the separate-window and device options, layout, languages, dimmer, scale, transparency, the last dialog state and the three guest callbacks. Flags are 0 or 1, `f32`s are kept as their bits, and callbacks as their guest addresses.
  - **Packed:** the pointer's x and y are one 64-bit word, so the overlay never reads an x from one update with a y from another. The colour is two words (red and green, blue and alpha) where it was one 16-byte `atomic_t`.
  - **Not yet:** the dialog's own state and input flags (`OskDialogBase`) are the second half.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks that the colour and the pointer come back as set, and that a reset gives the defaults back. Its first run failed: the colour started at zero instead of the default 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, 1.0, which the declaration now sets. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to `osk_info`.


- **The progress dialog's text slot is one 64-bit `retro_atomic` word, where it was a 16-byte `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the slot holds a count of updates, the index of the text shown and how many dialogs are open. They are packed into one `retro_atomic_64_t` (24, 20 and 20 bits), so it is a plain 64-bit compare-and-swap, not a 16-byte one (`cmpxchg16b`). The update count is only compared between two reads, so its wrapping does no harm. Opening and closing a dialog are compare-and-swap loops where they were `atomic_op` and `fetch_op`.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` packs and unpacks the slot, opens an outer and an inner dialog and checks the inner's text is shown, then the outer's, then none, and has two threads open and close 12000 dialogs and end with none open. It was shown to fail with a close that does not take one from the count. A check fails if the 16-byte `atomic_t` comes back.


- **The RSX thread's flags and counters are off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the frame-limit switch, the count of VBLANKs retro_run asks for, the RSX's running, initialized, vsync, second-VBLANK-handler and sync-point flags, the VBLANK count, the idle time, the pending GET/PUT pair, the ZCULL queue depth and the cached display rate (kept as the bits of its `f64`) are `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`.
  - **Waits:** the VBLANK thread waits for retro_run's next VBLANK request through `wait_on_custom` and `set_raw`, so a join still wakes it. The progress dialog's wait for the renderer to start holds `is_initialized` the same way.
  - **Savestates:** the vsync flags and the VBLANK count are saved through a `bool` and a `u64`, the types they were, so the format is unchanged.
  - **Removed:** `pause_on_draw`, a debugger's pause after a draw that nothing sets any more.
  - **Tests:** these need the RSX running a game, so there is no new test of their own. `test_emu_waits` covers a named thread waiting on a `retro_atomic` word woken by a notify and by a join, which is the VBLANK thread's wait. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to these fields.


- **The emulator's own state is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** `Emulator`'s state (stopped, loading, running, paused and so on), its pause start and pause total times, its stop counter, its close-pending flag and its count of guards against a boot are `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`. The class has `state()`, `set_state()` and `state_cas()` for the state; `Pause` and `Kill`, which changed it with a `fetch_op`, now do it with a compare-and-swap loop. The guard count's "down by one unless 0" is a compare-and-swap loop too.
  - **Waits:** `WaitReady` (the RSX thread waiting for the emulator to leave ready) waits on the state word with `retro_waitaddr`; `Run` and `Kill` wake it as before. The stop counter's wake is gone, since nothing waits on it.
  - **Tests:** the changes of state need a booted game, so there is no test of the swaps themselves. `test_emu_waits` still covers a wait for the state woken by the change. A check fails if `Kill` stops moving a loading emulator to stopping (`loading` sits below `stopping` in the enum, so an ordered test would miss it); it was shown to fail on that change. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to these fields.



- **`g_libretro_events`, the frame counter every wait for a retro_run is paced by, is off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** it is a `retro_atomic_int_t`. `System.h` has four small functions for it: `libretro_event()` (count a frame and wake every waiter), `libretro_events_seen()`, `libretro_events_wait()` for a thread that is not a named thread, and `libretro_events_wait_named()`, whose wait a join also wakes. Wait lists that held it hold it through `set_raw`. The 18 files that read or waited on it use these; nothing else changed in how they wait.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` covers a frame wait woken by a frame, a named thread waiting for a frame being joined with no retro_run, and a wait list holding a `retro_atomic` word. The close-hang check now also fails if the progress dialog's thread waits with `libretro_events_wait` (which a join does not wake); it was shown to fail on that change. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the counter.


- **cellVdec is off `atomic_t`, the fifth module, and a wait list can wait on a `retro_atomic` word.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the decoder context's sequence and command ids, its abort and running flags, sequence state, AU count and PPU thread id, the picture pool's slots, and the reaper's count of closed contexts are now `retro_atomic_int_t`, `retro_atomic_64_t` and `retro_atomic_ptr_t`. The context has small member functions for what is read and changed most (`state()`, `set_state()`, `state_cas()`, `seq()`, `take_cmd_id()`, `aborting_decode()`, `au_count_try_inc()`). They are methods of the struct, not a wrapper type.
  - **The ops:** the picture slots are taken by exchange and filled by compare-and-swap as before. The bounded AU count is a compare-and-swap loop, where `try_inc` was. Each sequence-state change is a compare-and-swap as before.
  - **Waiting on a `retro_atomic` word:** `atomic_wait::list` gains `set_raw`, so a thread's wait list (`thread_ctrl::wait_on_custom`) can hold one, woken by `retro_waitaddr_notify_*`. `cellVdecClose`'s wait for the decoder's thread and the reaper's wait for a closed context use it. It is what lets the rest of the `atomic_t` series keep its waits.
  - **Tests:** `test_vdec` and `test_vdec_frames` cover the decoder and its frame queues; the thread interplay needs a game, so it has no test of its own. `test_emu_waits` has a named thread wait on a `retro_atomic_int_t` through `set_raw`, woken by `retro_waitaddr_notify_one` and by a join; with `set_raw` not naming the word it fails. A check fails if `atomic_t` comes back to the file.

- **libretro-common's rslc synced from RetroArch: no call to skip whitespace before a token when there is none.** The lexer called its whitespace and comment skipping before every token, about half of that call's cost being the call itself, though most tokens follow nothing to skip. It now calls it only when the next byte could start whitespace, a comment or a splice. On `test_shader_corpus`'s 1774 stages it saved 1.6% (five alternated runs) with 3.4% fewer instructions, and the SPIR-V is the same word for word. That is the last of today's rslc tuning: a decompiled program compiles in about 0.142 ms, against 0.181 ms this morning. What is left is spread thin, and an inline compare in symbol interning, tried next, was slower than glibc's `memcmp`.

- **libretro-common's rslc synced from RetroArch: the lexer tells what a byte is with one table load.** Each test of whether a byte starts or continues an identifier, is whitespace, or can change how a skipped line reads was a chain of compares, run on every byte of a program's source. A 256-entry class table now answers each with one load. On `test_shader_corpus`'s 1774 stages it went from 0.156 to 0.141 ms a stage, with 2.5% fewer instructions; most of the time saved is branches the compare chains mispredicted. The SPIR-V is the same word for word. A decompiled program now compiles about 22% faster than this morning (0.181 ms). The fixed and known-module tables are unchanged.

- **libretro-common's rslc synced from RetroArch: a skipped line runs straight to what can change how it reads.** The skip of an inactive branch (0405) called the lexer's whitespace skipping for every character it passed. Once a line is under way, only a newline, a comment, a splice or a string matters, so it now runs to the next of those. On `test_shader_corpus`'s 1774 stages it went from 0.162 to 0.155 ms a stage, 12% fewer instructions, and the SPIR-V is the same word for word. A decompiled program now compiles about 15% faster than this morning (0.181 ms). The fixed and known-module tables are unchanged.

- **libretro-common's rslc synced from RetroArch: an inactive `#if` branch is skipped to its next directive without being lexed.**
  - **What it was:** the preprocessor lexed every token of a branch it was not taking, interning each identifier, and dropped it. This core's decompiled fragment programs are about 25 KB of GLSL each, of which about 3 KB survive preprocessing; the snippets they pull in are mostly switched off, and the rest is mostly `#define` lines (29%) and comments (10%).
  - **Now:** read straight from the source, such a branch is skipped to the next line that begins with `#`, through the lexer's own whitespace and comment skipping, so lines and splices count as before.
  - **Measured:** on the 1774 stages of `test_shader_corpus` (SHADER_CORPUS_DIR) in one process, best of 5, it went from 0.171 to 0.165 ms a stage, and the SPIR-V is the same word for word. With the type hash before it (0404), a decompiled program compiles about 9% faster than this morning.
  - **Tests:** RetroArch's `rslc_test` gains a case of what an inactive branch can hold; it fails with the skip not taking comments. The fixed and known-module tables are unchanged, which their checks hold.

- **libretro-common's rslc synced from RetroArch: types are found by a hash, and the SPIR-V reader's context is off the stack.**
  - **Types by hash (RetroArch's "rslc: types found by a hash, not a walk of every type"):** rslc found each type it was asked for by walking every type made in the compile so far. On this core's decompiled programs that was several percent of a compile. Measured on the 1774 stages `test_shader_corpus` gathers (SHADER_CORPUS_DIR), compiled in one process best of 5: 0.181 to 0.170 ms a stage, about 6%, and the SPIR-V is the same word for word. The fixed and known-module tables (`vk_fixed_spirv.c`, `vk_known_spirv_data.c`) are unchanged, which their checks hold.
  - **The reader's context (RetroArch's "rslc: the SPIR-V reader's context off the stack"):** this came in with the sync; the core does not read SPIR-V.
  - **Where a compile's time goes now:** the preprocessor and lexer are about 60% of it and the parser about 20%; about 93% of a program's GLSL is the shared helpers the decompilers put in every program. That is what building the programs by calls instead of writing them (AGENTS.md) takes away.

- **An overlay's `visible` flag is `retro_atomic`, which finishes the overlays' move off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** each overlay's "drawn" flag (dialogs, messages, the trophy notice, the performance overlay, the cursors) is `retro_atomic_int_t`, with release stores and acquire loads in the sixteen files that set or read it. The plain `visible` of an overlay element (a message box inside a dialog, a slider's parts) is not atomic and is not touched.
  - **The on-screen keyboard:** its `set_visible` now swaps the flag in one exchange and tells the game of the change only when the old value differs, where it read and then wrote. Its parameter is renamed so it no longer hides the flag.
  - **Tests:** behaviour is the same, and the overlays need a renderer to run, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if the flag goes back to `atomic_t`.

- **`test_emu_waits` no longer fails about one run in fifteen.** Its wait of 30 frames was checked to end after exactly 30 of the test's retro_runs. The test fires a frame, yields 20000 times and fires the next, and when the waiter is not back in its wait by then, that frame is not one it saw and it takes another; it came out at 31, 33 or 40, here with the machine idle. What the wait must do is never end before its 30 frames, and the test already checks that it is still waiting after 29; it now checks no more than that. 30 runs in a row pass.

- **The overlays' input flags and thread bits are `retro_atomic`, the third module off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **The flags:** a dialog's stop, interactive, stop-pad-interception and detached flags are `retro_atomic_int_t`, with release stores and acquire loads, in the dialogs that use them (the message dialog, the on-screen keyboard, the media list).
  - **The thread bits:** the bits of the threads using a dialog are `retro_atomic_int_t` too. A bit is taken by a compare-and-swap loop (it was `fetch_op`), and given back by an atomic and with a `retro_waitaddr_notify_all`. Closing a dialog waits for the other threads' bits through `retro_waitaddr`, which is what `atomic_t` forwarded to.
  - **What stays for now:** the overlays' `visible` flag is read by several dialogs through other types, and moves in a patch of its own.
  - **Tests:** behaviour is the same, and the overlays need a renderer to run, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if any of these goes back to `atomic_t`.

- **The recording state is `retro_atomic`, the second module off `atomic_t`.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** cellRec's state, pause flag and four timestamps, the video sink's error, pause and flush flags, and the recording mode (`g_recording_mode`) are now `retro_atomic_int_t` or `retro_atomic_64_t`. The recording mode is read by cellAudio, `sys_rsxaudio` and the GL and Vulkan presenters. Every read is an acquire load cast back to its type, every write a release store, the pause total an atomic add, and the two exchanges atomic exchanges.
  - **One read fewer:** ending a pause read the pause's start twice; it is read once.
  - **Tests:** behaviour is the same; `test_video_sink` and `test_record` cover the sink, and a check fails if any of these goes back to `atomic_t`.

- **The progress counters are libretro-common's `retro_atomic`, a first module off RPCS3's `atomic_t`.** Part of replacing `atomic_t` everywhere (AGENTS.md), host-only state first. Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the counters of files and modules to compile and compiled, the files' sizes, and the cancel and stop flags are now `retro_atomic_int_t` and `retro_atomic_64_t`. So are the "overlay message only" flag and the SPU cache's two progress words. Their readers and writers in the progress dialog, the PPU and SPU compilers and `Emulator::Kill` now load, add, subtract, exchange and compare-and-swap on them directly. Overloaded `progr_load`/`progr_add`/`progr_sub` keep the many call sites short; they are functions, not a wrapper class.
  - **The wait:** the one wait on these, the PPU's wait for the progress dialog to close before it opens a game's dialog, is a `retro_waitaddr` wait, woken by the `retro_waitaddr_notify_all` the dialog makes as the count falls to none. This is the call `atomic_t` already forwarded to.
  - **What is left in the module:** the dialog's text slot is a 16-byte word changed by a 128-bit compare-and-swap, and moves with the 128-bit conversion.
  - **Tests:** behaviour is the same; `test_emu_waits`' progress dialog server reads these counters, and a check fails if any of them goes back to `atomic_t`.

- **The PS Move camera tracker waits until each worker is done, and allocates nothing a frame.** Not yet run on a game; it runs only for a game that tracks a PS Move through the camera.
  - **The wait:** each frame it hands each lit controller's contours to a worker thread, then waited for it once, with a 5 s guard. A wait on an address can return early with nothing done (`retro_waitaddr_wait` says so), and it then read the worker's result while the worker was still writing it. It now looks again until the worker is done; the 5 s stays as the guard against a worker that never answers.
  - **The list:** the controllers it tracks were gathered into a `std::vector` made every frame. They are now an array on the stack, at most one entry each.
  - **Tests:** this needs a camera image with a lit controller in it, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if the one-look wait or the vector comes back.

- **A close while a game waits in `cellGcmCallback` no longer hangs.** Not yet run on a game; found looking for more of what hung closing content (0393).
  - **Before:** the game's PPU waited for the RSX's get pointer to leave the new command buffer, or for a frame, or a change of the emulator's state, and then looked at whether it had been told to stop. A close changes the emulator's state first and tells the PPUs to stop after. A PPU woken by the first went back to waiting before the second, and the stop only wakes its own state word, which was not in the wait. The RSX had stopped and no frame comes then, so the close could hang there, as libmixer's could (0394).
  - **Now:** the PPU's state is in the wait too.
  - **Tests:** this needs a game's PPU in `cellGcmCallback` as it is closed, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if the PPU's state leaves the wait. Every other wait on a list of words in the core already has the thread's state in it, or leaves once the emulator stops.

- **The close hang (0393) has a test that closes as Windows did, and the progress dialog's server no longer spins between a close's state change and its join.**
  - **The test:** `test_emu_waits` makes the progress dialog's server as a boot makes it, then joins it as `Emulator::Kill` does, with no retro_run at all. With the server waiting for frames alone, as before 0393, the join never ends and the test fails within seconds; it hung just so on Windows.
  - **The spin:** 0393 had the server wait through `libretro_wait_frame`, which returns at once once the emulator stops, and the server does not look at what it returns. So from a close's change of state until its join reached the server, it spun on it. It now waits on the frames and its own word (`thread_ctrl::wait_on`): untimed, and the join wakes it.
  - **Tests:** the join above; a check fails if the server goes back to waiting on the frames alone.

- **A close during trophy registration no longer waits for frames that never come.** Not yet run on a game; found looking for more of what hung closing content (0393).
  - **Before:** `sceNpTrophyRegisterContext` paces its status callbacks by the game taking each one, or by six frames going by. While the game had not taken one, a close's change of state woke the wait once, and it went back to waiting for more frames. None comes once retro_run has stopped, so the close could hang there.
  - **Now:** it leaves once the emulator stops, as every dialog's wait does.
  - **Tests:** this needs a game registering its trophies as it is closed, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if the stop leaves the loop's way out.

- **libmixer's mixing loop wakes when its PPU is told to stop.** Not yet run on a game; found while looking for more of what hung closing content (0393).
  - **Before:** a block ahead of the audio port, the loop on the game's PPU thread waited for retro_run's next frame, or a change of the emulator's state, and then looked at whether its PPU had been told to stop. A close changes the emulator's state first and tells the PPUs to stop after. A loop woken by the first went back to waiting for a frame before the second came, and none comes after retro_run has stopped, so the close could hang as 0393's did.
  - **Now:** it waits on its PPU's state as well, so being told to stop wakes it.
  - **Tests:** the loop needs a game that uses libmixer's surround mixer, so there is no test of its own; `test_emu_waits` covers the same shape on a named thread, and a check fails if the PPU's state leaves the wait.

- **Closing content hung, from the progress dialog's patch earlier today ("Progress dialog, pause message, home menu close: the game's frames").** Reported on Windows with the hang's backtraces; the fix is not yet run on a game.
  - **What hung:** at a close, `Emulator::Kill`'s join thread joins every thread the emulator keeps, telling each to end through the thread's own word and waiting for it. The progress dialog's server now waited for retro_run's frames alone. retro_run has stopped by then: the frontend's thread is in `stop_emulation`, waiting for the join. So nothing woke the server and the join waited for it for ever. The backtraces show it so: the frontend's thread in `stop_emulation`, the join thread in `thread_base::join`, the server parked on the frames.
  - **Now:** a frame wait on one of the core's named threads goes through `libretro_wait_frame` (System.cpp). It wakes for a frame, a change of the emulator's state, or the thread being told to end, as the overlays' waits already did (`overlay_wait`). It returns false for the last two, and `libretro_wait_frames` stops there too.
  - **What uses it:** the progress dialog's server waits for frames through it, and its wait for the renderer to start also wakes when it is told to end. So does the pause message's thread from the same patch, which the next pause's reset of it, or a close, joins in the same way.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` joins a named thread waiting for frames with no retro_run at all, as at a close. With the frame wait alone it never ends, and the test fails within seconds instead of hanging. A check also fails if either thread goes back to waiting on the frames alone.

- **Three things the game sees now go by the game's own clock instead of the host's.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **`cellGcmGetLastFlipTime`:** this hands the game the time of the last flip, and it was the host's microseconds. Games set it against `sys_time_get_system_time`, which in this core is the guest's clock that retro_run drives (the VBLANK's time already is). The two drifted apart with every frontend pause, fast-forward or slow frame. It is now the guest's time, as `sys_time_get_system_time` gives it, a second earlier as before (now floored at zero, since the guest's clock starts near it).
  - **The AV hardware's HDMI events:** `sys_uart` timed these 20 ms apart on the host's clock. They are now 20 ms of the guest's time, and its thread waits for that much of it.
  - **A pad's replug:** when the emulated controller changes, the pad is shown to the game as unplugged for 30 ms. That was the host's time; it is now the guest's.
  - **Tests:** each needs a running game (the RSX thread, the AV thread, the pads), so there is no test of its own; a check fails if any of them goes back to the host's clock.
  - **What is left on the host's clock:** what measures the host (the RSX's load and idle counters, the save timings logged), the RSX's guesses at what costs the host GPU (how often it flushes zcull queries or reads conditional-render results back), and the overlays' animation, which goes by real time.

- **The CPU profiler thread is gone from the core.** With the PPU or SPU profiler setting on (or SPU debug), it looked at every PPU's or SPU's current address every 20 to 60 µs of the host's time and logged what it found. This is measuring logged by shipping code, sampled on the host's clock. The settings themselves stay, since the SPU recompiler still reads them for what it emits. Its thread was there in every game, parked when no setting was on. The spu.log dump at the end of a game no longer has the profiler's usage figures added to it. A check fails if the profiler comes back.

- **The RSX frame capture and its replay are gone from the core.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What they were:** the capture wrote a frame's RSX commands and memory to a `.rrc.gz` file when the user asked; the replay booted such a file in place of a game. Its thread waited 10 ms at a time and yielded on the host's clock between frames.
  - **Why they went:** in the core nothing asked for a capture (standalone RPCS3 has a hotkey for it) and nothing booted one, so both were dead code.
  - **A branch off the hot path:** the capture's check sat in the FIFO's loop, once for every RSX command the game sends, and in every draw's end. It is gone with it.
  - **Tests:** a check fails if any of it comes back. Savestates keep their own `rsx_state` serializer, which the capture shared.

- **The GDB server is gone from the core.** It was RPCS3's remote debugger stub, and its setting defaulted to `127.0.0.1:2345`, so every game the core ran opened a listening TCP socket on that port. Its thread then looked for a connection every 5 ms of the host's time for as long as the game ran. A libretro core has no use for a debugger port of its own. A thread the game's code pauses (a breakpoint) still pauses as before; nothing is told of it now. A check fails if the server comes back.

- **`np_handler.h` no longer includes `<condition_variable>`.** Nothing in the core uses one. A check fails if the include comes back.

- **Two waits on the host's clock go by retro_run's frames instead.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **The RSX FIFO's recovery:** after a dead FIFO is reset, the RSX thread waited 2 ms of the host's time before starting again. It now waits a frame.
  - **A rename the host refuses:** a rename the host refused (a file another program holds open, which Windows refuses) was tried again in a yield loop, with the host's clock timing its report. It is now tried once a frame, and reported every 60 frames. It is never called on retro_run's own thread: the game's threads and sys_fs's call it, and a stop's teardown gives up at once, as before.
  - **Tests:** neither has a test of its own. The FIFO's recovery needs a game whose FIFO dies. A refused rename needs a host that refuses one, and this one runs as root, so Linux's permission errors never refuse it. A check fails if the host's clock or the yield loop comes back.

- **The SPU LLVM compile thread no longer runs a sampling thread.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** while blocks waited to be compiled, a "profiler" thread looked at every SPU's current block every 20 µs of the host's time, behind a lock of its own (`stx::init_mutex`), so the compile thread could hand out the busiest block first.
  - **Why it went:** the compile thread hands every waiting block to the workers as soon as it comes. Between a batch's arrival and its blocks being handed out, the sampler had next to nothing to tell, and it spun on the host's clock while it ran.
  - **Now:** each time the compile thread picks from more than one waiting block, it looks at the SPUs once and counts the blocks they are running. It picks by those counts as before.
  - **Tests:** the order of compiles shows only in a game, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if the sampling thread comes back.

- **The Vulkan renderer takes one lock to submit, not two: the frontend's, when it lends one.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** every submit, and every wait for the device to go idle, took the core's own `g_submit_mutex` and then the frontend's queue lock, which the libretro Vulkan interface lends with the queue.
  - **Now:** with the frontend's lock, that is all it takes. Every one of the core's queue calls goes through `acquire_global_submit_lock`, so that lock alone keeps the core's threads apart as well as the frontend's. The core's own lock is taken only for a frontend that lends none (`vk::libretro::has_queue_lock`).
  - **Tests:** `test_vk_handoff` checks that the core's lock is free while the submit lock is held with a frontend's lock, and taken without one. It fails with the two locks taken together, as before.

- **The performance monitor thread is gone from the core.** It woke every half second on the host's clock to read the host's per-core CPU use and the process's RAM use, and logged them every 10 seconds (every half second while RAM grew). This is measuring logged by shipping code, and it measured nothing of the game's. Nothing else used it. A check fails if it comes back.

- **`libretro_core.cpp` and `libretro_video.h` no longer include `<mutex>` or `<condition_variable>`.** Nothing in them, or in the files that include `libretro_video.h`, used either. A check fails if they come back.

- **The RSX's "Driver Wake-Up Delay" core option waits in the game's time, without a spin.** Not yet run on a game; it is off by default.
  - **Before:** it slept on the host's clock in the host scheduler's quantum, then yielded, then busy-waited 100 at a time until the host's clock said the delay had passed.
  - **Now:** it waits that many microseconds of the game's time at retro_run's pace (`libretro_guest_wait_as_host`, as the hardware thread switch's cost does), cut short by a change of the RSX thread's state.
  - **Tests:** the delay is a timing hack a game shows, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if the host's clock, a yield or a spin comes back in it.

- **The RSX thread's wait for the game to set it up, and the progress dialog's wait for the renderer, no longer poll or time out on the host's clock.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** the RSX thread looked every 1 ms, until the game set the RSX up and the emulator ran. The progress dialog waited up to 5 seconds of the host's time for the renderer to finish starting.
  - **Now:** the RSX thread waits for a frame of retro_run's, a change of the emulator's state, or the RSX being set up (both places that set it up now notify). It still runs its own tasks once a frame, as it does while paused. The dialog waits up to 300 frames, woken as the renderer finishes starting.
  - **Tests:** this is the boot path, which needs a game; a check fails if the 1 ms poll or the host timeout comes back.

- **cellGem's thread is paced by retro_run's frames, not a host timer.** Not yet run on a game; it matters only to games that use the PlayStation Move.
  - **Before:** it woke on a host timeout to update the controllers' connections and calibration 10 times a second. After loading a savestate made during a video conversion, it waited up to 5 seconds of the host's time for the game to finish it.
  - **Now:** it wakes once a frame, or when the game asks, and updates when 100 ms of the game's time (`get_hw_clock_time`) has passed since the last update. The savestate wait is up to 5 seconds of the game's time, looked at once a frame. A paused frontend now pauses both.
  - **Tests:** the thread needs a game that uses the Move, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if either host timeout comes back.

- **cellVdec's decoder thread waits for room for a picture without a timeout.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** while the game's picture queue was full, it looked again every 10 ms to see whether it had been told to stop, and counted those looks to time its "waiting 5 seconds" report.
  - **Now:** it waits untimed. The game taking a picture wakes it, as before. A close wakes it, as before. A new sequence now wakes it too (`cellVdecStartSeq` notifies the queue); before, that waited on the poll. A stop wakes it through the PPU's own state, since it waits as a PPU waits (`cpu_flag::wait`).
  - **The report:** it is timed on the guest's clock (`get_hw_clock_time`), so a paused frontend does not count.
  - **Tests:** the thread loop needs a game that plays video, so there is no test of its own; `test_vdec` covers the decoders. A check fails if the wait gets a timeout again or stops waiting on the PPU's state.

- **cellRec's recorder thread waits on what it records instead of polling, and records in the guest's time.** Not yet run on a game; very few games record.
  - **Before:** the thread that feeds the encoder slept 1 µs between turns, which was a spin for as long as a recording ran. It slept 10 ms a turn while paused. It stamped what it recorded with the host's clock.
  - **Now, after a turn that took something:** it looks again at once, since that may not have been the last one.
  - **Now, after a turn that took nothing:** it waits untimed for the sink (the game's frames or audio through the renderer and cellAudio) to be handed something. With no sink (the game's own buffers), it waits for the audio thread's next period (`g_audio_periods`, notified only while there is a waiter), one block of audio, so the game's own audio is still taken block by block.
  - **Paused:** it looks once a frame.
  - **Stopping:** this tells the thread to end and wakes it from whichever wait it is in before joining.
  - **Timestamps:** cellRec's and the video provider's are now the guest's clock (`get_hw_clock_time`), in both at once since they compare against each other. A fast-forward records at the game's speed and a paused frontend leaves no gap.
  - **Tests:** the sink's own wait is covered by `test_video_sink`. The recorder loop has no test of its own, since it needs a game that records; a check fails if a sleep or the host's clock comes back.

- **A PPU's yield and hardware thread switch wait the guest's time.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **The switch delay:** this is what lv2 adds on `sys_ppu_thread_start`, and on every wake with "All Timers": 100 µs to 30 ms. It was a host-clock `wait_for_accurate`.
  - **The yield nap:** a PPU or SPU given the yield flag alone took an 80 µs nap, which was a timed wait on a word that never changed.
  - **Now:** both wait on the thread's own state, so a stop, pause or new flag cuts them short. They are bounded by the guest's time at retro_run's pace (`libretro_guest_wait_as_host`), as the lv2 waits are, so a fast-forward shortens them and the host's clock does not set them.
  - **Tests:** none of their own, since they need the lv2 scheduler running a game; a check fails if either host-clock wait comes back.

- **cellGcmCallback waits for the RSX instead of spinning.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** this is the default callback a game gets when its command buffer wraps. The PPU busy-spun on the RSX's get pointer until the RSX had left the buffer it was moving to.
  - **Now:** it counts itself in as a waiter and waits untimed on the get pointer and `g_libretro_events` together, counted as a waiting PPU. While anyone waits, the RSX thread notifies the pointer each time it moves it (`rsx::FIFO::g_get_waiters`). With nobody waiting, that costs the RSX one atomic load.
  - **AGENTS.md:** now records why the other direction stays a spin. The game moves the FIFO's put pointer and writes the RSX semaphores' labels with plain stores that nothing signals. A once-a-frame wait, a write-protected page or HLE wakes would each cost smooth play, so these stay an exception like the vm reservations, until a hardware cache-line wait has been measured.
  - **Tests:** none of its own, since it needs the RSX and a game wrapping its command buffer; a check fails if the spin comes back.

- **A blocking message dialog is waited for untimed.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** `open_msg_dialog`, which other HLE dialogs use (the NP send-message confirmation, for one), woke every millisecond to see whether the dialog had closed.
  - **Now:** it waits untimed (`libretro_wait_on`), woken by the dialog's close or by any change of the emulator's state, as the overlays' openers do. A PPU waiting there counts as waiting, so a suspend_all does not wait on it.
  - **Tests:** none of its own, since it needs the overlay renderer; a check fails if a timed wait comes back.

- **libmixer's surround mixer no longer sleeps while it is ahead.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** the mixer's PPU thread, once a block ahead of its audio port, slept a millisecond and looked again, for as long as the game played.
  - **Now:** it waits untimed for retro_run's next frame (the port takes about three blocks a frame) or a change of the emulator's state. It counts as waiting meanwhile, as a PPU waiting does, so a suspend_all does not wait on it, and it ends when the thread is stopped.
  - **Tests:** none of its own, since it needs a game that uses libmixer; a check fails if the sleep comes back.

- **Writing a savestate no longer sleeps between looks.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** the zstd writer's threads hand chunks to each other through one-chunk slots. A thread waiting for a slot to empty slept 0.5 to 2 ms a look, because an `atomic_ptr` can be waited on to fill but not to empty. This happened when handing a chunk to a compression thread, when a compression thread handed its output to the writer, and while finishing.
  - **Now:** every take from a slot bumps a counter (`m_taken`) and wakes its waiters, and each of these waits on it untimed.
  - **Tests:** `test_savestate_memory` writes and reads back a compressed state through these threads; it passes three runs in a row. A mutant that drops the writer's wake was not caught, because the waits happen only when a thread falls behind, and here none does. A check fails if a sleep comes back.

- **The RSX thread, paused, draws its overlays once a frame.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Pause:** while the emulation was paused, the RSX thread woke every 16 ms to draw the overlays. It now waits untimed for retro_run's next frame or a change of its state, whichever comes first. With the frontend paused, nothing is drawn and nothing wakes it.
  - **Frame capture:** the 30 ms sleep after a capture paused the emulation is now a wait for the next frame.
  - **Tests:** none of its own, since it needs the RSX thread running; a check fails if either timed wait comes back.

- **The progress dialog, the pause message and the home menu's close after a load go by the game's frames.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **The progress dialog's thread:** it slept 5 ms between looks while idle and 0.5 to 10 ms while showing progress, for the whole session. It now looks once a frame, waiting untimed on `g_libretro_events` (a retro_run, or a change of the emulator's state). The forced refresh of the time left comes every 1200 frames, 20 seconds at 60. The time-left estimate itself still reads the host's clock, since it is a time shown to the player.
  - **The pause message:** its native-UI refresh slept 33 ms a turn. It now refreshes every other frame.
  - **Closing the home menu after a savestate load:** this slept two seconds. It now waits 120 frames (`libretro_wait_frames`).
  - **Tests:** `libretro_wait_frames` is tested in `test_emu_waits`. These three loops have no test of their own, since they need a booted game; a check fails if any of them sleeps on the host's clock again.

- **The NetStart dialog's delay is counted in the game's frames.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Before:** `cellNetCtlNetStartDialogLoadAsync` sent its loaded and finished events from a thread that slept half a second on the host's clock first. Diva F 2nd needs that delay, because it registers its sysutil callback only after the call.
  - **Now:** the thread waits 30 of retro_run's frames, half a second at 60, through the new `libretro_wait_frames` (System.h). It is a wait on `g_libretro_events`, untimed, and ends early if the emulator stops. A paused, fast-forwarded or slowed-down game takes the dialog along with it.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` checks a wait of 30 frames is still waiting after none and after 29, and done after the 30th. It fails on a mutant that counts one frame short. A check fails if `cellNetCtl` sleeps on the host's clock again.

- **The PPU JIT's stubs take no lock.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What they are:** as each PPU object is linked, a call to a function by its address (`__0x...`) is given a small stub that jumps into the guest's code there. The stubs were a map under a shared mutex, taken by every compiling thread for every such name, with the shared jump sample under it too.
  - **Now:** the stubs are an add-only map (`lf_u64_map`) with room for four times the modules' functions. A thread that finds one takes it. A thread that makes one keeps it unless another thread kept one first, in which case it takes that one; its own few bytes are never called. The jump sample is an atomic, read once per stub. A map that is full still hands out the stub it made, just without keeping it.
  - **Tidy-up:** a savestate helper's `shared_mutex` member, there only to make its object uncopyable, is now deleted copies.
  - **Tests:** a check fails if a lock comes back in `PPUThread.cpp`. The map itself is tested (`test_lf_u64_map`). This path has no test of its own: it runs only when a game's PPU modules are compiled by LLVM.

- **Five mutexes nothing took are gone.** These were in cellWebBrowser's, cellRudp's, cellRec's and cellFs's AIO state, and the camera handler's base. Not one of them was locked anywhere. The state objects keep their copies deleted, as the object table asks. Nothing behaves differently, so there is no test of its own; a check fails if any of them comes back.

- **The Vulkan driver's pipelines are kept between boots.** Not yet run on a game, and not yet run on a driver that keeps pipelines in its cache: the software driver the tests run on keeps none.
  - **Before:** the pipeline cache was made empty at every boot and never written. Every pipeline a game had used was compiled by the driver again each session, at the first draw that needed it. This is the costly part of a shader hitch: milliseconds to tens of milliseconds, against rslc's 0.24 ms average for a stage.
  - **Now:** the cache is kept beside the game's shader cache (`shaders_cache/pipelines/vulkan/v1.95/vk_pipeline_cache.bin`). It is read once at boot and merged into the cache the compilers use, before the shader cache's pipelines are made. As the compiler is destroyed, it is written whole and swapped in (`fs::pending_file`).
  - **Another GPU's cache:** it is taken only when this GPU and driver made it, by its header's length, version, vendor, device and cache UUID, since not every driver checks this well itself. Nothing is kept when the on-disk shader cache is off.
  - **Tests:** the shader corpus test makes a pipeline, and checks the file at the end is the driver's cache data byte for byte and that the next boot takes it. It also checks a header off by one in any field, or cut short, is refused. It fails on a mutant that does not compare the device. A check fails if the renderer stops keeping the cache.

- **The Vulkan renderer's compute tasks are compiled when the core is built.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **The source:** the byte shuffles, depth-stencil gather and scatter, float conversions, texture deswizzles, tile copies, multisample resolve and unresolve, and the FSR upscaler's two passes each wrote their GLSL and compiled it to SPIR-V the first time they ran, mid-game. The source had the GPU's best work group size written into it, so it was a different text on every GPU. That size is now a specialization constant (`local_size_x_id`, and `_y_id` for the resolves), set as the pipeline is made, so the text is the same on every GPU.
  - **The modules:** they are made ahead of time and kept in the core (`rpcs3/libretro/vk_known_spirv_data.c`, 39 of them). A task finds its module by its text's SHA-256 and length and lends it to the driver from the core's memory. A text not there is compiled as before.
  - **Making the table:** the shader corpus test makes every one of these texts through the renderer's own code. `make vk-known-spirv` writes the table again; this needs a Vulkan driver, and a software one will do.
  - **Tests:** the corpus test now covers every deswizzle the texture upload can ask for: 10 variants, up from 6. It fails while the kept table is not what the tasks make now: a text missing, a module that differs, or one left over. It was tested against a mutant that never finds a text. The task's use of the table in `load_program` has no test of its own; it needs a command buffer. A check fails if a GPU's work group size is written into a compute source again.

- **The Vulkan renderer's overlay passes are compiled when the core is built.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **What changed:** the passes are the on-screen UI overlay, the attachment and stencil clears, the video-out calibration pass, and the six depth/stencil multisample resolve and unresolve passes. Each compiled its GLSL to SPIR-V the first time it drew, which could be in the middle of a game. Their 14 stages are now compiled to SPIR-V ahead of time and kept in the core (`rpcs3/libretro/vk_fixed_spirv.c`). The module goes to the driver straight from the core's memory, so there is no compile, no copy, and no source string built and replaced at run time.
  - **How it's made:** `vk_fixed_shaders.h` lists each stage: its GLSL file and the same markers the renderer filled in itself. `make vk-fixed-shaders` compiles them with the core's rslc (`rpcs3/libretro/make/vk_fixed_shaders_gen.c`, a C89 tool built for the build machine) and writes the file again. It is kept in the tree, so a cross-compile or a CMake build needs no shader compiler.
  - **Moved:** the four clear stages that were written inline are now `.glsl` files beside the others.
  - **Tests:** `make check` fails when the file in the tree is not what the shaders and rslc make now. All 14 modules are byte-identical to what the renderer compiled itself before, checked against the shader corpus test's modules. The corpus test compiles each stage's source again, holds it word for word to the kept module, and runs it on the driver. It checks every kept stage is reached through the pass that draws with it, and fails on a kept module changed by one word, as does `make check`. A check fails if a Vulkan overlay pass compiles GLSL again.

- **libretro-common: rslc takes specialization constants**, synced from RetroArch's. It now accepts `layout(constant_id)` on a global const scalar and `local_size_x_id` to `_z_id`. They are SPIR-V only and never folded to their defaults, which lets the Vulkan renderer's fixed compute shaders be compiled once and still be sized for the GPU they run on.
  - A stage without one compiles word for word as before: all 1814 modules the shader corpus test makes are byte-identical. RetroArch's `rslc_ext_test` covers the new code; nothing in the core uses it yet.

- **The guest's process-shared objects take no lock.** Not yet run on a game.
  - These are the lv2 mutexes, condition variables, semaphores, event queues and shared memory a game creates with an IPC key. They were a map under a shared mutex, looked up and added to by the game's PPU threads.
  - The table is now read in place: a lookup enters, finds and leaves. The HLE thread alone changes it (`hle_call`). It copies the table, changes the copy and publishes it, and the table replaced is retired (`retro_retire`).
  - An object being made for a key is marked so in the table. It is still made on its caller's thread, as before. Another thread asking for the same key meanwhile waits untimed for it, and counts as waiting, as a PPU waiting on the HLE thread does.
  - A key already there is found with no call to the HLE thread.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 3. Those left are `s_cpu_lock` (suspend_all, kept) and the JIT allocator's lock, which is on macOS only. A check fails if a lock comes back in `IPC.h`. `test_held_queues` has four threads ask at once for the same 64 keys, with an object that is slow to make. It checks that each object is made once and is the same to all, and that removing and re-making a key works. It fails on a mutant that ignores an object being made.

- **The LLVM JIT's null functions take no lock.** Not yet run on a game.
  - A name a compiled module calls that nothing placed gets a stand-in function that reports it if called. These were a map under a mutex, taken on every such name as each object was linked, by the PPU and SPU compiler threads at once.
  - The names are now a table published whole and never changed once published. A new name copies the table, adds itself and publishes it by compare-and-swap. The table replaced is retired (`retro_retire`) and freed once no lookup is in it. A thread that loses the race to another giving the same name a function takes that one, and its own few bytes of code are never called.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 5. A check fails if the lock comes back. `test_llvm_jit` links four modules on four threads at once, each calling one name of its own and one all four call, and checks exactly five null functions are made. It fails on a mutant that never looks a name up.

- **The file system's virtual devices and the GL shaders take no lock.** Not yet run on a game.
  - **Virtual devices:** these are the mounted ISO's file system, looked up on every file call. They were a map under a shared mutex. The table is now published whole and never changed once published. Adding or removing a device copies the table, changes the copy and publishes it by compare-and-swap. The table it replaces is retired (`retro_retire`) and freed, with any device only it held, once no lookup is in it. The device's name is looked up in place, no longer copied out of the path on every call.
  - **GL shaders:** a shader two threads could compile at once was under a mutex. The first thread to mark it compiles it, and any other waits untimed for that one.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 6. A check fails if a lock comes back in either. `test_held_queues` adds and removes three devices 20000 times while two threads look them up, and checks each lookup finds its own device or none, the add-twice and remove-missing errors, and that every device removed is let go. It fails on a mutant that never retires the old table, and on one that adds over a name already there. The GL shader needs a real GL context and has no test.

- **UPnP and the IPv6 address book take no lock.** Not yet run online.
  - **UPnP:** the gateway, the config and the port bindings were under a mutex, while each request to the gateway was already handed to the HTTP thread. Each call (enabling, adding or removing a mapping, and the removals as it goes) is now handed whole to the HTTP thread, which alone touches them.
  - **The IPv6 stand-ins:** the IPv4 addresses that stand in for IPv6 peers were a map and a vector under a mutex, and the reverse lookup read the vector without it. Each address now gets a slot of its own, claimed by a fetch-and-add and published by a flag once written. The table is made once, for all of them, and read in place. An address two threads register at once for the first time can get two stand-ins, and either one maps back to it.
  - **The IPv6 check** (resolving the RPCN host and trying ipv6.google.com) was a function-local mutex. The first thread to mark the check as running does it, and the others wait untimed for its answer.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 8. A check fails if a lock comes back in these files. `test_held_queues` registers 12000 addresses from 4 threads, a third of them shared, and checks that each maps back and that registering one again gives the same stand-in. It fails on a mutant that never looks an address up. UPnP and the IPv6 check need a gateway and a network, and have no test.

- **A thread opening a dialog waits for it untimed.** Not yet run on a game.
  - The on-screen keyboard, the message dialog, the home menu, the friends, user and trophy lists and the receive and send message dialogs each woke every millisecond to see whether the dialog had been taken up or closed. They now wait untimed (`libretro_wait_on`) on the dialog's own word, woken by it or by any change of the emulator's state, a stop among them.
