  - The receive and send message dialogs also look at `sceNpBasicAbortGui`'s flag, and the send dialog at the press asking for its confirmation. Both now wake the waiter (`libretro_event`) as they are set.
  - A check fails if a timed wait comes back anywhere in the overlays. This was tested against a mutant that puts one back. No test drives a dialog's waiting thread: it needs the renderer and the overlay input thread.

- **A dialog belongs to the renderer: its input and its news are handed over, with no lock.** Not yet run on a game; the friends list and message dialogs not yet run online.
  - **Input:** the overlay input thread called each dialog's button and key handlers itself, while the renderer drew the same dialog. So the on-screen keyboard, the home menu's tabs, the friends list and the receive and send message dialogs each held a mutex. Each press is now handed to the renderer on a lock-free stack (`user_interface::post_call`, an `mpsc_stack`). The renderer runs them, in order, just before it updates the dialog (`take_input`), so it alone touches the dialog's elements. Calls never taken up are dropped with the dialog.
  - **The game's calls and the server's news** go the same way: `cellOskDialog`'s clear, set-text, insert and close, the friend and message callbacks from RPCN, and the lists the dialogs open with.
  - **The on-screen keyboard's text** was a plain array the renderer wrote and the game's calls read. It is now published whole (`osk_text_t`, as the text served to the game already was) and read in place. A clear from the game publishes the empty text at once. The confirm check's result is turned back in the buffer already lent to it, not a second one.
  - **What a press chose** (the message picked, the friend to send to) is taken with the press, so the thread waiting on the dialog no longer reads the renderer's list.
  - **No blocking call on the renderer:** adding, removing or rejecting a friend is a round trip to the server, and is now a job on the NP thread. Before, the friends list also waited for the connection on the renderer as it redrew. It now waits once, as the dialog opens, and the renderer only looks at whether it is connected.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 14. A check fails if a lock comes back in any of these dialogs. `test_held_queues` hands calls over from two threads while a third takes them up: it fails on a mutant that runs them newest first, and on one that runs those left over as the dialog goes.

- **On-screen messages, dialog text and the home menu's icons take no lock.**
  - **Messages:** any thread queuing a message took the message overlay's mutex, and the renderer took it on every frame the messages were drawn. A message is now handed to the renderer on a lock-free stack (`mpsc_stack`). The renderer takes it up as it updates the overlay, dropping it there if the same message is already shown or queued, as before. The caller asks for a flip so it shows even when the game is not flipping.
  - **Dialog text:** the message dialog's text, its progress bars' text and the debug overlay's text were a string and a dirty flag under a `std::mutex`. They are now published whole and taken by exchange (`atomic_ptr`).
  - **The home menu's icons:** these were a map under a mutex. They are now one slot per icon, loaded once: the first to publish it by compare-and-swap wins, and a loser frees its own.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 32. A check fails if a lock comes back in any of these. `test_held_queues` drives the message overlay through its own code: a duplicate is now dropped as the renderer takes it up, and the test fails on a mutant that keeps it. The text and the icons have no test of their own. Not yet run on a game.
- **The overlays take no lock on the draw path.** The Vulkan and OpenGL presents took the overlay manager's mutex while they drew the overlays and freed the removed ones, so a dialog opened or closed on another thread could hold up a flip.
  - **The lists:** the overlays shown, and those taken off whose resources are yet to be freed, are now published whole. A change (add, remove, dispose) is made to a copy and published by compare-and-swap; it is made again from the newer lists if another change got in first. The lists it replaces go once no reader is in (`retro_retire`).
  - **The renderer** reads the lists as they were when its draw began (`display_manager::reader`) and never waits; a change is drawn from the next flip. The queue of removals deferred while the lock was taken is gone, as removing no longer waits either.
  - **The input thread** keeps its own stack of the dialogs it runs, rather than pushing the interrupted ones back on the shared queue under a mutex. Another thread's push can no longer get in between, and the order is as before.
  - **The performance overlay** is set up on the RSX thread, which draws it, so it no longer takes the manager's lock.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 38. A check fails if a lock comes back in the overlay manager or the presents. No new test: the publishing is the pattern the VFS and RPCN states use. Not yet run on a game.
- **The Windows build is whole again.** Two of the last patches broke it, unseen by the Linux build:
  - **The network thread:** its `windows_poll` call gave `::size32` a count rather than a container.
  - **The TUS manager:** its `std::mutex` lost the header that brought in `<mutex>`. The mutex was never taken, so it is gone rather than included for.
  - Both files are now built with MinGW (`x86_64-w64-mingw32-g++ -fsyntax-only`) before a patch goes out. Not yet built on Windows here.
- **The signaling handler takes no lock.**
  - **The handler's state:** its one mutex, over the peers, the queued packets and the contexts, was taken by 19 calls and by its thread's whole round. All of it now belongs to its thread, and calls are handed over (`on_thread`, on `owner_calls`, whose waker wakes the thread's timed wait).
  - **Asking RPCN for a peer's details:** `init_sig1` now does this from its caller's thread, not from the signaling thread. The NP thread may itself be waiting on the signaling thread, so the two never wait on each other.
  - **Retransmit timing:** the signaling protocol's retransmits stay on the host's clock. The peers on the other end of the network keep real time.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 45. A check fails if a lock comes back. No new test: only a game online drives it. Not yet run online.
- **The NP handler takes no lock.**
  - **The handler's state:** it had 14 mutexes, over the requests pending, the Matching2 results, the basic events and their handler, the messages selected, the players' history, the GUI's requests, notifications and result, the quick matches and the custom menu. All of it now belongs to the NP handler's thread, and each change is handed there (`on_np_thread`, on `owner_calls`).
  - **The NP handler's thread** now runs with RPCN or without: it returned at once before when RPCN was off. It wakes on a call handed over as well as on the client's packets and the emulator's state.
  - **`sceNpInit` and `sceNpTerm`:** these take turns by a compare-and-swap. One made while the other is under way returns as if that one came first.
  - **The Matching2 set-up and take-down** flip their flag by test-and-set. The `sceNp2` checks of a single flag drop their lock.
  - **The signaling callbacks:** a signaling context's extended handler and a Matching2 context's signaling callback are now one word each with their argument (`sysutil_cb_word`). The signaling thread reads them whole; it read the Matching2 one with no lock before, too.
  - **Unused mutexes gone:** those in the TUS, score and Matching2 contexts were never taken.
  - **Save states:** they keep the bytes of the basic handler, a word in place of its mutex.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 62. A check fails if a lock comes back in any of these. No new test: the hand-over is `owner_calls`', which `test_owner_calls` covers, and only a game online drives the rest. Not yet run online.
- **Socket data is sent and received in place.**
  - **Receiving:** `recvfrom` allocated a vector the size the game asked for, received into it, returned it by value, and then copied it into the game's buffer. Now it receives straight into the game's buffer: the native socket's `recvfrom`, the DNS hook, and the P2P and P2P stream queues all write there.
  - **Sending:** `sendto` copied the game's data into a vector first. Now it sends from the game's memory, which stays as it is while the game is in the call.
  - **P2P sends:** these copied the data again to put the vport header in front. Now the system gathers the header and the data into one datagram: `sendmsg` with two `iovec`s, or `WSASendTo` with two `WSABUF`s, in a new overload of `np::sendto_possibly_ipv6`.
  - **P2P stream packets coming in:** each was copied into a vector to be checked. It is now checked, with its checksum zeroed, in the port's own receive buffer.
  - **Bad buffers:** a buffer that is not all there gets `EFAULT` at the start, not partway through.
  - **No more spinning:** the P2P stream's send no longer spins on `EAGAIN`. A packet that wants an ack is sent again by the retransmitter when due; one that does not is dropped, as a datagram may be.
  - **Lock parameters gone:** the socket calls drop their `is_lock` parameter, which nothing locks any more.
  - A check fails if a vector in between, or the spin, comes back. No test drives the calls: only a game online does. Not yet run online.
- **sys_net takes no lock.** Before, each socket had its own mutex, the P2P port list and each port's vports had theirs, the threads to wake had one, and each network thread had one for its loop. That came to 61 `std::lock_guard`s and the rest.
  - **One owner:** all of it now belongs to the network thread, and it alone touches any of it. A syscall, the RPCN and signaling threads, and the P2P stream retransmitter hand what they need done over to it (`net_call`, on `owner_calls`) and wait.
  - **Waking the network thread:** handing a call over pokes its wake-up socket. To make that possible, `owner_calls` gains a waker (`owner_calls_set_waker`), called with every wake, for an owner that waits on something other than its wake word.
  - **The P2P thread:** its polling is folded into the network thread. One untimed `poll()` covers the native sockets, the P2P ports and the wake-up.
  - **A call that blocks** (`accept`, `connect`, `recvfrom`, `sendto`, `sendmsg`, `poll`, `select`) queues on the network thread, then sleeps once back on its own thread. If its wake comes first, the sleep is cancelled, as `sys_event_queue_receive` does.
  - **Closing a socket** runs between the network thread's rounds, so no copy of the socket is left in one. This replaces taking both threads' loop locks.
  - **The P2P retransmitter:** it spun on `EAGAIN` from a full send buffer. It now leaves the packet for its next due time.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 109. `test_owner_calls` gains an owner that waits on its own word, woken only by the waker: 2000 calls from two threads all run, and a mutant that never calls the waker hangs. The sys_net hand-over itself has no test of its own: only a game online drives it. A check fails if a lock comes back anywhere in sys_net. Not yet run online.
- **The network threads no longer poll on a timer.** The network thread called `poll()` with a 1 ms timeout and the P2P thread with a 1 ms one (5 ms on Windows), around and around, whenever anything was waited on. Both now wait in `poll()` untimed.
  - **The wake-up:** each thread's poll set includes a wake-up socket, from a new C89 module, `net_wake.c`. It is a UDP socket on the loopback, connected to itself.
    - It is poked when a socket is queued for polling or taken off, when a P2P port is made, and when the thread is to stop.
    - Pokes made before the thread reads them off are one datagram.
  - **Socket timeouts (`SO_RCVTIMEO`, `SO_SNDTIMEO`):** these were checked against the clock by the network thread on every 1 ms round. They are now the waiting PPU thread's own timed wait (`lv2_obj::wait_timeout`), as `poll` and `select` already were.
    - When the wait ends, the thread takes itself off the socket's queue.
    - If it was still on the queue, the call timed out.
    - If it was not, the network thread got to it first and its result stands.
  - **Windows:** with no socket to poll, `windows_poll` slept for the timeout; it no longer sleeps. A connect that fails silently, which `WSAPoll` did not report before Windows 10 2004, is now noticed at the thread's next wake-up rather than within 1 ms.
  - The locks in sys_net are not touched here.
  - `test_net_wake` checks that a poke wakes a thread blocked in `poll`, that pokes before a drain are one datagram, and that a poke after it wakes it again. It fails on a mutant that never sends, and on one that never clears. A check fails if a timed poll or sleep comes back in the network threads, or a clock check in the socket calls. Not yet run online.
- **Trophies take no lock and no clock.**
  - **The manager:** every sceNpTrophy call took its `shared_mutex`, which kept a context alive while the call looked at it. The manager and its contexts now belong to the HLE thread, and each call looks at them there (`hle_call`). Unlocking a trophy reads the clock on the PPU thread first, since the HLE thread has no guest stack.
  - **Registering a context:** this runs in three parts on the HLE thread: before the game's callback, after it, and after the sync with the RPCN server. Neither the game nor the network is waited on in any of them.
  - **Registering's pacing:** it took at least 2 seconds by the wall clock, and sent each event after a 50 ms timeout if the game had not taken it. Now each event goes once the game has taken the one before (`cellSysutilCheckCallback`). If the game has not taken it within a few `retro_run` frames, the rest go anyway. There is no minimum time.
  - **The home menu's trophy name:** this was a string under a mutex. It is now published whole (`atomic_ptr`), and the menu holds the published string rather than copying it.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 170. A check fails if a lock or the clock comes back. No new test: only a game with trophies drives it. Not yet run on a game.
- Four more places take no lock:
  - **The PPU JIT's modules:** 256 maps, each under a mutex, now belong to the HLE thread, and a module is added and removed there (`hle_call`). The entries stay in place, so a caller keeps its own entry. A removed module is still destroyed on the caller's thread.
  - **The shared memory area (`sys_mmapper_get_shared_memory_area`):** it was made once, under a mutex. Now the first caller to finish publishes it with a compare-and-swap. A caller that loses gives its own area back and uses the winner's.
  - **sys_ss's allocations:** these were an `unordered_set` under a mutex, which allocated as it grew. Now they are one bit per 64 KiB page of main memory, set when given out and cleared when given back. Only one caller can clear a bit, and nothing is allocated.
  - **The AV chat callbacks (cellSysutilAvc, cellSysutilAvc2):** each callback and its argument were two fields under a mutex. They are now one 64-bit word, set if none is there by compare-and-swap. Save states keep the format they had.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 172. A check fails if a lock comes back in any of these. No new test: each is one word or the HLE thread's hand-over, which `test_hle_service` covers, and only a game drives them. Not yet run on a game.
- Three more places take no lock:
  - **sys_usbd's event waiters:** the events already belonged to the USB thread, but the threads waiting for one were a list under a mutex. Now that list belongs to the USB thread too, and taking an event or joining the waiters is done there (`on_thread`).
    - A waiter given its event before it has gone to sleep has that sleep cancelled, as `sys_event_queue_receive` does.
    - A thread stopping asks the USB thread whether it is still waiting.
  - **The PPU linkage tables:** these are the exports and imports made as a module loads or unloads, plus the library locks and the PRX names. They took two mutexes and a third for the names. They now belong to the HLE thread, and every change to them is made there (`hle_call`). Looking an export up already took no lock.
  - **The PNG encoder:** it kept a copy of its config and resources under a mutex, to check a picture against three limits. The limits are now one 64-bit word; the resources it never read are gone.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 183. A check fails if a lock comes back in any of these. No new test: they use the USB and HLE threads' hand-over (`owner_calls`), which `test_owner_calls` and `test_hle_service` already cover, and only a game drives the paths here. Not yet run on a game.
- **The on-screen keyboard's text takes no lock.** The text cellOskDialog serves to the game was a buffer under a `shared_mutex`, taken by eight calls; three of them did not touch the text at all, and one held it while waiting for the game's force-finish callback.
  - The text now lives in a new C89 module, `osk_text.c`. Each set writes a whole text into a node from a fixed pool of eight and publishes it by swapping a pointer.
  - The node it replaces goes back to the pool once no reader is in (`retro_retire`). Getting the text copies it from the published node straight into the game's buffer, so a reader never sees half of one text and half of another.
  - A set waits for a free node only if readers are holding back every retired one, and is woken as they leave. Nothing is allocated.
  - `test_osk_text` (also under ThreadSanitizer) has two threads setting texts 100000 times while two read them. It fails on a mutant that writes into the published node. A check fails if a lock comes back in cellOskDialog. `std::lock_guard` count 192. The overlay keyboard's preview lock is not touched here. Not yet run on a game.
- Guest page faults take no lock and no clock:
  - **The areas watched for faults** (`sys_mmapper_enable_page_fault_notification`) were a vector under a mutex, searched on every fault. They are now 16 slots, one per 256 MB, since such an area starts on that bound. Enabling takes a slot by compare-and-swap, freeing the area clears it, and a fault reads its slot with no search. Save states keep the format they had.
  - **The threads waiting out a fault** were a map under a mutex. Each thread is now marked in its own word, set before the event goes so that the game's recovery finds it. A recovery takes the mark by compare-and-swap, so only one wins. Freeing an area looks at the threads' marks only while some thread is faulted.
  - **A full event queue:** the faulting thread tried again every millisecond. It now waits for the game to take an event from the queue, or for the queue to go away, the port to be disconnected or the thread to stop, as the RSX's event sends already do.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 200. A check fails if the lock or the clock comes back. No test drives it: a fault needs a guest page fault on a watched area, which only a game sets up. Not yet run on a game.
- Four more places take no lock:
  - **The VFS:** mounting and unmounting were one at a time under a mutex. Each now publishes its changed copy of the tree with a compare-and-swap; one that loses to another is made again from the newer tree. The trees replaced are kept on a lock-free stack (`mpsc_stack`) until the manager goes. Lookups already took no lock.
  - **A PRX's exports:** loading and unloading them took the module's mutex, and unloading the module took it to wait out either. The module's state, won by compare-and-swap, already lets only one thread at them; unloading the module now waits on a busy count, woken as the last thread leaves.
  - **The Gem (Move) positions:** these took a mutex for two floats. They are now one 64-bit word each, read and written whole.
  - **The audio backend's callbacks:** a `std::timed_mutex` and a `shared_mutex` guarded callbacks only the setting thread touches: the Null backend never calls them and the libretro one keeps its own, taken with no lock.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 203. `test_vfs_positional` and `test_audio_frontend` cover the VFS and the audio backend. A check fails if a lock comes back in any of these. Not yet run on a game.
- The PPU's far jumps (the branches patches and HLE redirect, taken on every call through one) take no lock. Every far jump took the jump table's `shared_mutex` as a reader, and adding or removing one (a patch applied, a module loaded or unloaded) took it as the writer. The table is now one published state: read with no lock under `retro_retire`, and replaced whole by a change (copy, change, compare-and-swap), the old one freed once no reader is in it. Its range, which every jump checks first, is a word read with no entry at all. A jump's mini-function is made before the jump is published with it. The expected TOCs checked with `ppu_debug` (`ppu_check_toc`, a reader lock on each check) are a published map the same way, and a module's are published before their checks are put in. `std::lock_guard` count 211. No CI test: the jumps need guest code and memory. A check fails if a lock comes back on the jump or the TOC check. Not yet run on a game.
- Score and TUS transactions (`sceNpScore*`, `sceNpTus*` online leaderboards and storage) take no lock and start no thread. Each transaction had a mutex and two condition variables, an asynchronous one started a `std::thread` of its own just to wait out its timeout, and the transactions in flight were a map under another mutex:
  - **Registration:** a request is now registered before it is sent, in slots claimed with compare-and-swap (`rpcn_reply_slots.c`). Before, it was registered after it was sent, so a reply faster than the registration found nothing.
  - **Finishing:** it is finished once, by the first to claim it of its reply, an abort or its timeout (`claim`, `finish`); the others are told no.
  - **Timeouts:** whoever looks after the deadline sees the timeout: the status asked, the wait, or the reply itself. A synchronous call waits on the transaction's state, woken as it finishes or at its deadline.
  - **Multi-part reads:** a requester changing the output buffers of a transaction still in flight (`get_score_data`, `tus_get_data`) has the change made on the NP handler's thread, where the reply's handler runs. No lock is needed between them.
  - **Tests:** `test_emu_waits` now has two threads claim each of 20000 transactions at the same moment and checks one wins each; it fails with the claim as a load and a store. It also checks a waiter woken by the reply, and a timeout seen by the wait and by the status. `std::lock_guard` count 215.
  - A check fails if a lock, a condition variable or a thread per request comes back. Not yet run online.
- The RPCN client is reached with no lock:
  - **The NP handler's reference to it:** this was a `std::shared_ptr` under `mutex_rpcn`, and the lock was held across the whole connection and login, so the handler's thread and every presence or trophy call waited on the network. About half the uses (every `get_rpcn()` in the requests) did not take the lock at all, against a reset on another thread. The reference is now published with no lock: a thread takes its own copy under `retro_retire`, and replacing or dropping it lets the old copy go once no reader is in it.
  - **The one client (`rpcn_client::get_instance`):** this was a `weak_ptr` under `inst_mutex`. It is now published the same way. Making a client is claimed with compare-and-swap; a thread that finds it claimed waits for the one being made, then takes that.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 242. No CI test: it needs the RPCN server. A check fails if `mutex_rpcn` or `inst_mutex` comes back. Not yet run online.
- The RPCN client's friends, messages and connection take no lock:
  - **Friends:** the friend list and its callbacks were under one `shared_mutex`, held even while the callbacks ran. They are now one published state, read by any thread with no lock and replaced whole by a change (copy, change, compare-and-swap). The old state is freed once no reader is in it (`retro_retire`, libretro-common). A callback registered with a read of the list still sees every change after it and none before. Notifications now call the callbacks after the change is published, so a callback that reads the list sees it.
  - **Messages:** these were under a `shared_mutex` (the messages, the new and active lists, and the message callbacks), and are now one published state the same way. The message counter is atomic.
  - **Connecting and logging in:** waiting for a connection or a login took a mutex the RPCN thread held for the whole attempt, then a semaphore given once. A second thread waiting at the same time could wait for good. Each is now a generation word moved by every attempt, and all its waiters are woken.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 253. No CI test: it needs the RPCN server. A check fails if one of these locks comes back. Not yet run online. The client's instance lock (`inst_mutex`) and the NP handler's locks are next.
- The RPCN client's queues take no lock. What the reader thread takes in for others was kept under four `shared_mutex`es:
  - **Notifications, replies and presence updates:** these go to the NP handler's thread, which takes each list whole. They are now pushed as nodes onto `mpsc_stack`s and taken oldest first. Presence updates still end with the last one for each user winning, as they did in the map. Taking the full presence states drops the updates so far, as before.
  - **Synchronous replies:** each goes to the one thread waiting for its id (login, friends, messages and the rest). They were a `std::map` under a lock. They are now 64 slots: the reader claims one with compare-and-swap and publishes it under the reply's id, and the waiting thread takes it the same way (`rpcn_reply_slots.c`, new, C89). The connection ending drops what no thread is waiting for any more, where the map kept it.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 275. `test_rpcn_reply_slots` (also under ThreadSanitizer, which found the slot's id read racing a new publication until it was made atomic) has one reader publish 20000 replies to four threads each waiting for its own ids. It fails if a slot is taken without matching its id. A check fails if a queue lock comes back. Not yet run online.
- The RPCN client and the NP handler's thread no longer poll. Waiting for a synchronous reply (`get_reply`: login, friends, messages, presence and the rest) and for the server's protocol version as it connects slept 5 ms at a time. The NP handler's thread, with nothing to hand on, slept 200 ms between looks ("TODO: replace with an appropriate semaphore"). The client now has an event word (`rpcn_client::m_events`), moved by every packet its reader takes in and by the connection ending or the client going away. The two waits wait on it, and the handler's thread waits on it as well as on `g_libretro_events` (the emulator's state, a client made or let go) and on being told to end. A reply or notification now reaches the game as it arrives, where it took up to 200 ms. No CI test: it needs the RPCN server. A check fails if a poll comes back in either. Not yet run online. The RPCN client's and NP handler's own locks (63 between them) are still there; they are the next patch.
- The recording encoder, the TAR extractor and the package install no longer sleep or poll:
  - **Recording encoder (`video_encoder`):** with nothing to take it slept 1 µs at a time, effectively spinning, or 10 ms while paused. It now waits on the sink's filled word, which moves with every frame or audio block handed over and with a pause, flush or stop asked (`wake_consumer`). It is also woken as its thread is told to end. A pause or stop that asked for a flush slept 1 µs or 1 ms at a time until the encoder had flushed; it now waits on the freed word, which the encoder moves as it finishes the flush or ends.
  - **TAR extraction:** the archive reader, with every buffer full, polled every 1 ms for room, and the file writer yielded in a loop for the next chunk. Each now waits on the other's count, which is notified as it moves.
  - **Package install (`install_pkg`):** it slept 5 ms at a time to log the progress while the installer ran. It now joins the installer, which waits.
  - **Tests:** `test_video_sink` (also under ThreadSanitizer) covers the sink's waits. A check fails if a sleep, a poll or a yield comes back in these three. Not yet run on a game.
  - `cellRec`'s own recording thread still wakes every microsecond, pending a decision on how it should catch up on audio blocks. The RPCN client's 5 ms polls go with its own patch.
- The renderer no longer spins while waiting for the GPU or another thread:
  - **Vulkan fences:** a wait with no time limit (`vk::wait_for_fence`, used by every command buffer's `wait()` and so by `begin()`, `wait_all()` and the resize sync) spun on `vkGetFenceStatus`. It now blocks in `vkWaitForFences`, which the driver wakes as the GPU signals. Waiting for the offloader to submit (`fence::wait_flush`) spun on its flag; it now waits on it, and the submit notifies it.
  - **Occlusion query results:** a ZCULL read that needed its result spun on `vkGetQueryPoolResults`. After the first non-waiting look (which can still answer early if a sample passed), it now asks once with `VK_QUERY_RESULT_WAIT_BIT`, so the driver blocks until the result is there.
  - **Host GPU labels:** draining the label queue (`RSXDMAWriter::drain_label_queue`) spun on the counter the GPU writes. Vulkan now waits for the submitted command buffers' fences instead. OpenGL still polls, as it has no such wait yet.
  - **Smaller spins:** an unexpected ZCULL hard sync no longer spins with `busy_wait()` before the sync that waits anyway. A draw skipped because its program is still compiling no longer yields the renderer (Vulkan and OpenGL).
  - **Thread hand-offs:** retiring the swapchain's images yielded until the frontend's readers let go; the last one out now notifies. Changing the recording sink yielded until the renderer and cellAudio let go of it; the last user now notifies. A recording producer waiting for a free slot yielded; the consumer handing one back, or the encoder failing (`video_sink::set_error`, new), now wakes it.
  - **Timed waits:** a swapchain that could not be made slept 100 ms before trying again; it now tries again at the next `retro_run`. The shader cache's progress loop slept 16 ms; it now wakes once a frontend frame, or as a worker finishes.
  - **Tests:** `test_video_sink` (also under ThreadSanitizer) now waits for a free slot through the new wake, and hangs if the hand-back does not notify. The Vulkan waits have no CI test: they need a GPU. A check fails if a spin, a yield or a sleep comes back in these waits.
  - Not yet run on a game. `vk::wait_for_event` still polls, because Vulkan events have no blocking wait.
- More HLE modules wait on events instead of yielding, polling or locking:
  - **OSK (`cellOskDialog`):** waiting for the game's force-finish and confirm callbacks spun on `std::this_thread::yield()` (and its "done" was a plain `bool` written on another thread). Both now wait on a word the callback moves, or on the emulator's state changing (`libretro_wait_on`, new, in `System.h`). The confirmed text goes to the callback from a fixed array on the waiting thread's stack, lent to it, where a `std::vector` was made and moved into the callback each time.
  - **Message dialog (`cellMsgDialog`):** a blocking open yielded in a loop until the dialog closed; it now waits on the dialog's state, which is notified as it closes or is aborted. The timed close polled the guest's clock every 10 ms; it now waits on `g_libretro_events`, which moves as the guest's time does each frame, or on a new close time being set.
  - **Cross Controller:** its thread did nothing (connecting to a Vita is a TODO) but look every millisecond, 10 ms while paused; it now waits to be told to end.
  - **NP bandwidth test (`sceNpUtil`):** this is a fake test with a fixed result. It ran a thread that slept 1 ms a hundred times, and its shutdown polled every millisecond for that thread to finish, all under a `shared_mutex`. It is now one word: the guest time it started at, plus an aborted bit. Start, status, abort and shutdown are each a compare-and-swap or a load, and the status reads "finished" after 100 ms of guest time or an abort. There is no thread, no lock and no wait.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 283. `test_emu_waits` now also checks `libretro_wait_on`: it does not return while nothing happens, and is woken both by its word and by an event. With the event left out of the wait, the test hangs. The modules themselves have no CI test, since each needs a booted game. A check fails if a yield, a poll, a lock or the test thread comes back in these modules. Not yet run on a game.
- `pad_thread`'s own loop is gone: about 330 lines that polled the pads with sleeps of 30 to 50 ms and per-handler threads sleeping `pad_sleep` between looks. The libretro core never ran it: it drives the handler from `retro_run` (`frontend_update`). What only that loop used goes with it: the PS-button press tracking and `pad::g_started`/`g_home_menu_requested`, which nothing read. A pad plugged in mid-game no longer allocates. `cellPad_NotifyStateChange` built a `std::vector` of every product of the pad's class, on each compare-and-swap try, to take its first; it now borrows that one from the table (`input::get_first_product_by_class`, new). A check fails if the loop or the vector comes back. Not yet run on a game.
- The AV UART (`sys_uart`, the firmware's link to the AV hardware) takes no lock, polls nothing and allocates nothing per call:
  - **Locks:** its eight mutexes and condition variables are gone. One send and one receive run at a time, each holding a word claimed with compare-and-swap. A second non-blocking caller is told it is busy, as the lock's try was; a blocking send waits for the other to finish. Each ring's side moves a word as it reads or writes, and the other side waits on it.
  - **Polls and sleeps:** the blocking receive and send each waited with 5 ms timeouts and looked again. The AV thread polled every 5 ms while paused (it now waits on `g_libretro_events`) and slept 100 µs before replying ("to give vsh some time"). All of these are gone. The wait for the next HDMI event's time stays: it is the AV hardware's own timer.
  - **Copies and allocations:** a receive read into a heap buffer and copied it out, and a send copied the game's data to a heap buffer first. Both now use the game's buffer in place; a receive checks the whole buffer is writable before reading. Each AV packet made its command with `std::make_shared`; the commands keep no state, so there is now one of each, made once.
  - `std::lock_guard` count 287. No CI test: the UART is the firmware's (root only). A check fails if a lock, a poll, a sleep or a buffer per call comes back. Not yet run.
- The overlays (message, save data, OSK and media list dialogs, the shader loading dialog) no longer poll or sleep. A dialog's input loop slept 1 ms between looks at the pads, or 10 ms with the emulator paused; the message dialog's refresh loops slept 16 ms; the shader loading dialog spun on `utils::pause()` for the main thread's calls. The pads only change once a `retro_run`, which now moves `g_libretro_events` after handing them over. Each loop waits on that word (`overlay_wait`), and is also woken by a dialog closing, the input stack changing, the overlay manager going away, or the emulator's state changing. The shader dialog waits on its reference count (now notified) and on the same word, for a stop that skips those calls. A dialog now reads its input once a frame, which is when it changes, and redraws once a frame. No CI test: the dialogs need a booted game. A check fails if a spin, a sleep or a poll comes back anywhere in the overlays. Not yet run on a game.
- The RSX's report timestamps (zcull, semaphore and notify reports) no longer spin. Reports in one tick of the guest's clock (12 ns) got the tick plus 2, 4 and so on; one past what fits in the tick spun on `utils::pause()` until the clock moved on. A report now gets the clock, or 2 past the last one when that is later (`rsx_timestamp.c`, new, C89). They still rise strictly, a burst runs a few nanoseconds ahead of the clock until it catches up, and nothing waits. `test_rsx_timestamp` checks a burst of 1000 in one tick and fails if the step past the last is taken out. The overlay manager, going away, spun on `utils::pause()` for its input thread to finish; it now joins it (`thread_state::finished`), which waits. A check fails if either spin comes back. Not yet run on a game.
- Four more sleeps, polls and yields are gone:
  - **`cellVdecClose`** polled every millisecond for the decoder's PPU thread to start. It now waits on `ppu_tid`, which that thread notifies as it starts, and on its own state. It also returns if the emulator stops, where before it waited forever.
  - **The video decoder reaper**, which frees closed decoders off the guest thread, slept 100 ms after each close. A context still in use was pushed back and retried after another sleep. The sleep is gone. A closed context goes to the reaper through its own node (`mpsc_stack`), so nothing is allocated per close (it was an `lf_queue` item). The reaper lets go of it, and if an HLE call still holds it, that call frees the decoder as it lets go (`~vdec_context`) rather than the reaper polling.
  - **The pad config event thread (`sys_io`)** slept 10 ms after each event, for no stated reason, and polled every 100 microseconds for `cellPad_NotifyStateChange` to succeed. That function takes no lock and always returns true now, so the sleep and the poll are both gone.
  - **`sys_ppu_thread_get_priority`** yielded between looks at its own state. A suspended thread already waits in `check_state` until it is resumed, so the yield did nothing.
  - No CI test: each needs a booted game. A check fails if a sleep, a poll or a yield comes back in these places. Not yet run on a game.
- `sys_ppu_thread_start` no longer spins or reads the host clock. Its caller yielded in a loop for up to 5 ms of wall time, and the new thread for up to 1 ms plus a 10 microsecond "head start". Both now wait on words, with no timeout:
  - **Lower or equal priority:** the caller waits until the new thread takes its entry command, or until either thread is told to stop by the scheduler. The new thread then runs no guest code until the caller has left the syscall (`start_gate_open`).
  - **Higher priority:** the caller waits until the new thread has started, then leaves it to the scheduler. If the caller is past the run slots, it is told to stop as it leaves the syscall.
  - A scheduler edge put to a thread now moves its `sched_watch` word, which is what the waiters wake on.
  - This changes behaviour in two places. A higher-priority new thread no longer holds its caller until it blocks: that wait had no end but the clock, and without one it could hang a game whose new thread spins waiting on its caller. The 10 microsecond head start is gone.
  - No CI test: it needs PPU threads in a booted game. A check fails if a clock or a yield comes back in `sys_ppu_thread_start` or the entry command. Not yet run on a game.
- A thread holding the RSX still (`rsx::eng_lock`: the FIFO's get and put set, the RSX context freed, an IO mapping) no longer spins, and neither does the renderer it holds. The asking thread spun on `utils::pause()` until the renderer said it was held; it now waits on the renderer's answer word, which the renderer moves as it holds or when it leaves for good. The held renderer spun too, serving faults between spins; it now waits on its wake word (`rsx_wake.c`), moved by the last thread letting go, the context being freed, or a fault posted, and serves faults when woken. The handshake is a C89 module (`rsx_hold.c`, new). It also closes a gap the old flag had: a thread asking just as the last one let go could see the renderer still held and go on while the renderer went back to work; the renderer now holds again for it. `test_rsx_hold` (also under ThreadSanitizer) has two threads hold a working renderer 40000 times, with faults served while held. It fails with the ask not waiting or the hold-again gap reopened, and hangs with the wake on hold or on letting go taken out. A check fails if the old pause words or a spin come back. Not yet run on a game. Still polling, as the hardware does: the RSX waiting for a guest semaphore (`nv406e`), and the FIFO waiting for the game's next put.
- The RSX offloader allocates nothing per job and spins nowhere. Copies over 3584 bytes, emulated index buffers and Vulkan queue submits were each a `new` list item (`lf_queue`); they are 32-byte packets in a ring made once (`retro_spsc`, 4096 of them), and the unused `vector_copy` path, a copy through an intermediate `std::vector`, is gone. Idle, the offloader looped on `std::this_thread::yield()`; it now waits on a wake word moved by the next job or the end (`rsx_wake.c`, new, C89: a signal with no waiter is one add and one load). The renderer waiting for it to catch up (`dma_manager::sync`) spun on `utils::pause()`; it now waits on its own wake word (`rsx::thread::wait_attending`), moved by the offloader catching up or faulting, a guest fault posted, unmapped memory to let go of and an emulator flip asked - what it answered between spins before. Another thread's `sync` waits on the processed count. A full ring has the renderer wait for the offloader, then do the job itself. `test_rsx_wake` (also under ThreadSanitizer) plays 100000 ping-pongs on two wake words; with the notify or the waiter's mark taken out, it hangs. The offloader and the renderer's waits themselves have no CI test: they need the multithreaded RSX with a GPU. A check fails if `lf_queue`, a vector, `new`, a yield or `utils::pause` comes back in the offloader. Not yet run on a game. `rsx::eng_lock`'s pause still spins.
- The RSX offloader no longer spins when it faults on memory the renderer has locked. It hands the fault to the renderer and waited, spinning on `utils::pause()`, for the renderer to clear its deadlock flag; it now waits on the queue status word (`atomic_bitmask_t::wait`, new) and the renderer wakes it as it clears the flag (`do_local_task`). No CI test: it needs the multithreaded RSX with a Vulkan device and a game that faults that way; a check fails if the spin comes back. Not yet run on a game.
- Vulkan's DMA blocks and query pool cache take no lock. Mapping a stretch of guest memory for the first time and unmapping it (`vk::map_dma`, `vk::unmap_dma`) took a mutex, though only the renderer does either - it alone enters the texture cache and invalidates ranges, guest threads handing it their faults - so the mutex is gone (looking a mapping up never took it). A query pool let go goes back to the cache from whichever thread lets it go: it is pushed on a stack with no lock (`mpsc_stack`, its link in the pool itself, nothing allocated) and the renderer takes the pools in, in the order they were let go, when it needs a new one. No CI test of its own: neither runs without a Vulkan device; a check fails if a lock comes back to either. Not yet run on a game.
- The ID manager takes no lock: `id_manager::g_mutex`, the one lock over every ID, is gone. An ID's key is one word (`id_key_slot`), given out by a compare-and-swap from free to the new ID - a fixed ID, the next past the highest (that index claimed first), or the first free one with its invalidation count moved on - so two threads making IDs at once never get the same one; the object is made with no lock held, and found only once it is in its place. Taking an object away (remove, remove_verify, withdraw, and a withdrawal that asks its object first) is done by the one thread that holds the ID's deciding mark, which a second remover, a close or a thread entering the ID waits for, woken by the answer (`id_manager::claim_deciding`); the threads entered on the ID and pinning the object are waited for before the key is let go, so its place is never given to another object while they look. The mark is let go before the pins are waited for, since a thread pinning the object may be waiting on it (`still_named`): a first version that held it hung `test_vfs_positional`'s mutex withdrawal under pinning threads. `idm::select` holds each object it visits with a counted reference instead of a reader lock. The stop that waited for the threads being made to see it waits for no ID being made (`id_manager::wait_uncreating`). The unused `idm::check` and `idm::get` with a function under the reader lock are gone. Where callers leaned on the lock: event port connect and disconnect close the port first and then look it up, as does `_sys_interrupt_thread_establish` with its tag for the whole of making its handler (its inner close went with it); a raw SPU's interrupt tag is made with the thread held by a counted reference; a PPU thread leaving the IDs at its exit does so with its ID closed; the PPU thread cleaner's last thread is an atomic pointer; `ppu_state` and the reboot's capture of memory containers look with no lock; and an lv2 memory block shared with audio is given back by whichever of the game's free and audio's last reference sees both gone, once (`lv2_memory::holds`). `test_idm` (new, linked with the core): IDs given out to the count, freed and given again with another ID; four threads making, finding and removing IDs while a fifth walks them, none given an ID another holds; two removers of one ID, one succeeds; two withdrawals asking at once, never both asking; a close keeping a withdrawal waiting. A plain store in place of the compare-and-swaps gives an ID twice and fails it; a withdrawal that does not take the mark for itself breaks it. Not yet run on a game - every lv2 object goes through this.
- No host semaphore is left in the core. The RPCN client's five `std::binary_semaphore`s - its threads waking each other (the reader, the writer, the main loop), and "connected" and "authenticated" - are wake-ups given once until taken: a word given with an exchange and waited on (`rpcn_signal.c`, new, C89); the main loop's ping timer is a bounded wait on it. With RPCS3's `semaphore<>` gone from save data and the JIT, `Utilities/sema.h` and `sema.cpp` are deleted, and CI fails if a host semaphore comes back (Vulkan's semaphores are the GPU's and stay). `test_rpcn_signal` plays ping-pong between two threads 100000 times and checks a give is taken once and a timed take runs out; a give that wakes no one leaves it hung. Not yet run online: RPCN needs a server.
- The PPU JIT's compile cores take no semaphore. The cores LLVM compiles may use at once (`llvm_threads`) were RPCS3's `semaphore<>`, which spun ten times before it waited; they are a count taken with one compare-and-swap and given back with an add (`jit_cores.c`, new, C89). A compile that finds none free - a worker about to start, or an overlay module's analysis - waits on the count until a core is given back, woken by the give; nothing spins or sleeps. A worker still starts the moment a core frees up, as before. `test_jit_cores` runs eight threads on three cores, some only trying (never more than three held); a give that wakes no one leaves it hung, and a take that ignores the count fails it. Not yet run on a game: compiles run at boot, and mid-game when a module loads.
- `test_lf_hazard` no longer fails now and then: after the swapping thread's wait for the old object's holders, it also counted a holder still there as an error - but a reader that loaded the old object before the swap may hold it just after the wait, see the slot changed and drop it unused, which is what hazard cells are for. The check that matters, that no reader ever uses a poisoned object, stays.
- Save data takes no semaphore. `cellSaveData`'s list and fixed-save calls took its `semaphore<>` with a try-lock only - a second call meanwhile is told `CELL_SAVEDATA_ERROR_BUSY`, never made to wait - so it is a word claimed with one compare-and-swap and let go as the call returns (`savedata_claim`); what the game sees is unchanged. No CI test: the calls need a booted game. A check fails if a lock or a semaphore comes back to `cellSaveData.cpp`. Not yet run on a game.
- The IDs' waits no longer spin. A thread that takes an object away from its ID waited, yielding in a loop, for the threads entered on the ID to leave and for the threads that pinned the object to stop looking; a thread entering an ID, and one asking whether its ID still names its object, waited the same way for a withdrawal's answer. Each now waits on the ID's word (`retro_waitaddr`), counted on it above the threads entered, and whoever takes a thread or a withdrawal's mark off the word wakes it (`id_manager::release`); a pinned object's withdrawer waits for the drops (`lf_hazard_await`, new), which wake it only when someone waits. A leave or a drop with nobody waiting costs what it did. `test_lf_hazard` now has its swapping thread wait this way under four readers (a drop that wakes no one leaves it hung, as the broken version showed). The ID manager's own waits have no test of their own; the core suite runs through them. Not yet run on a game.
- An RSX event the game's full handler queue cannot take no longer has its PPU sender poll every 100 microseconds. The thread is descheduled with no timeout and waits for the queue to change - an event taken from it, as before, or now the queue destroyed or the port to it disconnected, which tell its watchers to look again (`lv2_evq_poke`, new); it then sends again and finds the queue gone. `test_lv2_evq` checks that a poke wakes a watcher (and the broken poke that tells no one fails it). Not yet run on a game; the RSX thread's own sends waited this way already.
- `turn_calls` is gone from the core: `turn_calls.c`, `turn_calls.h` and their test. Nothing called it since the lv2 scheduler stopped taking a turn; CI now fails if it, or a `turn_call`, comes back (in place of the falling `TURN_CALL_MAX` count). Nothing is left in the core that queues a caller behind another's turn.
- The lv2 scheduler takes no turn: the last `turn_call` in the core is gone (TURN_CALL_MAX 1, the definition in `turn_calls.h`). Every sleep, wake, yield and priority set is one change to the run queue's snapshot (`lv2_sched.c`), put in place with a compare-and-swap, and decides who runs as the turn did: priority then arrival order, a yield to the back of its equals, threads past the run slots told to stop, and the threads let in only once every thread told to stop has stopped. The linked run queue (`next_ppu`), `g_pending` and `ack_suspend` are gone; a thread's place, whether it runs and whether it was told to stop are read from the snapshot with no lock; a thread's priority as the rest of lv2 reads it is kept in step with the snapshot's. Timeouts are a deadline in each PPU slot with a count against stale ones, and the earliest kept beside them, so a change reads the clock only when one may have run out, as before; a timeout set as the thread is woken is dropped either way, never left to fire later. A wake that finds its thread still running cuts that thread's next sleep short, as before - now kept in the snapshot with a count of the waits the thread opened (`cancel_sleep` counts them), so a wake in one wait never cuts short a later wait's sleep. The threads still to call their first sleep are in the snapshot too (an SPU thread's a flag of its own, `lv2_first_sleep`). A PPU thread leaving for good waits, at its exit, for any edge still being put to it, so none comes to it after (`lv2_sched_settle`, a wait on that word, not a sleep); a thread new in a slot starts afresh in it (`lv2_obj::sched_register`). The preemption the RSX asks for claims its period with a compare-and-swap, so one is taken a period, as before. `test_lv2_sched` also checks the cut-short sleeps (and the broken version that cuts short any later sleep fails it), a new thread in a slot, and a thread's edges settled. Not yet run on a game - this is the scheduler every PPU thread goes through, and it needs Tag 2, Demon's Souls and Tekken 5 Dark Resurrection again.
- The lv2 scheduler's run queue with no lock and no turn, ahead of the scheduler itself (`lv2_sched.c`, new, C89, unused yet: the scheduler still takes its turn). What the scheduler knows - the runnable PPU threads in order (by priority, then as they came), each thread's priority, which threads were told to give up their hardware thread and have not yet stopped, the threads still to call their first sleep - is one snapshot. A change reads it, works out the next one in a buffer of its own (256 set aside at init, nothing allocated) and puts it in place with one compare-and-swap on a word naming the buffer and a generation; a change that loses works it out again from the newer one, so changes stay one after another as under the turn. Each change decides who runs exactly as the turn did: sleeping, waking, a yield to the back of its equals, a priority set, the threads past the run slots told to stop, and the threads let in only once every thread told to stop has stopped. A thread is let in or told to stop by an edge, put to it once, in order, never two at once, by whichever change finds its next edge not yet put - so a change that finishes late never undoes a newer one. A thread is taken as stopping in a change of its own only once its stop has been put to it (or it says it saw its stop), so it can never go back to running after the threads that replace it were let in; one told to stop in its own change (a yield past the run slots) has the next thread let in before it returns, as the turn did. `test_lv2_sched` checks the order, yields, priorities, first sleeps, a change overtaken by another, an edge made while the one before it is put, and a told thread's change as its stop is put; and under ThreadSanitizer 12 threads on 3 run slots sleeping, waking each other, yielding and setting priorities with a waker thread and a checker reading every snapshot - never more than 3 on their hardware threads, no sleeping thread woken, each thread's edges alternating, every snapshot whole. Each of seven broken versions (no stop-before-start gate, puts not one at a time, a told thread taken as stopping before its stop is put, a thread queued twice, no immediate let-in after a self-told yield, told threads not counted, a change put in place without its compare-and-swap) fails it. Not yet run on a game: the scheduler is moved onto it next.
- SPU thread groups take no turn - the second half. A group's state is one word changed with compare-and-swap: starting claims it (INITIALIZED to READY) while the threads are set up, then lets it run; suspending, resuming, waiting on an event and being resumed from it are each one change; a termination - `sys_spu_thread_group_terminate`, or a thread's `sys_spu_thread_group_exit` - marks the word `terminating`, so the first decides the cause and an SPU cannot start waiting on a queue meanwhile. The threads' suspend flags follow the state: whoever changes it sets every thread's flag as the state has it and looks again until the state stood still, so the last to finish leaves them right, and threads let go are woken. The cause and status are written before the threads are told to stop, so the last to stop reads them; it puts the state back first and counts the stop after, so a terminator that reads the count and finds the state as it was waits for the right stop. A joining thread subscribes with compare-and-swap and looks at the group again, and whichever of it and the last thread to stop takes the subscription back gives the status, once. Gone: the last thread's spin waiting for the others' exit statuses (each writes its own before it counts itself out), the yield loop waiting for terminators when the stop count wraps (they wake it as they go), the 3 ms spin in `sys_spu_thread_group_terminate`, and the debugger's spin for a group's turn. Still a spin: the terminate's kick of threads that are waiting until they leave the wait. Save states keep their layout. Not covered by a test - the tests run no SPU. Twelve `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 4: what is left is the lv2 scheduler's own). Not yet run on a game.
- SPU thread groups: their threads' ports and queue bindings, the group's event ports, and the placing of threads take no turn - the first half of the group's turns. An SPU's 64 ports and the group's run, exception and system-module event ports are atomic pointers, connected with compare-and-swap (over a queue destroyed meanwhile) and disconnected the same way; connecting a port on all of a group's threads takes a port free on every one on each in turn, and gives those back when another thread took one meanwhile. A queue binding is claimed with compare-and-swap, filled, and published, so the SPU reads one whole as it binds. `sys_spu_thread_send_event` reserves the inbound mailbox when it is empty in one compare-and-swap of it. `sys_spu_thread_initialize` claims the thread's number and its place with compare-and-swap, fills the place, marks it placed and looks at the group again: whoever moves a placed thread on - the group's destroy, or the initializer that finds the group destroyed after placing - takes it down, once, so a destroy no longer waits a group's turn out. Writing or reading a thread's local store, writing its mailbox and setting its arguments take no turn either (the local store is the SPU's memory, written as it runs). Save states keep their layout. `test_vfs_positional` reserves a mailbox from four threads at once 2000 times - one has it each time (it fails with the compare-and-swap made a look and a store). The rest of the group's state - running, waiting, suspended, terminating, joined - still changes in the group's turn. Eighteen `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 16). Not yet run on a game.
- lv2 event queues take no turn and do not spin (`lv2_evq.c`, new, C89). The events are the lock-free ring (`lv2_event_ring.c`); the threads waiting for one are a bit each in two 128-bit words - a PPU thread's slot is its ID's index, an SPU thread's its group's, as one thread of a group waits at a time - each with the ticket it took as it joined. A receive that finds no event joins and then looks at the ring again; a send puts its event in the ring and then looks at the waiters, so one of the two sees the other. A send that finds waiters claims the best (by priority on a priority queue, then in the order they came), takes the oldest event out for it and gives it - in its registers, or an SPU's mailbox with its group resumed - then tells it so (its verdict) and wakes it; when a receive took the event meanwhile, the waiter is put back in its place. A waiter whose time runs out as a send claims it waits for its verdict. A destroy claims every waiter in one change and cancels each. An SPU's group is marked waiting before the SPU joins, so a send that takes it finds its group to resume; an SPU that finds an event after joining resumes its group itself. The RSX thread's drain of its event queue, which spun on the queue's turn, waits for the queue to change now - a thread joining, an event taken - and a send to a full queue waits the same way instead of sleeping 100 µs a try. Gone with the turns: the linked queues and the 25 µs of busy-waiting before a PPU receive slept. Save states keep their layout. `test_lv2_evq` covers the order, waiters in both words, time-outs, a destroy's claim, a send as a thread joins (it fails with the second look removed), and under ThreadSanitizer two senders and four receivers timing out - every event taken exactly once (it fails with the put-back removed); `test_lv2_events`, `test_held_queues` and `test_sys_io_gate` read the ring. The SPU side is not covered by a test - the tests run no SPU. Thirteen `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 34). Not yet run on a game.
- sys_rsx takes no turn, and two of its waits no longer sleep or spin. The IO map's entries were atomic already and a map or unmap holds the memory map's writer lock, so the turn around them is gone; tiles, zcull regions and display buffers are set with no turn (two threads setting the same tile at once is the game's race, as on the console). The one RSX context is claimed in steps with compare-and-swap - being made, made, being freed - so one thread frees what one made, and a context loaded from a save state can still be freed; the device's address is kept by whichever `sys_rsx_device_map` gets there first, with compare-and-swap. `sys_rsx_context_free` waited for the RSX thread to finish its task by sleeping 1 ms at a time: it waits on the thread's state now, which the RSX thread wakes when it clears it. Storing the FIFO's GET/PUT pair spun on `utils::pause` until the pair before was taken, then slept 1 ms at a time until its own was: both wait on a count the RSX thread raises and wakes as it takes each pair, or on the calling thread's own state. Left for the event queue patch: the drain of the RSX event queue and the retry of a send to a full queue. Not covered by a test of its own - the tests run no RSX context. Eight `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 47). Not yet run on a game.
- cellGcmSys's IO map takes no turn. The IO pages a map or unmap works on are marked in eight 64-bit words (`gcm_io_pages.c`, new, C89), a word's pages marked together with compare-and-swap: a range across words is marked a word at a time, and one already marked refuses it and gives back the words marked before. Marked, the pages' entries in the offset tables and IoMapTable are that map's or unmap's alone, so they are written with no turn. `cellGcmMapMainMemory`'s search for free pages passes over pages another thread marked first and goes on after them; an unmap looks again after marking that the pages are still mapped as it looked them up; `cellGcmMapLocalMemory` claims once with compare-and-swap. The RSX thread clears the entries of pages it unmapped without the turn. `test_gcm_io_pages` covers ranges across words, refusals that leave nothing behind, a mark between another's look and its mark, and under ThreadSanitizer four threads marking overlapping ranges - no page held by two (it fails with the give-back removed, and with the compare-and-swap made a plain OR). Seven `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 55). Not yet run on a game.
- A raw SPU's MFC proxy command takes no turn. The command the PPU writes a register at a time (local store address, effective address high and low, size and tag, command) and which of them were written since the last command are one 128-bit word: the command as LV2 lays it out, with the five written bits in the top of the local store address, which never needs them. Each register's write is a compare-and-swap of it, and the read of the command status that starts the command takes it and clears the written bits in one more. Save states keep their layout. `test_vfs_positional` writes the five registers from five threads at once 2000 times and finds every one kept (it fails with the compare-and-swap made a plain store). Six `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 62). Not yet run on a game.
- lv2 lightweight mutexes and conditions take no turn and do not spin. An lwmutex's signal and the threads asleep on it are one 128-bit word (`lv2_lwq.c`, new, C89): taking the signal or going to sleep, leaving on a timeout, and an unlock handing it to the best sleeper (by priority unless the lwmutex is FIFO, then in the order they came) or leaving the signal are each one compare-and-swap. A sleeper given it by an unlock2 is told so in its slot before it is out of the queue, so it returns EBUSY even when it finds it was given it as its time ran out. An lwcond's waiters are a waiter set: a signal takes the thread named or the best in one change, then wakes it or puts it to sleep on the lwmutex, telling it which (its verdict) before it wakes it; a waiter whose time runs out as a signal takes it waits for that verdict, then leaves the lwmutex's sleepers if it was put there. A broadcast takes every waiter in one change and hands them over in the order single signals would. A waiting thread joins the waiters before it gives the lwmutex up, as before. Gone with the turns: the linked queues and the 25 µs of busy-waiting before each sleep. Save states keep their layout. `test_lv2_lwq` covers the signal, the order, unlock2 (it fails with the sleeper not told), and under ThreadSanitizer six threads locking with timeouts - never two owners; `test_vfs_positional` sleeps against destroys by ID as before, through the word. Seventeen `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 68). Not yet run on a game.
- lv2 rwlocks take no turn and do not spin (`lv2_rwq.c`, new, C89). The owners and the writers waiting are one 128-bit word: a bit for each writer waiting (with the ticket it took), the readers that hold it, and the writer. Taking it to read or write, queueing to write, leaving on a timeout, the last reader handing it to the best writer waiting and a writer handing it to the next (by priority on a priority rwlock, then in the order they came) are each one compare-and-swap. The readers waiting are a waiter set of their own: in the same word a thread that left as a reader and queued again as a writer could leave it as an unlock had seen it, and be given a read it no longer waited for. A reader that queues looks at the word again after it joined and takes the lock itself when no writer holds it or waits; readers are let in - by a writer's unlock, or when the last writer waiting times out - by counting enough readers to hold writers off, taking every reader waiting off the set, then counting those taken. Gone with the turns: the linked queues and the 25 µs of busy-waiting before each sleep. Save states keep their layout. `test_lv2_rwq` covers the order, writers before readers, readers let in together and on a writer's timeout, an unlock as a reader joins (it fails with the second look removed), and under ThreadSanitizer six threads reading and writing with timeouts - never a writer with anyone else, no thread left waiting. Eight `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 85). Not yet run on a game.
- lv2 event flags take no turn and do not spin. The flag's bits stay one word; its waiters are a waiter set (`lv2_flagq.c`, new, C89), each with the bits it waits for and how. A waiting thread joins and then looks at the bits again; a set ORs its bits in and then looks at the waiters, so one of the two sees the other. A set takes the waiters it satisfies best first (by priority on a priority flag, then in the order they came): it claims one and then takes its bits, and when the bits went meanwhile it puts the waiter back in its place (`lv2_waitset_rejoin`, new) and looks again. A waiter whose time runs out while a set has it claimed is given its time-out, or the bits, by that set. A cancel takes every waiter in one change. Each waiter's result - taken, cancelled or timed out, and the pattern - is given in its slot before it is woken. A destroy is refused while anything waits or a set has a waiter claimed. Of two threads that wait on a single-waiter flag at once, the later is refused. Gone with the turns: the linked queue and the 25 µs of busy-waiting before each sleep. Save states keep their layout. `test_lv2_flagq` covers the modes, the order, single waiters, cancels and time-outs, a set that comes as a thread joins (it fails with that look removed), and under ThreadSanitizer a token passed round five threads through the flag's bits with time-outs and other threads setting and taking bits meanwhile - never lost, never held twice (it stops with the put-back removed); `test_vfs_positional` joins against destroys by ID as before. Five `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 93). Not yet run on a game.
- lv2 mutexes and condition variables take no turn and do not spin. A mutex's owner and the threads waiting for it are one 128-bit word (`lv2_ownq.c`, new, C89): a bit for each waiting thread's slot (a PPU thread's ID index; idm has room for 100, checked at build time), each with the ticket it took as it queued, and the owner's slot. Taking it, queueing for it, leaving the queue on a timeout, handing it at unlock to the best waiter (by priority for a priority mutex, then in the order they came) and handing it to a thread a condition wakes are each one compare-and-swap. A condition's waiters are a waiter set (`lv2_waitset.c`): a signal takes the best off in one change and hands it to the mutex in another - owner then, and woken, or queued for it. A thread waiting on a condition joins its waiters before it gives up the mutex, so a signal between the two hands it to the mutex it still owns, and the unlock that follows hands it back in turn; its timeout takes it off the waiters, or finds it signalled. Gone with the turns: the linked queues, the mutex's 3 to 10 rounds of busy-waiting before it queued, the 20 to 25 µs of busy-waiting before each sleep, and `sys_cond_signal_to`'s retry that spun 5 µs at a time on the host's clock for 50 µs - it yields to the target thread in the guest's scheduler now, up to eight times. Save states keep their layout. `test_lv2_ownq` covers the order, priorities, the hand-over at unlock and to a condition's thread, and under ThreadSanitizer six threads locking with timeouts - never two owners; `test_vfs_positional` locks against destroys by ID as before, through the word. Fifteen `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 98). Not yet run on a game.
- lv2 semaphores take no turn and do not spin. A semaphore's count, or the threads waiting for one, is one 128-bit word (`lv2_countq.c`, new, C89): bit 127 says which - a count in the low 64 bits, or a bit for each waiting thread's slot (a PPU thread's ID index; idm has room for 100, checked at build time) - since a count and waiters never coexist. Taking a count, joining the waiters when there is none, leaving them on a timeout and posting - which picks the best waiters, by priority or in the order they came (a ticket each), clears them and counts the rest in the same change - are each one compare-and-swap, so nothing can come between a thread finding no count and its waiting. A post wakes the threads it chose after its change; one that gives to a thread whose timeout came at the same moment cannot happen, as whichever change lands first decides. The count is 64 bits, wider than the s32 the API allows. The waiting loop's 25 µs of busy-waiting before it sleeps is gone. `test_lv2_countq` covers the order, priorities, the most as LV2 keeps it (as many more as there are waiters) and, under ThreadSanitizer, eight waiters with timeouts and two posters at once - every count posted taken, given to one waiter or still counted (shown to fail when leaving does not check whether a post chose the thread); `test_vfs_positional` makes waiters by ID against destroys as before. Four `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 113). Not yet run on a game.
- The lock-free pieces the lv2 sync objects are to be built on, ahead of the objects themselves (C89, unused yet): `lv2_waitset.c`, the threads waiting on an object as a 128-bit word with a bit for each thread slot and each waiter's ticket - joined, left on a timeout and woken (by priority, then in the order they came) with one compare-and-swap each, the bit deciding whether a thread left or was woken, so two wakers never take one thread and a thread never both leaves and is woken; and `lv2_event_ring.c`, an event queue's events in a bounded ring of sequenced cells that senders and receivers claim with compare-and-swap, full at the queue's own size, allocating nothing after it is made. `test_lv2_waitset` covers the order, the priorities and wrap-around, and under ThreadSanitizer eight threads joining and leaving or being woken by two wakers - every wait ended once - and four senders with four receivers on a ring of 127 - every event received once, each sender's in order (shown to fail when leaving does not check its bit, and when a receive does not claim its cell).
- lv2 timers take no turn. A timer's state - stopped or running, its next expiry, a count of changes - is one 128-bit word changed with compare-and-swap (`sys_timer_ctrl.c`, new, C89), by the syscalls and the timer thread alike: firing is claimed like any other change, so whoever claims it sends the event once, and a stop meanwhile wins. The period is written only while the timer is being set up, which nothing else touches. The queue a timer sends to, with the event's words, is put in place whole with one compare-and-swap (`atomic_ptr`), so a firing reads one connection or the other. `sys_timer_get_information` wakes the timer thread when it fired a timer itself, in case the thread looked meanwhile. Save states keep their layout. `test_timer_ctrl` covers one shots and periodic timers as LV2 starts them and four threads firing one periodic timer at once under ThreadSanitizer, every expiry once (shown to fail when the event is sent before the firing is claimed); `test_held_queues` runs the timer's own code from four threads as before. Seven `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 117).
- sys_config takes no turn. The service events by ID, and each listener's events, are changed by posted changes applied one thread at a time (`lf_post.h`): a service registered or unregistered posts its notice to each listener that wants it, and the thread applying that listener's changes makes the event, keeps it and sends it to the listener's queue; `sys_config_get_service_event` looks the event up in a copy of the events the applier publishes (`retro_retire`). An event let go of while the events are being changed - its destructor takes it out of them - posts that, and is applied before the applier lets go. Whether a once-only listener has had its event is one word the others read. Four `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 124).
- The Matching2 room cache (rooms, members, their attributes, passwords and signaling options, filled from the NP threads and read by the game's `sceNpMatching2Get*` calls) takes no turn. Changes are posted and applied by one thread at a time (`lf_post.h`), each taken from the guest's data when posted, so a change applied later by another thread reads nothing the guest may have freed; after each batch the applier publishes a copy of the rooms, and readers read that (`retro_retire`). Adding or removing a member still answers whether the room is cached, from the copy. Twelve `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 128). Not yet run with RPCN.
- The matching GUI's room cache (the rooms and members `sceNpMatchingGetRoomMemberListLocal` reads) takes no turn. A change, from the NP threads, is posted and applied by one thread at a time (`Utilities/lf_post.h`, new, C89): a thread that finds another applying goes on at once, and that one applies its change too before it lets go. The rooms are the applier's; after each batch it publishes a snapshot of them, and the game's threads read that (`retro_retire`), so a reader never waits on a change nor a change on a reader. `test_lf_post` runs four threads posting at once under ThreadSanitizer - every change applied once, in each thread's order, never by two threads at once - and a change posted while another thread applies, which comes back at once and is applied by that thread (shown to fail when the applier does not look again before going). Five `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 140).
- The NP DNS hook (made-up replies for the hosts in the DNS switch list) takes no turn. The sockets watched are a fixed table of 128, a slot claimed for a socket with one compare-and-swap; each has its replies on a lock-free stack, pushed by the thread that sends the query and taken oldest first by the socket's receiver. A reply is one block, its node and its bytes, where it was an entry in a growing list and a block of its own. The switch list's patterns are made into regular expressions once, at start, where each query made every one of them again. Six `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 145); `test_held_queues` runs the hook's own code as before.
- The NP libraries' memory pool (the guest memory `sceNpInit` hands over, out of which NP replies and events are given to the game) takes no turn, and no thread waits for another. It is `np_pool.c` now (C89): three bitmaps of 64-bit words over the pool's 4-byte granules - in use, first and last of each allocation. An allocation takes the lowest run that fits, as before, claiming its words with compare-and-swap; a thread that finds part of the run taken meanwhile gives back what it claimed and looks again. A free clears the allocation's marks, then its granules. The bitmaps are made at `sceNpInit`; the `std::map` of allocations, which allocated a node at each allocation, is gone. Save states keep the map they always had. `test_np_pool` covers first fit, frees, shrinks, the statistics and a save state's round trip, and `test_np_pool_tsan` four threads allocating and freeing at once under ThreadSanitizer with every granule stamped by its holder (shown to fail with a claim that does not check the run is still free). Five `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 151).
- The disc locations kept for the session (`games_config`) take no turn: only the emulator's boot registers and looks up games there, on the thread that runs `retro_run`. Three `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 156).
- The emulated USB controllers' configurations (Buzz!, the guitars and turntable, the light guns, USIO, the PS Move's) take no turn. Each is loaded as its device is set up, by one thread; a thread that comes while another loads or saves the same configuration goes on without waiting, as what it would read or write is the same file. The polls read the button table under its sequence count as before and take nothing. The lookups and setter by button the turns guarded had no caller and are gone. Seven `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 159).
- The performance meter is gone. It read the host's clock at the start and end of every SPU DMA, MFC list, PUTLLC, PUTLLUC, GETLLAR and 128-byte store, every PPU LARX and STCX, every page map, protect, lock and unmap, and every audio and video decode - whether or not "Enable Performance Report" was on - to keep statistics it logged only with that setting, and registered each thread's counters in a turn (`turn_calls`). A libretro core logs no timings (AGENTS.md); measuring belongs in a tool of its own. Gone with it: the setting and its threshold, the per-thread statistics logged as SPU and PPU threads stop, and the "took too long" warnings for 128-byte stores and GETLLAR. Where the SPU's GETLLAR and the PPU's LARX went by the meter's start time for their reservation heuristics, they read the clock themselves, once, as before. Three `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 166). The Makefile takes a header that is gone as no error, as it does a source: a tree built before still names `perf_meter.hpp` in its dependency files.
- OpenGL's shader interpreter takes no turn and does not sleep. Its three caches - vertex shaders, fragment shaders and programs, added to by the renderer and the pipeline compiler's threads - are lock-free maps that only gain entries (`lf_u64_map`): a shader two threads made at once is kept once and the other's goes, and the optimized build of an unoptimized program hangs off the one it replaces instead of replacing the map's entry. What the caches hold is kept until the renderer is destroyed, so nothing looked up is ever freed under the renderer; the `shared_ptr`s and their reference counting on every lookup are gone.
