The loading dialog at boot waited on the compiler threads by sleeping 16 ms at a time; it waits on the count of programs built now, and wakes on each (`retro_waitaddr`). Seven `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 169); CI checks the turn, the maps with a lock, the `shared_ptr`s and the sleep stay gone. Not yet run on a game with the OpenGL renderer.
- Vulkan descriptor sets take no turn: only the renderer's thread fills, binds and flushes them, so the list of sets to flush at the next submit and each set's pending writes are the renderer's alone. A set goes on that list once, however often it is bound before the submit, where it went on again at each bind; and one taken off as it is destroyed is looked for only if it is on it. Six `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 176); CI checks the turns stay gone.
- The Vulkan renderer's async transfer scheduler takes no turn: only the renderer's thread records, syncs and submits there, so nothing in it is shared. Its 256 command buffers are made at its first use instead of one at a time mid-game, in place, so the pointers it hands out to them are never moved by the list growing. Made once too: its events or semaphores, which were made again (with a second command pool) when a semaphore was asked for first. Four `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 182); CI checks the turn stays gone and no command buffer is made mid-game.
- Vulkan device memory is allocated and freed from any thread with no turn, and no thread waits for another. The blocks are taken with one compare-and-swap; a thread that finds them another's - the renderer in the middle of asking the driver for a 64 MB block, say - asks the driver for a `VkDeviceMemory` of its own instead and gives it straight back at its free, and a thread that frees while the blocks are another's leaves a bit for the allocation, which whoever takes the blocks next frees (`vk_heap_alloc_shared`, `vk_heap_free_shared`). The totals taken from each driver heap are counters, counted before the driver is asked, so the VRAM limit holds with threads asking at once. `test_vk_heap` covers both cases and a run of four threads allocating and freeing at once under ThreadSanitizer: nothing handed out twice, all of it back. Four `turn_call`s fewer (TURN_CALL_MAX 186); CI checks the turn stays gone.
- The renderer's host memory protections are applied at once, by the thread that asks for them, instead of queued to be applied later: the deferred protection queue, the turn it changed in and every flush of it are gone - before each GPU submit, each report write, each DMA copy and blit, each semaphore release, each guest fault, each memory unmap and at the end of each frame (there handed to the offloader). A guest thread that faulted no longer runs the renderer's pending protections, and the renderer no longer runs the protections queued by the thread it is waiting on. The queue grew mid-game; nothing is allocated now. Six `turn_call`s fewer. The cost is that adjacent protections are no longer merged into one call, unmeasured. The "Disable Asynchronous Memory Manager" setting, which turned the queue off, went with it. AGENTS.md: the end state is no lock of any kind, no sleep and no wall clock, with `turn_calls` on its way out too; CI counts its uses (TURN_CALL_MAX), and the count may only fall.
- The performance meter takes no lock: the threads' timing sources, added and removed as threads start and stop, and the totals the report prints change in a turn (`turn_calls`), where each took a `shared_mutex`. Three `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 303); CI checks it stays gone.
- sys_config takes no lock. The config's service events - added as each is made, looked up by `sys_config_get_service_event`, taken out as each goes - change in a turn (`turn_calls`), and a listener's events in the listener's, which makes, registers and sends a new event with the config's turn inside it; each had a `shared_mutex`. An event is never let go of in the config's turn: its destructor takes it out of the map in that turn, so the one taken out, and a listener's event whose sending failed, are let go of after it, as they were after the lock. Three `std::lock_guard`s and a reader lock fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 306); CI checks they stay gone. Not yet run in a game.
- No fullscreen/windowed distinction left in the core: the frontend presents, and a libretro core is always fullscreen. Gone: the frame's `toggle_fullscreen` (empty in both of the core's frames), the home menu's "Toggle Fullscreen" item - it set a flag nothing read - with its string and icon (no longer embedded in the core), the window-manager event list, the "Start games in fullscreen mode" and Vulkan "Exclusive Fullscreen Mode" options, and the window-system swapchain (`swapchain_WSI`, the only user of exclusive fullscreen) with the per-platform native swapchain headers, which nothing made: the core always presents through the libretro swapchain. CI checks none of it comes back.
- Big Picture mode is gone: its overlay (the game grid, the game details and its main menu), the game enumeration that filled the grid by scanning the game folders, and the ISO cache that scan kept on disk. The core never entered it - the only call that booted it was its own, to come back to it after a game it had started stopped, and the core runs only with content from the frontend - so a stop no longer looks for it either. Its option and strings go with it. Three `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 309) and some 2,000 lines; CI checks it stays gone.
- No `games.yml`. RPCS3 kept its game list there - every game it had seen and where it was - and the core read it at start, wrote it at boot whenever a game registered its disc, and scanned the games folder into it on the way into Big Picture mode. A libretro core has no game list of its own: the frontend keeps the library and hands the core its content. What the boot still needs is where a disc game's disc is, by title ID - to mount `/dev_bdvd/` again for its update or patch, for a save state of it (`retro_unserialize` boots the state again) and for an executable it launches from the disc - and that is kept in memory now, registered by the disc's boot in the session. RetroArch loads the content before a state, so states are found as before. What no longer works: booting an installed update's EBOOT straight from `dev_hdd0` as content when the disc has not been loaded in the same session (loading the disc boots an installed update itself). The games folder scan and the removal functions only the standalone list used (`AddGamesFromDir`, `AddGame`, `AddGameToYml`, `RemoveGamesFromDir`, `RemoveGames`, `RemoveGameFromYml`) are gone; CI checks games.yml stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- NP: the memory pool the NP libraries hand guest buffers out of takes no lock: setting it up, allocating, freeing and shrinking change its allocations in a turn (`turn_calls`), where each took a `shared_mutex`. Five `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 312); CI checks it stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- NP: the matching GUI's room cache takes no lock: its rooms and members change and are read in a turn (`turn_calls`), where each of five calls took a `shared_mutex`. Five `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 317); CI checks it stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- NP: the DNS hook takes no lock. The sockets it spies on and the replies it makes for them - added and removed by the game's socket calls, filled when a lookup is answered, taken by the receive - change in a turn (`turn_calls`), where each of six calls took a `shared_mutex`. Six `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 322); CI checks it stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Game enumeration (the Big Picture game grid's scan) takes no lock: the games found and the paths to look in, filled from the scanning threads, change in a turn of their own each (`turn_calls`), where each had a `shared_mutex`. Seven `std::lock_guard`s and a `std::scoped_lock` fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 328); CI checks they stay gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Emulated pads (the guitar, drums, turntable, Buzz and other controller configurations) take no lock. A player's buttons change in the player's turn (`turn_calls`), and loading and saving the configuration in the configuration's, taking each player's inside it, where each had a `shared_mutex` - the load's held by hand across the file read and let go before saving defaults when there was no file; that save is made after the load's turn now. Polls took no lock already. Six `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 335); CI checks they stay gone. Not yet run in a game.
- The game list (`games.yml`) takes no lock: looking a game up, adding or removing one and saving and loading the file are done in a turn (`turn_calls`), where each took a `shared_mutex`. Six `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 341); CI checks it stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- NP: the matching room cache takes no lock. Its rooms - filled from RPCN's replies and notifications, read by the game's matching calls - change and are read in a turn (`turn_calls`), where each of eleven calls took a `shared_mutex`; `update_opt_param` took nothing and now changes them in the turn too. On the way: `del_member` erased the room whose ID was the member's instead of the member from its room; it takes the member out now. Eleven `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 347); CI checks the cache keeps no lock. Not yet run in a game.
- Network: the P2P port's RPCN and signaling messages take no lock. The P2P thread queued each message it received for RPCN or for signaling in a vector behind a `shared_mutex`, and the RPCN and signaling threads took the vector under it; each message now goes on a stack (`mpsc_stack`) with one compare-and-swap, and a reader takes the whole stack with one exchange, in the order the messages came. Four `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 358); CI checks they stay gone. Not yet run in a game.
- cellGcmSys takes no lock. Its IO mapping took `gcmio_mutex` around each map and unmap and the syscall each makes, so that a search for free IO pages and the mapping of them, and a syscall and the offset tables written after it, went together. Now the tables change in a turn (`turn_calls`), and the IO pages a map or unmap is working on while it makes its syscall - on its own thread, out of the turn - are marked there: a search for free pages passes them over, so two `cellGcmMapMainMemory` calls at once never get the same pages, and another map or unmap of one of them meanwhile is refused with `CELL_GCM_ERROR_FAILURE` rather than waited out (two threads mapping the same pages at once is the game's race; libgcm is not thread-safe there either). Reserving and unreserving IO space are a compare-and-swap of the reserved size; local memory is claimed in a turn and allocated out of it; binding a zcull region and `cellGcmInit` take nothing, sys_rsx changing what they change in its own turn. The RSX's unmap notification, which cleared the offset table entries of guest memory being unmapped under the same lock, clears them in the same turn. Eleven `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 362); CI checks the lock stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Raw SPUs: run control takes no lock. A stop request held `run_ctrl_mtx` while it waited for the SPU to stop, and every run request took it shared, so a run request waited out any stop in progress - the lock was what kept a run request from starting the SPU again under the stop's wait, which watched the running bit and could have slept on through it. The SPU now counts each time it stops (`stop_count`, with a wake), and a stop request waits for that count to move from what it read before its look: a run request after the stop no longer matters to it, and starts the SPU with its one compare-and-swap of the status, as it did under the lock. A `std::scoped_lock` and a reader lock fewer; CI checks the lock stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Raw SPUs: the MFC proxy command takes no lock. Its registers, written one at a time by the PPU, and the status read that takes the command once all are written change in a turn (`turn_calls`), where `mfc_prxy_mtx` was taken for each. Five `std::lock_guard`s and a `std::scoped_lock` fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 373); CI checks the lock stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- OpenGL: the shader interpreter's caches take no lock. Its vertex and fragment shader caches and its program cache - filled from the renderer and the pipeline compiler's threads - change in a turn (`turn_calls`), where each had a `shared_mutex`; the GL calls those locks were held around - deleting a shader another thread had already cached, syncing and setting up a program - are made out of the turn, on the thread whose context they are. A 16 ms sleep after the interpreter's precompile, there to avoid a visual flash, is gone; the precompile's wait for its compiles is still a polling loop. Four `std::lock_guard`s and three reader locks fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 378); CI checks the caches keep no lock. Not yet run in a game.
- sys_rsx takes no lock. What its syscalls change of the RSX - the IO map, tiles, zcull regions, display buffers, the device's address - changes in a turn (`turn_calls`), where every one of them took `sys_rsx_mtx`, a `shared_mutex`. Setting the FIFO's get and put already went in one compare-and-swap, a second set waiting for the first to be taken, so it takes nothing more. Making and freeing the context are the calling thread's (its event queue and port, the guest's stack variables): the one context is claimed with compare-and-swap, so a second make still fails as before, and the freeing thread takes the context's address in a turn. A tile set on main memory takes the memory map's writer lock before its turn, as `sys_rsx_context_iomap` does - it took it inside the RSX lock, the other way round - and tells the renderer of an unbound tile after it. The device's memory is mapped out of the turn and kept by whichever thread gets there first; one that comes second gives its mapping back. Five `std::lock_guard`s and four `std::scoped_lock`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 382); CI checks the lock stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Vulkan: a texture upload allocates nothing once warm. Each upload made three lists - its buffer-to-image copies, its buffer copies and the runs of copies from each source buffer - and freed them at its end; they are the uploading thread's now, emptied at each upload and kept, so they stop growing once they hold what uploads need. Not yet run in a game.
- The core builds with no warnings from its own code. `RETRO_HW_FRAME_BUFFER_VALID` is passed as a constant made with a C++ cast, where libretro.h's C cast warned at each frame handed over (libretro.h stays as RetroArch has it); an unused frame counter, an unused flag in the OpenGL present and a duplicate `rpng_alloc` declaration in libretro-common (fixed in RetroArch and synced) are gone. Two things the warnings found mattered: OpenGL's texture creation called `glGetError` - a round trip to the driver - after each of up to eleven steps of every texture it made, for a log line that is compiled out; it is made only in builds with the texture trace on now. And `SBCSstoUTF8s` read eight bytes out of the four-byte buffer a multibyte character was converted into; it reads the four. The timer thread's and USB thread's hand-over nodes are the bases of what they hand over, so a node goes back to its owner with a `static_cast` instead of a type-punned pointer, and the Vulkan feature merge copies the features out and back instead of reading them through a `VkBool32` pointer. The four 64-bit atomic waits left - an SPU thread waiting for another's compile of the same block, the savestate HLE locks, the compressed savestate writer waiting for pending memory to go down - wait on 32-bit words now: a ready flag published with each compiled block (also after a compile whose trampoline could not be rebuilt, which woke nobody before), the HLE lock count as the 32-bit value it always was, and a wake count bumped with each wake of the writer. Not yet run in a game.
- OpenGL: the samplers take no lock, as Vulkan's do. A tile unbound by a guest thread took the renderer's sampler lock to set the flag that has every sampler looked at again, and so waited for whatever texture load the renderer was in the middle of, which held the lock throughout. It now sets a flag of its own that the renderer takes at its next load (`request_samplers_reload`), before its look, so one set during a load counts for the next; on the renderer's own thread - an invalidated range, a fault it serves - the reload is asked for at once. The renderer's load no longer takes the lock either. Four `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 387); CI checks it stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- RSX: ZCULL's report pages take no lock. The pages it protects while reports to them are in flight were a map behind a `shared_mutex`, taken at every report queued and completed, every read barrier and every fault: another thread's fault on such a page was dealt with on that thread, which took the lock and then paused the renderer (`eng_lock`) to turn the optimizations off. Now such a fault is handed to the renderer, as a fault on the texture cache's pages is (the guest fault queue): the faulting thread asks `may_own` - the count of locked pages, read with no lock as before - and the renderer deals with it as with one of its own, so the map is the renderer's alone and the pause is gone. A fault from the offloader on such a page goes with its deadlock handover, where the renderer now hands ZCULL the address too. Six `std::scoped_lock`s fewer; CI checks ZCULL keeps no lock. Not yet run in a game.
- Vulkan: the renderer's flush queue and secondary command buffers take no lock. Since faults and unmaps from other threads are handed to the renderer (the guest fault queue, the invalidated range), only the renderer flushes for a request or a sync, invalidates a range into the secondary command buffers or evicts textures when VRAM runs out - yet each took a `shared_mutex`: the flush queue's at every zcull sync hint and occlusion result read, and at every look at a flush request, the secondary command buffers' at every invalidated range and eviction. The flush queue's `try_lock` kept a local task met inside such a flush from flushing again; a flag of the renderer's own does that now. A flush request had a count of the threads waiting on it, which the renderer spun on after each flush, and a spin for the poster: nobody but the renderer posts one now, so the count and both spins are gone. Four `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 391); CI checks the locks and the spins stay gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Vulkan: volk is gone. The Vulkan functions the core calls are its own list, in C89 (`rpcs3/Emu/RSX/VK/vk_loader.c`): 130 pointers named as the functions, where volk declared and loaded some 700 for every extension there is. They are taken from the frontend's `vkGetInstanceProcAddr`, or from the system's loader opened with libretro-common's `dylib` (`vulkan-1.dll`, `libvulkan.so.1`, MoltenVK; `dynamic/dylib.c` and its header, synced from RetroArch, as they are there), and through the instance once it is made or adopted, as volk did it. The new test_vk_loader (C89) opens the system's loader, makes an instance and checks every function on the list is there; taken without the instance they are not, and it fails. The renderer's own pass over the shader corpus, on the instance and device the core makes, runs on it too. `3rdparty/volk` is removed from the tree and from both builds; CI checks it stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Vulkan: the Vulkan Memory Allocator is gone. Device memory comes from the renderer's own heap, in C89 (`vkutils/vk_heap.c`), as parallel-ps2's and parallel-xbox's do: blocks of 64 MB (an eighth of a smaller driver heap, such as a 256 MB BAR window) taken from the driver and handed out as offsets, the lowest free span that fits; a free finds its place by binary search and merges with its neighbours. Buffers and linear images never share a block with optimal images, so `bufferImageGranularity` is kept without padding what VMA had to treat as of unknown kind. A host-visible block is mapped once when it is taken and stays mapped: `map` is an address and `unmap` nothing, where each was a VMA call. A block's span array is made with it, at a fixed size, and an allocation that could outgrow it goes to another block, so nothing is allocated on the host but when the driver is asked for a block. Over half a block is a block of its own, given back at its free; one empty block of each type and kind is kept for what comes next, the others go back, and when the driver or the VRAM limit has nothing left the empty blocks go back and it is asked again. With "Disable Vulkan Memory Allocator" every allocation is a block of its own, as it was. Allocations and frees are made in the heap's turn (`turn_calls`), where VMA took its `shared_mutex`; the eviction that makes room when memory runs out is done out of it. The load the renderer looks at is what is taken from each driver heap against four fifths of it, as VMA reckoned it without the memory budget extension. The `mem_allocator` class hierarchy and its virtual calls are one class now, and the allocation-strategy switching that only VMA used is gone with it. The new test_vk_heap (C89, on a driver of its own) runs 200,000 random allocations and frees of every size, alignment, memory type and kind and checks that nothing overlaps, alignment, addresses and accounting hold, the kinds keep apart, double frees are refused, the VRAM limit holds and empty blocks give way to it, the span arrays hold, and a warm heap asks the driver for nothing; without the merge on free, the double-free check, the span bound or the kinds apart it fails. VMA's submodule, `VKMemAlloc.cpp` and the CMake option for a system VMA are removed; CI checks VMA stays gone. Not yet run in a game.
- Vulkan descriptor sets take no lock. The list of sets whose writes wait for the next submit changes in its turn (`turn_calls`), where a `std::mutex` was taken at every bind that deferred its writes, every submit that flushed them and every set destroyed; and a set's pending writes are flushed, and a pipeline's descriptor template filled into them, in the set's own turn, where its `shared_mutex` was taken at each draw. The list's turn takes a set's inside it as it flushes, never the other way. Six `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 395); CI checks both stay lock-free. No test runs them: they need a Vulkan device.
- Vulkan memory accounting takes no lock and allocates nothing. Every allocation and free of device memory - each buffer, image and texture the renderer makes or drops, from the renderer and the texture upload threads alike - took a mutex to add or look up an entry in a table of every allocation, which grew a node at each allocation, and to add to the totals, which were maps behind the same mutex. An allocation's type, size and pool are now kept by its `memory_block` and given back at its free, and the totals per memory type and per pool are atomic counters. The warning when a type nears its budget and the leak report at teardown stay, the report now by pool. test_held_queues allocates and frees from four threads at once and checks the totals come back to where they were; with the counters updated out of one atomic step they do not. Three `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 401); CI checks the table and its lock stay gone.
- RSX host memory manager: the queue of deferred memory protections takes no lock. Queueing a protection, flushing all or part of the queue (for an address, ranges, a tag) and asking the offloader to flush it are done in turn (`turn_calls`), where `g_mprotect_queue_lock` was taken for each - at every `mm_flush` the RSX makes. What is done in a turn, protecting host memory and posting to the offloader, is for any thread to do. Six `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 404); CI checks the queue keeps no lock.
- RSX: the invalidated memory range takes no lock. It is one word, its start and end packed into the two halves: an unmap merges its range into it with compare-and-swap (or has the renderer handle a range it does not touch first, as before), and the renderer handles the range waiting and takes it off with compare-and-swap - handling it again when an unmap merged more in meanwhile. The range stays in the word while it is handled, so a map over it still waits for the renderer. Where `m_mtx_task` was taken by every unmap, every map that found a range waiting, and the renderer at each look; the flag saying a range waits is the word itself. Five `std::lock_guard`s and a `unique_lock` fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 410); CI checks the lock stays gone.
- Vulkan: the samplers take no lock. A thread that wanted every sampler looked at again - a guest thread's write over a texture, an unmap, a tile unbind - took the renderer's sampler lock to set the flag, and so waited for whatever texture load the renderer was in the middle of; it now sets the flag the renderer takes at its next load (`request_samplers_reload`), at once on the renderer's own thread, where a draw retrying after running out of memory looks for it. The renderer's load no longer takes the lock either. CI checks it stays gone.
- Vulkan: the asynchronous texture streaming scheduler takes no lock. Choosing its command buffer, inserting the sync label and submitting are done in turn (`turn_calls`), where a `shared_mutex` was taken for each - the one upstream's comment calls "inexplicably expensive" on the label among them - and its options are set in a turn of their own. CI checks it stays lock-free.
- SPU thread groups take no lock: what a group is - its threads, their ports and bound queues, its run, join and exit state as they change with them - changes in the group's turn (`turn_calls`), where its `shared_mutex` was taken by the group and thread syscalls (initialize, start, suspend, resume, terminate, join, the local store reads and writes, the mailbox, connecting and binding events and queues), the SPU's own stop, exit, send and receive paths, the wake of a group waiting on an event queue, and the CPU dump. The SPU receive paths take the group's turn inside the event queue's, as the two locks were taken; the group's turn takes the scheduler's (`lv2_obj::awake`) inside it, never the other way. Going to sleep is the joining thread's own and is done after its turn: a group that exits before the joiner has gone to sleep cancels the sleep, its status in r4-r5 already. The waits for the threads to stop, the joiner's and terminator's sleeps and a thread made but not placed being removed again are all the calling thread's, out of the turn; what a syscall reads from the guest's memory is read on the calling thread before it. The CPU dump takes the group's turn only once it has seen it free, as it took the lock only with a try. Twenty `std::lock_guard`s fewer, with the `unique_lock`s, `scoped_lock`s and `reader_lock`s on the group (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 423); CI checks the groups keep no lock.
- The lv2 scheduler takes no lock: its queues of running and sleeping threads change in the scheduler's turn (`turn_calls`, `lv2_obj::g_turns`), where `lv2_obj::g_mutex` was taken by every `lv2_obj::sleep`, every `lv2_obj::awake` and every `ppu_state` look - each wait, wake and yield of every PPU thread, and every wake from an SPU or the timer thread. A turn may run on another thread than the one whose scheduling it does, so what the scheduling asks of that thread - which thread it is, its list of threads to wake and its notification slots - is handed to the turn (`lv2_obj::g_caller`) and read there in place of the running thread's own. The scheduler's turn is taken inside the sync objects' turns and never takes one itself. Two `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 443); CI checks the scheduler keeps no lock.
- Lightweight mutexes and condition variables take no lock. An lwmutex's signal and queue were already one 128-bit word; what is not one compare-and-swap - giving it to a waiter on unlock, taking a timed-out waiter out of the queue, a destroy - now changes in the lwmutex's turn (`turn_calls`), and an lwcond's queue in the lwcond's turn, with the lwmutex's turn taken inside it where both change (a signal that hands the lwmutex over, a wait that unlocks it, a timeout, a stop). An lwmutex's turn never takes an lwcond's, so the two cannot wait on each other. Going to sleep, and the de-schedule a destroy does while waiters leave, are the thread's own and done after its turn; a waiter woken before it has gone to sleep cancels the sleep. The lwmutex's next owner, when an lwcond wait unlocks it, is still woken on the waiting thread together with its sleep. Thirteen `std::lock_guard`s and a `std::scoped_lock` fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 445); CI checks both keep no lock.
- Event queues take no lock: a queue's events and waiters change in the queue's turn (`turn_calls`), where its `shared_mutex` was taken by every send (from PPU and SPU threads, event ports, the timer thread, RSX), every receive, try-receive, drain, timeout, stop and destroy, the SPU receive paths and the RSX queue drain. An SPU group's lock is still taken inside the queue's turn where the SPU paths need both, never the other way, as the two locks were. What a send asked of the thread it ran on (the current thread, for an abort and for the priority of the thread woken) is read before its turn. Going to sleep is the receiving thread's own and is done after its turn: an event sent, or a destroy that cancels it, before it has gone to sleep cancels the sleep, and the receive returns what it was given. A destroy withdraws the queue in its turn and wakes the waiters it had in a second one: once withdrawn and no longer existing, nothing can join them. The RSX drain's wait between its looks is cut short by the queue's turn being taken, where it watched the lock's word; that drain is still a polling loop on the host clock, to be replaced. test_lv2_events sends from two threads and takes from two others at once: every event comes out exactly once and in each sender's order; with the send out of its turn a third of them go missing and some come out twice. Seven `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 458); CI checks the queues keep no lock.
- Reader-writer locks take no lock: their reader and writer queues, and the owner word as it changes with them, change in the rwlock's turn (`turn_calls`), where a `shared_mutex` was taken by every reader or writer that had to wait, every unlock that had waiters to wake, every timeout and every stop. Taking it free and giving a read up while nobody waits stay a compare-and-swap of the owner word. Going to sleep is the waiting thread's own and is done after its turn, as in `sys_mutex_lock`: a thread given the lock before it has gone to sleep cancels the sleep and returns with it. Eight `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 465); CI checks the rwlocks keep no lock.
- Mutexes and condition variables take no lock. A mutex's owner and queue were already one 128-bit word, taken free and queued on with compare-and-swap; what is not one compare-and-swap - giving the mutex to a waiter on unlock, taking a timed-out waiter out of the queue, a destroy - and every condition's queue, which a signal moves waiters from onto the mutex's, now change in the mutex's turn (`turn_calls`), where its `shared_mutex` was taken. Going to sleep is the waiting thread's own and is done after its turn, in `sys_cond_wait` as in `sys_mutex_lock`: a thread given the mutex before it has gone to sleep cancels the sleep and returns as the owner. The mutex's next owner, when a wait unlocks it, is still woken on the waiting thread together with its sleep. Eleven `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 473); CI checks both keep no lock.
- Timers take no lock: a timer's expiry, start, stop, information and event-queue port change in the timer's turn (`turn_calls`), where a `shared_mutex` was taken by the timer thread at every expiry and by every start, stop, get_information, connect, disconnect and destroy. The state, expiry and period stay words of their own. test_held_queues checks a periodic timer's expiry from four threads at once, as the timer thread and PPU threads do: every period sends one event, in order, none twice; with the check out of the turn nearly every round sends some twice. Six `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 484); CI checks the timers keep no lock.
- Event flags take no lock: their waiters' queue changes in the flag's turn (`turn_calls`), as the semaphore's does, where `shared_mutex` was taken by every wait that had to queue, every set that found waiters, every cancel and every timeout. The pattern stays a word of its own, taken and changed with compare-and-swap. Going to sleep is the waiting thread's own and is done after its turn: a set or a cancel that wakes it before it has gone to sleep cancels the sleep, and the wait returns what the waker gave it. A timed-out wait leaves the queue in the flag's turn, which also waits out a set or cancel that is waking it. The ID test's event-flag half queues its threads in the flag's turn. Five `std::lock_guard`s fewer (LOCK_GUARD_MAX 490); CI checks the event flags keep no lock.
- rpcs3/Crypto's ECDSA (`ec.cpp`, the PS3's own 160-bit curve) is gone, with `check_data()`, the EDAT signature check that was its only user, and the VSH curve and key constants. Nothing called `check_data()`: its call was commented out because the check, on a big-number code working a byte at a time, made EDAT loads some twenty times slower, and it only ever logged a warning. CI checks it stays gone.
- rpcs3/Crypto's AES (PolarSSL, with its own AES-NI code) is gone; every caller takes libretro-common's (`crypto/crypto.h`), which runs on AES-NI or the ARMv8 AES instructions, several blocks at a time where the mode allows: the SELF, SPRX and NPDRM decryption (AES-128 CTR, AES-256 CBC), EDAT/SDAT and RAP, the Infinity portal, the encrypted-ISO sectors read during play (CBC, each sector's chain decrypted many blocks at once) and PKG installs, whose stream cipher was one ECB block at a time over a big-endian 128-bit position and is now `aes_ctr128_crypt` over the buffer where it lies. The CTR the SELF loader uses carries through all 128 bits of its random IV, as PolarSSL's did (`aes_ctr128_crypt`, added to libretro-common for this). A CBC length that is not whole blocks still does nothing, as before. `aes.cpp`, `aes.h`, `aesni.cpp` and `aesni.h` are deleted from both builds, and with them `mbedtls_zeroize`, which nothing called any more. Two fixes on the way: `hmac_hash_compare` wrote HMAC-SHA1's 20-byte MAC into a heap buffer of the key's length - 16 bytes for the EDAT keys - and allocated it for every block an EDAT read checks; the MAC goes to the stack now, as `cmac_hash_compare`'s does. And `cmac_hash_forge` was declared with a const output it writes to, so a call through its header could not link. The new test_crypto_core (`make check`, linked as the core is) checks the core's CBC, ECB, CMAC and HMAC helpers against SP 800-38A, FIPS-197 and RFC 4493, that a part-block CBC leaves the output alone, and that the PKG stream on a be_t<u128> counter matches the block-by-block ECB it replaced across a carry out of the low 64 bits; CI checks the old files stay gone.
- libretro-common: synced with RetroArch, for `aes_ctr128_crypt` (`crypto/crypto.h`): AES-CTR with the counter carried through all 128 bits, on the same AES-NI and ARMv8 multi-block paths as `aes_ctr_crypt`.
- rpcs3/Crypto's SHA-1 and HMAC-SHA1 (PolarSSL) are gone; their callers take libretro-common's `sha1_stream_*` and `SHA1Digest` (`lrc_hash.h`) and `hmac_sha1` (`crypto/kdf.h`): the PPU and SPU recompilers' cache hashes, sys_spu's image hashes, the PPU modules', the SELF, EDAT, PKG and TRP checks, PUP's HMAC and the Infinity portal's. libretro-common hashes whole blocks where they lie, with the SHA-NI or ARMv8 SHA-1 instructions where the CPU has them, where PolarSSL's went through its scalar code; a hash is the same either way, so the caches it names stay valid. `sha1.cpp` and `sha1.h` are deleted from both builds. test_crypto_hashes checks SHA-1 against the FIPS 180-1 vectors and HMAC-SHA1 against RFC 2202's (a key longer than a block among them), whole and in pieces; CI checks the old files stay gone.
- rpcs3/Crypto's SHA-256 (mbedtls) is gone; `sha256_get_hash`, which cellGame hashes themes and files with, takes libretro-common's `sha256_stream_*` (`lrc_hash.h`). `sha256.cpp` and `sha256.h` are deleted from both builds. The new test_crypto_hashes checks libretro-common's MD5 and SHA-256 against the FIPS 180-2 and RFC 1321 vectors, whole and fed in uneven pieces across every block edge; CI checks the old files stay gone.
- rpcs3/Crypto's MD5 (mbedtls) is gone; the disc and IRD content checks hash with libretro-common's `MD5_Init`/`MD5_Update`/`MD5_Final` (`lrc_hash.h`), the one LLVM already uses, so the core carries one MD5 instead of two. `md5.cpp` and `md5.h` are deleted from both builds. CI checks they stay gone; test_llvm_jit already checks libretro-common's MD5 against the published vectors.
- Windows: the static build's host TableGen tools link again. libretro-common's CPU feature code, which they link for XXH3, sleeps with `retro_sleep_us`; that is a macro on Linux but a function in `time/rtime.c` on Windows and macOS, and that file was not on their list, so `llvm-min-tblgen.exe` failed with "undefined reference to `retro_sleep_us'". rtime.c is on the list now. The Windows CI job builds with CMake and so never ran this link: the library tests now cross-link the host's libretro-common objects for Windows with MinGW, every object taken in, from the Makefile's own list (`make llvm-host-lrc-srcs`); without rtime.c it fails with the same error.
- Windows: libretro_core.cpp includes winsock2.h before anything else. stdafx.h and the headers after it bring in windows.h, so the winsock2.h it included later warned "Please include winsock2.h before windows.h" on every build (and winsock.h's declarations came first). It is included without WIN32_LEAN_AND_MEAN, as windows.h always was in that file, since the timer resolution calls need its mmsystem.h. Checked with MinGW-w64: the warning is gone, the file compiles.
- LLVM's XXH3 is libretro-common's now, and `lib/Support/xxhash.cpp` and its header are gone. Every caller calls `xxh3_64` (`encodings/xxh3.h`) directly: the StringMap hash every LLVM compile goes by - the PPU and SPU recompilers' too, during play - FoldingSet's and the stable hashes, the DWARF linker's string pool, the PDB writer's, the pass instrumentation's names, the FS discriminators, and the runtime-call names' perfect hash, which TableGen works out at build time with the same function the core then calls. libretro-common's has the short inputs (all a StringMap key mostly is) inline in its header, and SSE2, AVX2, AVX-512 and NEON for long ones, where LLVM's had only SSE2 or NEON and a call for every key. Its 128-bit variant had no users. The host's TableGen tools link libretro-common's XXH3 and its CPU feature detection. test_llvm_jit checks the StringMap hash and the stable hash against XXH3's published values, and a StringMap of a thousand keys.
- libretro-common synced with RetroArch's (libretro/RetroArch 84ed678): the 16 files of it the core has that had fallen behind (lrc_hash, retro_endianness.h, the VFS implementation, rjson, rflac, rvorbis, the crypto, the audio transfer, image.h and the rest) are RetroArch's again, and its XXH3 (`encodings/xxh3.h`, `encoding_xxh3.c`: the canonical algorithm, with SSE2, AVX2, AVX-512 and NEON paths and the short inputs inline) is added and built. A core's libretro-common is RetroArch's, file for file.
- LLVM's own MD5, CRC-32 and XXH64 are gone; libretro-common's are used. The CRC-32 and JamCRC callers (the COFF writer's section checksums, the PDB hashes, the profile and coverage hashes, the symbolizer) call `encoding_crc32`, which takes the CPU's carry-less multiply (PCLMUL/VPCLMUL, ARM CRC) where LLVM went a byte at a time through a table; `lib/Support/CRC.cpp` and `CRC.h` are deleted. The KCFI type ID calls libretro-common's `xxh64`, and LLVM's XXH64 is cut from `xxhash.cpp` (its XXH3 stays until the next change). `llvm::MD5` keeps its API, since its result type carries the MC layer's and DWARF's file checksums through 27 files, but hashes with libretro-common's MD5 (`utils/md5.c`): `MD5.cpp` has none of the algorithm left, only the calls, and the state is libretro-common's `MD5_CTX`, kept as bytes so LLVM's header does not bring in libretro-common's. The host's TableGen tools link the two libretro-common files they now need. test_llvm_jit checks MD5 (whole, midway with `result()` and carried on, across blocks, empty) and the KCFI ID against the published vectors. CI checks the files stay gone.
- LLVM's own SHA-1, SHA-256 and old atomics are gone from the tree (`lib/Support/SHA1.cpp`, `SHA256.cpp`, `Atomic.cpp` and their headers, with `raw_sha1_ostream.h`), and libretro-common is used in their place. The bitcode writer's module hash, the bitcode analyzer's check of it, and the memory profiler's clone hash now use `lrc_hash.h`'s streaming SHA-1 (`sha1_stream_init/update/final`). It hashes whole 64-byte blocks where they lie in the caller's buffer, where LLVM's copied every byte into its own block buffer one at a time, so the module's bytes are hashed in place with no copy. SHA-256 and `llvm::sys::CompareAndSwap`/`MemoryFence` had no users at all; the core's atomics are `retro_atomic`. `gen.py` leaves files marked `#removed` in `unlinked.txt` out of every list, the host's TableGen tools' too, so a regeneration does not bring them back. CI checks the files and their headers stay gone and nothing includes them.
- Semaphores take no lock (4 `std::lock_guard`, and the semaphore's `shared_mutex`). This is the first of the kernel's sync objects done the per-object way. A new primitive, `turn_calls` (`Emu/CPU/turn_calls.c`, C89), gives an object its turn with no lock and no thread of its own. A thread that finds the object free runs its call at once, then the calls others handed it meanwhile, in order, and gives the object up. A thread that finds it taken hands its call over and sleeps on its own record until it has run. Free, as an object mostly is, that costs one compare-and-swap and one exchange, as a lock would; nothing spins and nothing is allocated. A semaphore's queue of waiters, and its count going below zero with them, now change in the semaphore's turn. A waiter queues itself there, then goes to sleep on its own thread, after its turn, since going to sleep is the one scheduler step that belongs to the sleeping thread; a post that wakes it in between cancels that sleep, as sys_mutex already has it. A post takes its waiters off the queue and wakes them in its turn, so a waiter whose timeout comes is never left between the two. A new test has four threads make 50000 calls each on one object: every call runs once, never two at once, and each thread's in order. Hooks in the test build widen the two windows where a call could be missed; the test hangs on either mutant (reported as a stall, not a failure). CI checks sys_semaphore has no lock. Unverified in games.
- Fixed: the Vulkan resource pool (`lf_item_pool`) could still be left looking one slot too far, the case the fix before missed; test_lf_item_pool failed now and then again. A thread that emptied the top slot and lowered the limit could be held up before it gave the slot up. Meanwhile another thread put an item in the slot above (the limit went up again) and took it out: coming down, it stopped at the first thread's slot, still owned. The first thread, which had lowered the limit already, then gave its slot up without looking again. Now every thread that gives a slot up looks at the limit again, whether it lowered it or not, and comes down from there if it is now just above its slot. A new test holds a thread up at that point with a hook while the other puts and takes, and fails every run on the old code. A stress run of the old code left the limit wrong in 130 runs of 3000, the new one in none.
- The frontend's files take no lock (7 `std::lock_guard`). A frontend that reads and writes files at an offset (VFS v6) never needed one. An older one has a single position per file handle, shared by seek, read and write, while one file is used from several threads at once (every SPU worker appends to the SPU cache). An operation's seek and its read or write used to stay together under the file's `std::mutex`. They now run on a thread of their own (`serial_call`, a `service_thread_t`), one at a time; the thread is stopped when the game is unloaded and at `retro_deinit`. test_vfs_positional's VFS v3 pass (four threads reading at offsets and appending through such a frontend) still gives every byte right. That pass takes about 2 s longer on this 2-CPU box, since each operation now goes to the other thread and back; current frontends never take that path. CI checks the file has no lock.
- Vulkan, "Async with Interpreter" and "Interpreter only" shader modes: the interpreter's pipelines now take their fixed-function state as dynamic where the device can (`VK_EXT_extended_dynamic_state` 1 to 3, which current NVIDIA, AMD and Mesa drivers have). The interpreter runs any of the game's shaders, so what set one of its pipelines apart from another was the fixed-function state: cull mode, front face, depth and stencil tests and ops, depth bias, primitive restart, primitive type, blending, colour write mask, logic op and depth clamp. Each combination was a pipeline of its own, made on the RSX thread the first time a draw needed it. That was a hitch mid-game, since the interpreter's shaders are large, and the warm-up at boot only made about 36 common combinations. Now one pipeline per set of render targets stands for every such state, and the draw's own values are set on the command buffer once it is bound (`vk_interpreter_dynamic.c`). The warm-up covers those sets of render targets, including the formats it used to leave out for time (RGB565, ARGB1555, RGBA32F, 16-bit and float depth), so a pipeline made mid-game should be rare. It runs while the PPU and SPU modules compile, as before. It also waits on its compiles with `retro_waitaddr` instead of a 16 ms sleep loop. Fields that every interpreter pipeline had as dynamic already (stencil masks and reference, depth bias factors, depth bounds, line width, blend constants) no longer set two pipelines apart either. A device without the extensions keeps one pipeline per state as before. The device is made with the extensions only in the interpreter modes. A new C89 test checks that two passes that differ only in dynamic state key one pipeline, and that each draw gets its own values back; test_shader_corpus now makes such a pipeline on lavapipe. Unverified on real hardware.
- Vulkan, "Async with Interpreter" shader mode: less work per draw. This is the mode that draws with the shader interpreter, instead of skipping the draw, while a shader is still compiling, so elements don't flash black on a cold shader cache. It is still not the default. In this mode draws go back and forth between compiled shaders and the interpreter, and every interpreted draw used to copy the vertex and fragment programs into the interpreter's instruction buffers again (up to 8 KiB and more), whether they had changed or not. Now only what changed goes in: the program changes seen by every draw, the compiled ones included, are gathered, along with the program's few words of state and the buffer it is in (`vk_interpreter_uploads.c`). Each switch between the interpreter and a compiled shader also copied all 468 transform constants (7.5 KiB) again. The whole set last put on the ring is now taken as it is while no constant was written (a count the RSX moves on with each write) and no frame's ring space was given back. This also helps compiled shaders that read the constants by index, which took the whole set again with each new vertex program. A new C89 test checks that a change taken up by a compiled draw still reaches the next interpreted one, and when the constants can be reused. Unverified on real hardware.
- sys_fs_opendir lists the host directory through libretro-common's `retro_dirent` (`retro_opendir_include_hidden`, `retro_readdir`, `retro_dirent_stat`) instead of `fs::dir`, so a frontend's VFS is used when one is given. Each entry's kind, size and time come from the one stat the listing already makes (on Win32 from the search itself), with `fs::get_stat` for an entry it has none for; an entry with no metadata at all, such as a broken symlink, is left out as before. An entry's access and change times are now its modification time; `fs::dir` already gave that as the change time. The entries go straight into the listing, with no blank one made and dropped at the end. Since `retro_dirent` gives no reason when a directory won't open, a stat tells a missing path (`CELL_ENOENT`) from a file (`CELL_ENOTDIR`) and from an empty directory such as a Win32 volume root (listed as empty). An error it can't place no longer stops the emulator. A new test lists a directory with a hidden file, a subdirectory, a split file, a broken symlink and an escaped name, and checks each entry's kind, size and time.
- Fixed: the Vulkan resource pool (`lf_item_pool`) could be left believing a slot was still in use after both of its two top slots were emptied at the same moment. The thread emptying the top one came down to the slot under it, found it still owned by the thread giving it up, and stopped there; that thread had looked at the limit before it came down. Every search of the pool then looked one slot too far, for good. Now a thread that gives a slot up without lowering the limit looks at it again and, if the top has come down to it, comes down from there. A new test has two threads put and take at once 50000 times and checks the limit is back to 0 each time: it failed every run before the fix. This is what made test_lf_item_pool fail now and then.
- sys_fs takes no lock on a mount point (24 `std::lock_guard`, and the mount point's `shared_mutex`). What took a mount point's lock (open, write, close, opendir, stat, mkdir, rmdir, unlink, rename, truncate, chmod, utime, the LSN lock, mount, unmount and the rest) runs on sys_fs's own thread through `fs_call` (`owner_calls`), one at a time, in the order asked. Reads don't go there and stay on the game's thread. A file being read is held by `io_begin`/`io_end`, so a close or rename waits for those reads with `retro_waitaddr` instead of a lock, and loading a PRX, an overlay or an SPU image does the same. The HLE thread's service is now a `service_thread_t` that any module can have its own of; sys_fs is the second. The HDD and Blu-ray read timing (`schedule_read`) moves its busy-until mark on with a compare-and-swap. A rename done for the game hands its error back from sys_fs's thread. cellFs's AIO thread, cellGame and cellSysCache go through `fs_call` too. test_hle_service runs a second service: 1000 calls from another thread, calls from it onto the HLE thread, and a stop and restart. CI checks sys_fs has no lock.
- The log takes no lock, and its writer thread neither sleeps nor spins (14 `std::lock_guard`). The channel registry is made at static initialisation and only read after. The level ceiling is an atomic. Messages logged before `set_init` go on an `mpsc_stack` instead of a vector under the registry's mutex, which every message took until then. The log file's writer thread waits with `retro_waitaddr` for the lines put into its buffer: it used to sleep 10 ms at a time, and spin on `yield` while a line was being put in. `sync` waits for the writer's write-outs instead of spinning. Closing early is asked of the writer, which closes between its writes. A line that finds the 32 MiB buffer full is dropped and counted. The thread logging used to write the buffer out itself, or wait for the writer, and so never waited for the disk only by luck. A new test has four threads log 20000 lines at once: all of them reach the file within 2 s without a sync, each thread's in its order, none twice.
- sys_rsxaudio has no lock left (15 `std::lock_guard`). The rsxaudio object and the pointer to the one being processed belong to the data thread: the game's sys_rsxaudio calls run their part there through `rsxaudio_data_call` (`owner_calls`), and the data thread takes them up at the top of each round. The object's `shared_mutex` and `rsxaudio_obj_upd_m` are gone, and so is the lock the data thread held on the object for each whole period. The data thread's wait on its periods also wakes on its calls' word, so a call is taken up at once even when no timer runs. A new test checks that calls run on the data thread and are taken up at once while it waits with no timer. CI checks the file has no lock.
- The RSX audio hardware's parameters take no lock. They were a `transactional_storage`: a `shared_ptr` swapped under a mutex, with a pool of old copies swept on the host's clock, and each change made a new copy on the heap. Now they belong to the HLE thread. Every change runs there through `hle_call`, one at a time, together with what it sets off (the backend's new ports and the timers' rates). The result is published through a sequence count, which the data thread reads each period. Nothing is allocated for a change. `Utilities/transactional_storage.h` is gone (7 `std::lock_guard`). A new test has three threads changing the parameters at once while the data thread's read runs: no change is lost and no read sees half of one.
- The RSX audio backend thread takes no lock and keeps the guest's time. Its news (the emulator's config may have changed, the stream's ports have, the device may have, the backend failed, its stop) are bits of one word that it takes as one and waits on with `retro_waitaddr`, beside the guest clock's word. That replaces `state_update_m` and its condition variable. It reads the config itself, and the ports reach it through a sequence count. Its rings are made once, at their most, and never made again, so none is taken from under the frontend's pull. That was possible before: a reconfiguration freed and remade the ring the callback reads. What the thread wants of the rings' writing side (a flush, their limits, the dump's ring) is a request the data thread carries out before it next writes; `ringbuf_mutex` is gone. The dump's service periods go by the guest's clock instead of the host's. A new test asks for a flush and checks it is carried out by the writer, and checks the dump's ring is taken up and the ports are posted.
- cellVdec takes no lock of its own (19 `std::lock_guard`, and the decoder context's `shared_mutex`). The pictures for the game go through a 64-slot ring that the decoder thread puts into and the game takes from, with no lock. A new sequence or a close is a flush mark from the decoder thread: the game skips to it and gives those pictures' buffers back. GetPicItem keeps a cursor instead of a flag on each picture. A second game thread taking at the same moment finds nothing that time, as with an empty queue. The decoder thread waits for room on the ring's taken count with `retro_waitaddr`, where it slept 10 ms of host time at a stretch, and a close wakes it. The sequence state moves on by compare-and-swap (StartSeq to resetting, EndSeq to ending), and Close no longer locks and unlocks the mutex as a barrier. test_vdec_frames runs the decoder thread and the game on two threads with a flush every 5000 pictures: the pictures come in order, the decoder never has more than 60 queued, and every buffer ends up back in the pool. CI checks cellVdec has no lock of its own.
- libmixer's HLE takes no lock (12 `std::lock_guard` and its `std::mutex`). The mix buffer and the SSPlayers are the HLE thread's: every call that touches them runs its part there through `hle_call`. cellAANAddData and cellSurMixerSurBusAddData mix the game's samples from its memory straight into the mix buffer, with nothing staged. The HLE's mixer thread is never started (as upstream, which loads the real libmixer), so only the game's own threads ever reached that lock. CI checks libmixer has no lock.
- cellMusicDecodeRead runs on the game's own thread again, with no lock and no trip to the HLE thread. The decoding (its command, read position and decoder) is one thread's at a time, by a word taken with a compare-and-swap that nobody waits for. A read takes it; a read that finds a command running gives no data that time. Commands (start, stop, next, previous, finalize) go into a ring of slots and are run by whoever holds the word next: the thread posting them at once, or the holder before it lets go. Their results reach the game's callback as before. The audio decoder hands its tracks over without its `m_mtx`. Decoded bytes go into 1 MiB chunks that never move once made and are kept from track to track, and its thread publishes how many bytes there are, so the copy goes straight from the decoder's chunks into the game's buffer. A track's number tells the reader when the thread has moved on to the next one. The block times are a fixed array instead of a queue the reader popped. A new test decodes two WAV tracks while a reader takes them: every sample of both, in order.
- cellMusic and cellMusicDecode take no lock of their own (26 `std::lock_guard`). Their state (the selection context, and for the decoder its commands, status and read position) is the HLE thread's: every call that touches it runs its part there through `hle_call`. The callbacks on the game's sysutil thread change the selection context there and call the game's callback after, no longer under a lock. The audio decoder's own thread and its `m_mtx` are the decoder's and stay as they are for now. CI checks neither module has a lock of its own.
- cellGame takes no lock of its own (7 `std::lock_guard`, a reader lock and a `std::mutex`). The disc change manager's two game callbacks are one 64-bit `retro_atomic` word and the disc's state another. The frontend's eject and insert claim the state by compare-and-swap (inserted or ejected to busy), and the game's callback thread gives it back. Its `atomic_t`s are gone too. The insert callback calling cellGameUnregisterDiscChangeCallback no longer needs special handling. The directory of the last game data check is the HLE thread's, set and read there through `hle_call`. The lock on the hdd0 mount point stays: it is sys_fs's. CI checks cellGame has no lock of its own.
- cellAudioOut takes no lock (11 `std::lock_guard`). The part of an output's configuration that can change (state, channels, encoder, downmixer, copy control and the sound mode) is one 128-bit `retro_atomic` word. It is read whole, and changed by compare-and-swap (`audio_out::change_settings`), so cellAudioOutConfigure, the audio reset switching the output off and on, and cellAudio's and the RSX audio path's reads never wait on one another. The list of sound modes is made with the configuration and never changed after, so it needs nothing. CI checks cellAudioOut has no lock.
- sys_io's gate has no spinlock. A pad connected or disconnected goes from retro_run to the game's sys_io event queue through a gate, which was a count of sends under way. sys_config_stop spun on it until no send was left, and only then destroyed the queue. The gate is now one word: the queue's id, a count of changes and an open bit. A send reads it, takes hold of the queue by that id, and sends only if the word has not changed, so it never reaches a queue made since under the same id. sys_config_stop closes the gate and destroys the queue at once. A send still holding the queue finds it gone. CI checks the audio hand-over and the gate have no spin.
- The audio hand-over to the frontend has no spinlock. When cellAudio opened, closed or set up the stream, its thread spun until retro_run was out of a pull or had given back samples it had on loan. Now the backend has a ring of its own, made with it and freed with it, and lends it to cellAudio (`LendRing`/`EndLend` in place of `SetLendRing`), so it is never taken from under a pull. The stream's format is one word that retro_run reads before and after each pull: what it pulled across a change is dropped. The write callback lives in three slots, and a new one is set in the slot that is neither published nor in use by a pull. Neither side waits for the other. The test holds a loan for 50 ms while cellAudio's thread reopens the stream: it takes 0.002 ms, where the spin took the full 50 ms.
- The camera takes no lock and keeps the guest's time. Its state belongs to the camera thread: cellCamera's calls and cellGem's look at the camera's info run their part there (`camera_call`, through `owner_calls`), and `camera_context`'s two `shared_mutex`es are gone (24 `std::lock_guard`). The thread waits with `retro_waitaddr` on its calls' word and the guest clock's, instead of a 1 ms sleep of the host's. CI checks the camera code has no lock and no host clock.
- Fast-forward is smooth and as fast as the game can draw. The last fix gave the game four frames of time at once after each picture. Those went by while the game was still drawing, and its flip then waited for the next frame, which came only after half a frame of real time. So at 30 fps each picture moved the game on two and a half of its own pictures (choppy), and there was never more than one picture per half frame. Now each picture gets exactly the frames it gets at normal speed: one at 60 fps, two at 30, learned while the frontend runs at its normal pace. All but the last frame come straight after the picture, and the last comes as soon as the RSX thread says the game's flip is waiting for it. The game's timed waits (`lv2_obj::wait_timeout`) also wake when a frame is granted. Before, while the clock stood at the end of its frame, each of them slept at least a millisecond of host time, right through the grant. The fast-forward test now draws a game that takes 1 ms per picture: about 280 pictures in 300 ms at both 60 and 30 fps, with exactly one and two frames per picture.
- Fast-forward no longer breaks the sound or stalls loading screens. Every `retro_run` gave the guest another frame of time, whatever the game did with it. In an uncapped fast-forward that meant hundreds of frames a second while the game's threads still ran at the host's speed. A loading screen's timeouts ran out before the loads they waited for, and the game's audio fell periods behind cellAudio's and often never caught up. Now a `retro_run` that comes faster than real time grants a frame only while the game is showing frames: up to four frames past the last one it showed, otherwise at most twice real time. A run that is not owed a frame shows the last frame again, with no VBLANK, no audio pulled and no time for the guest. At the frontend's own pace every frame is still owed, frames shown or not. A new test runs a fast-forward with nothing shown, with a frame shown for every frame, at 30 fps, and at an uneven normal pace.
- The guest's clock starts each frame where it stands, a few microseconds on, and moves at the rate that reaches the frame's end in as long of the host's time as the last frame took: when frames come unevenly it catches up smoothly in the next frame instead of jumping, and it is never more than a frame behind the frames asked for. The time of each grant is read after readers are told it changes, so no thread sees the clock go back. The fast-forward test measures jumps against the host's time between its looks, so a busy machine does not fail it.
- The microphones take no lock and keep the guest's time. Their devices are the microphone thread's: cellMic's calls, cellAvconfExt's device registration and the USB microphone's transfers run their part there (`mic_call`, through `owner_calls`), and the thread's `shared_mutex` is gone. The thread stepped every 256 samples by the host's `steady_clock` with a timed wait; it now steps by the guest's clock and waits with `retro_waitaddr` on its calls' word and the guest clock's. CI checks the microphone code has no lock and no clock of the host's.
- The overlay cursors take no lock (10 `std::lock_guard`). The PS Move cursors' requests are one 128-bit `retro_atomic` word each - position, colour as 8-bit channels, duration, a count and the force flag - which the render thread takes up before it draws; the sys_rsx bitmap cursor's place, visibility, screen size and bitmap address are atomic words, and the render thread makes the bitmap and moves it only when they changed. CI checks the cursors have no lock.
- Fast-forward has sound again. The guest's clock ran at the host's pace inside a frame and then jumped to the frame's end at the next `retro_run`: at 4x, 4 ms of the host's time moved the guest 4 ms and then it jumped 13 ms, two and a half audio periods at once. cellAudio took that for a game that had not fed its buffers in time and played silence for as long as the fast-forward lasted. Now the clock moves through each frame at the pace `retro_run` has been coming (four times the host's in a 4x fast-forward, half in slow motion), still never past the frame granted and never backwards; host times taken as the guest's are converted the same way, in the TSC's ticks. A new test runs a 4x fast-forward: the largest jump is about 2 ms, where it was 13.
- cellScreenshot, cellPhotoImport, cellAvconfExt, cellSearch, cellSysutil's background music and cellVoice keep their state on the HLE thread: every call of theirs that took the module's lock runs there through `hle_call`, and the managers' mutexes are gone. CI's list of the files whose state is the HLE thread's has them too.
- cellAudio takes no lock of its own. Its ports, event queues and shared memory are the cellAudio thread's: the game's calls run their part there (`audio_call`, through `owner_calls`), and the thread runs them at the top of each round and in its waits. cellAudioInit, cellAudioQuit, cellAudioPortOpen and cellAudioPortClose map and unmap memory with the calling PPU thread's syscalls, so they do that on their own thread between their parts on the cellAudio thread; the port is closed there before it is unmapped. The configuration change the frontend asks for is a word the thread looks at and acts on itself, never waited for. `g_audio.mutex`, `lock_audio` and `emu_cfg_upd_m` are gone (11 `std::lock_guard`, and the `unique_lock`s); with another audio provider the thread still runs the calls. CI checks cellAudio has no lock of its own.
- cellHttp, cellImeJp and cellSubDisplay keep their state on the HLE thread: every call of theirs that took the module's lock runs there through `hle_call` (166 `std::lock_guard` fewer), and their managers' mutexes are gone. CI checks the files whose state is the HLE thread's have no lock.
- The HLE modules have a thread of their own (`Emu/Cell/Modules/hle_service.c`, C89): a module whose state only calls touch - from several PPU threads, an overlay, the frontend - keeps it there, and `hle_call` runs each such call on it through `owner_calls`, one at a time, with no lock; a PPU thread waiting on it counts as waiting for `suspend_all`. Started by its first call, stopped when the game is unloaded. The keyboards are first: `KeyboardHandlerBase`'s `std::mutex` is gone (16 `std::lock_guard` in the handler, cellKb and the overlays), and an overlay takes its keys there and acts on them on its own thread. `test_hle_service` (and its TSan build) calls it from four threads over 403 starts and stops.
- cellAudio's periods go by the guest's clock. Its thread timed the audio periods (5.33 ms) on the host's clock and slept between them with `thread_ctrl::wait_for`, so with the frontend paused it went on mixing and sending the game audio events its own clock no longer moved for. Now its ring's time is the guest's slaved clock (`get_hw_clock_time`, as the RSX audio path's), and it waits with `retro_waitaddr` on the guest clock's word for as long of the host's time as the guest's should take; the ports' timestamps, `cellAudioGetPortTimestamp`'s, libmixer's and the acknowledgement's are the guest's time. CI checks cellAudio has no sleep or clock of the host's.
- `Emu/CPU/owner_calls.c` (C89): calls run on one owner thread, so the data it owns takes no lock - the caller's record on its own stack, handed over on an `mpsc_stack`, waited for with `retro_waitaddr`; closed when the owner stops, after which a call runs on the caller's thread. The USB thread's calls use it (its own copy of this is gone); the network threads are next. `test_owner_calls` (and its TSan build) makes 80000 calls from four threads, and closes the owner midway in 2000 short rounds.
- The SPU limits take no lock. `sys_spu_initialize`, thread group create and destroy and raw SPU create and destroy each took the limits' `shared_mutex`, and every create scanned every thread group to check the limits. Now the limits and what is in use are one 64-bit `retro_atomic` word - the physical SPUs, the groups cooperating with the system, the raw SPUs, the two limits, and the groups with a scheduler counted by size - so a check and the change it allows are one compare-and-swap, with no scan. A raw SPU's place is given back when it is cleaned up; a savestate counts the groups and raw SPUs again once they are loaded. A new test checks the word gives the scan's answers for random sets of groups, and that two groups which do not both fit are never both taken from four threads.
- The toy portals - Skylanders, Disney Infinity, Lego Dimensions, Kamen Rider - take no lock (27 `std::lock_guard` fewer, and the Dimensions toypad's own lock and unlock calls): sys_usbd runs every transfer of their devices on the USB thread, so each portal is that thread's alone. CI checks the portals have no lock.
- sys_usbd's lock over its devices, pipes and drivers (`usbh.mutex`, 18 `std::lock_guard`) is gone: those lists and the devices are the USB thread's alone. A syscall, the pad thread connecting an emulated gun or tablet, or anything else runs what touches them on the USB thread (`on_thread`) - handed over on an `mpsc_stack` with a record on the caller's stack, nothing allocated - and waits with `retro_waitaddr` for it to have run; on the USB thread, and libusb's callbacks run there, it runs at once. The pad thread's connect took no lock at all before. The USB thread now runs without libusb too, and when it stops it runs the calls already made; one made after runs where it is made. A new test makes 80000 calls from four threads.
- sys_usbd's transfers take no lock (6 `std::lock_guard` fewer). A syscall claims a free transfer with a compare-and-swap on its busy word; the USB thread writes a completed transfer's result, count and isochronous packets inside the transfer's `retro_seqlock`, which the status syscalls read around; fake transfers to complete are handed to the USB thread on an `mpsc_stack`, the list being the thread's alone with room for every transfer. The USB thread no longer sleeps on the host's clock: it waits with `retro_waitaddr` on its own word and the guest clock's, and the fake transfers' delays and the device scan's four seconds go by the guest's clock, which stops while the frontend is paused. A new test claims transfers from four threads with nearly all of them busy and reads a status while it is written.
- `std::lock_guard` is going from the core's own code (979 left, in 131 files). The tests' check counts them: a merge or a change that adds one fails, and each patch that takes some out lowers `LOCK_GUARD_MAX` in `rpcs3/libretro/tests/Makefile` to what is left.
- The lv2 timer thread's list of timers takes no lock: `sys_timer_create` and `sys_timer_destroy` hand the timer over on a lock-free `mpsc_stack`, with two links each timer carries (nothing allocated), and the thread takes them up in order before each look at the timers; the list's `shared_mutex` is gone. The thread no longer sleeps on the host's clock (`thread_ctrl::wait_for`) and no longer polls every 10 ms while the emulator is paused: it waits with `retro_waitaddr` on its own word (a timer handed over or started) and on the guest clock's, for as long of the host's time as the guest's should take to the next expiry. A new test hands timers over from four threads while the thread takes them up.
- The P2P streams' retransmit thread ("Tcp Over Udp Timeout Manager Thread") has no lock and no clock of the host's. The sockets and the P2P port hand it each message sent, each acknowledgement and each stream closed on a lock-free `mpsc_stack` from libretro-common; the thread alone keeps the messages and round-trip times, where every send and every acknowledgement took its `shared_mutex`. It timed the retransmits on `steady_clock` with timed waits; now it times them on the guest's clock and waits with `retro_waitaddr` on its own word and the guest clock's, as the RSX audio thread does, so no retransmit is counted while the frontend is paused. CI checks the class has no lock and no host clock.
- The game's watchdog (`sys_game_watchdog_start`, `_clear`, `_stop`) has no thread of its own. It was a thread that slept 50 ms at a time on the host's clock and timed the game by it, so it went on counting while the frontend was paused and could restart a game nobody was playing. Now `retro_run` looks at it once a frame on the guest's clock, which stops with the frontend; its state is one 64-bit `retro_atomic` word the syscalls change. CI checks `sys_game.cpp` has no thread and no clock of the host's.
- `atomic_t` out of lv2, part 6: the file's stream lock and the directory's read position (`sys_fs_readdir` claims each entry with a compare-and-swap, so several threads reading one directory each get every entry once), the firmware version and the 16 bytes of board storage in `sys_game`, the counts of ready sockets in `sys_net_bnet_poll` and `_select` and the log-once flags, and the RSX audio backend's callback config (16 bytes, changed with a 128-bit compare-and-swap) are `retro_atomic` words called directly. A new test reads one directory from four threads.
- `sys_memory` has no locks and no `atomic_t` left. The default memory container keeps how much it has given to other containers in the high half of its 64-bit use word, so `sys_memory_get_user_memory_size` reads both in one load instead of taking a reader lock against container creates and destroys and scanning every container; a savestate recounts that half from the containers it loads. The address table's slots are `retro_atomic` pointers, and its two secondary areas are mapped without a mutex: two threads mapping one at once both map, the address stored first is kept and the other is unmapped.
- `atomic_t` out of lv2, part 5: every object's existence count (`lv2_obj::exists`), the memory containers' used size (`take` and `free` are compare-and-swap loops), the shared memory's map count, the virtual memory blocks' physical size and the table of their IDs, the PRX modules' state, the lv2 configuration's state and event IDs, the virtual UART's and USB's initialisation flags are `retro_atomic` words called directly. Savestates write and read the same values as before. A new test takes and gives back a memory container's memory from four threads.
- `atomic_t` out of lv2, part 4: the mutex's and the lightweight mutex's control words - the owner or the signal together with the head of the queue, 16 bytes changed as one - are `retro_atomic_128_t` words called directly. Each change is a 128-bit compare-and-swap loop; a read of one field alone loads only its half, with `retro_atomic_load_acquire_128_lo` and `_hi` from RetroArch's `retro_atomic.h`, which the core's copy now matches. Savestates write and read the same fields as before.
- `atomic_t` out of lv2, part 3: the semaphore's count, the event flag's pattern, the reader-writer lock's owner word and the timer's state, next expiry and period are `retro_atomic` words called directly. Their `fetch_op`, `try_dec` and `compare_and_swap` uses are compare-and-swap loops doing the same; the event flag's check-and-clear is one helper on its word that all four waits go through. The two places that took the old value from a reader-writer lock's `compare_and_swap` get the value the compare-and-swap saw. Savestates hold the same values. The whole check passes (the timers' list test compiles `sys_timer.cpp`); nothing in it runs these syscalls from a PPU thread. Not run with a game.
- `atomic_t` out of lv2, part 2: the SPU thread group's initialisation count, state, exit status, join state, running count, stop count and the count of threads waiting for it to stop are `retro_atomic` words called directly. Changes of the state that went through `fetch_op` and `compare_and_swap` are compare-and-swap loops; the stop count is waited on with `retro_waitaddr`. Savestates hold the same values. Left in the group: its priority (shared with the PPU threads' through the scheduler's templates) and the SPURS running count (waited on through `thread_ctrl::wait_on`). Not run with a game.
- The RSX audio timer has no lock either. Its virtual timers - one for each of the serial and S/PDIF outputs - are the data thread's alone now; a syscall that changes the audio configuration only posts each output's rate, one 64-bit word with how many times the output was turned off beside the rate (so off and on again at the same rate still restarts its periods), and wakes the thread, which takes the rates up before it next works out when its next period is. The mutex that the data thread held while it pulled audio, and that the syscalls took to change a rate, is gone. `test_guest_clock` now starts the data thread before there is a timer and asks for the rate from another thread, as a syscall does. Not run with a game.
- The RSX audio path no longer has timers of its own system's. Its data thread waited for each audio period on a Linux `timerfd` and `epoll`, a Windows waitable timer and event, or a BSD/macOS `kqueue` - three implementations, and `#error "Implement"` for any other system - and ran the period's work whenever the host's timer fired, whether the guest's clock (slaved to retro_run) had got to the period or not: 13 times in 200 ms with the frontend paused, and more often than the guest's time called for while playing. It now waits with libretro-common's `retro_waitaddr`, on its own word (the timers changed, or the thread is stopping) and on the guest clock's, which retro_run now notifies each time it grants a frame; on waking it looks at the guest's clock and does a period's work only when one is due. `test_guest_clock` checks it: one period for each period of the guest's time with retro_run at the declared rate, none while paused, and a cancelled wait ending at once - the old timer failed the first two. `make check` also fails on a timer or event poll of the system's anywhere in the core but `thread_ctrl::wait_for`, in `Utilities/Thread.cpp`, which goes with the thread primitives. The lock around the timer's state is still there. Not run with a game.
- `atomic_t` starts going from `Emu/Cell/lv2` (16 of its 86): the scheduler's ready flag, yield frequency and limit, the preemption count RSX reads, the priority order tag (saved and loaded explicitly now), a mutex's recursion count, the file statistics, the config event's destroyed flag, USB hotplug state and device numbering, the RSX audio context flag, the process's virtual memory total, the external references of shared memory and the event port's busy count are `retro_atomic` words called directly. The virtual memory total is reserved with a compare-and-swap loop where it had a `fetch_op`.
