- No `std::deque` or `std::queue` is left in the core, and the library tests fail if one comes back. The last ones ran before or outside play. The music decoder's timestamps and the patch manager's kept strings are `retro_deque`s; so are the routes the PPU call-stack dump follows through a function, made once a dump for 4096 routes and never moved (a function walked over 41 routes: 48 ns where it took 314). The rest were not queues and became what they were: the savestate compression threads' contexts an array made before the first thread starts (they were added one by one while the earlier threads already ran), a package's install entries a vector with room for every entry first, a module's held allocations a vector, the SPU cache read back a vector (with room reserved by the file's size, 23 to 24.5 ns an entry where the deque took 25 to 27; without that it was slower, 30.5). `package_reader::extract_data` takes its readers as a span, and both callers have one. `test_plain_queues` reads an SPU cache back in the order the deque gave it. The call-stack dump, the package install and the music decoder are compiled, not run.
- Three more places off `std::deque` and `std::queue`. The overlay's messages (eight lists) hold each message by pointer: one going from the waiting list to the screen is no longer moved member by member, and a stand-in of a message's size goes through queued, shown and expired in 55 ns where it took 102; the list check that took a `std::function` is called as what it is. NP's basic events are plain data with one block for their bytes: 20 ns in and out where the `std::queue` took 25 (held by pointer they took 28, so they are not). The DNS hook's made-up replies were a map of queues of vectors, each reply a vector grown five times as it was put together and copied out again when read; a reply is now written once into a block of its own and read straight into the receiving buffer - 25 ns made and read where it took 43 - and is byte for byte the reply it was. `test_held_queues` runs the overlay's and the DNS hook's own code, every message and reply accounted for. NP's events are compiled and timed, not run: the handler needs a server.
- The renderer's per-frame queues are `retro_deque`: RSX's queued flips and host GPU write jobs, and in the Vulkan renderer the frames waiting to be presented, the free occlusion query slots, the spare query pools and the pool of images the GPU is done with. Each was a `std::deque`, taking a block from the heap and giving one back as its contents moved along, on the render thread, all through play. The query slots are made for every slot there is and the image pool for twice its trim size, so neither grows. An image found in the pool and put back, 64 pooled, built with the core's flags: 31 ns where it took 48; a query slot freed and taken 1.5 ns where it took 2.0, in 21 instructions and no call where there were 98 and 4. The pool's images are plain pointers now, deleted or handed on by hand wherever one leaves the pool. `test_plain_queues` runs the flips against the `std::deque` they were and the write jobs through `RSXDMAWriter`'s own code. The Vulkan parts are compiled and not run: nothing here runs the renderer, and this needs a game on a GPU - an image leaked or freed twice would show there.
- A packet received for a P2P datagram socket is copied once on its way to the game's read, where it was copied twice into two vectors and queued in a `std::queue`. The queue is a `retro_deque` of plain packets, each with a block of its own filled straight from the receive buffer and freed when the game has read it. A packet in and out, built with the core's flags: 19 ns where it took 38 at 64 bytes, 41 where it took 76 at 1200. A savestate has the queue as it had it, byte for byte - and no longer empties it: saving took every waiting packet out of the queue, which standalone RPCS3 can afford because it stops after saving, and here lost them for the game that carries on. `test_plain_queues` runs the queue against the one it was and the savestate both ways. Not run with a game on a network.
- A decoded video picture goes from the decoder to the game without the heap or a shared count. cellVdec kept each AU's pictures in a `std::deque` made anew for every AU and the pictures waiting for the game in another, and every frame owned its picture's buffer through a `shared_ptr` to the pool, counted up and down each time a frame moved. The frames are plain data in two `retro_deque`s made with the decoder, the pool is the decoder's own, and a buffer is given back to it by hand wherever a frame leaves a queue: taken by the game, flushed by a new sequence or a close, left by an aborted AU, or still queued when the decoder goes. One picture's trip - decoded, queued, handed on, taken - built with the core's flags: 33 ns where it took 44 to 48, 308 instructions, 6 calls and 1 locked operation where there were 368, 28 and 11. `test_vdec_frames` counts the buffers through 10000 pictures and every one of those ways out. Not run with a movie: that is the check this needs.
- Four more `std::deque`s gone. The timer thread's list of timers is a `retro_deque` made for every timer there can be (8192, 64 KB), each element a held reference kept as plain bytes: creating and destroying a timer no longer takes a block from the heap or gives one back, and takes 4 ns of list work where it took 15 (121 instructions against 146); the thread's pass over the timers is the same 33 instructions and the same time. cellVoice's queues to notify were a map of `std::deque`s and are one list of key and source, written to a savestate as the map was - the map's own code reads it back, and the reverse. cellSysutil's callback abort handles were a `std::deque` used as an array that does not move, and are an array. `sys_tty`'s input queues are removed: only standalone RPCS3's debugger window ever put anything in them, so a read finds no input, as it always did here. `test_held_queues` runs the timer list against the `std::deque` of `shared_ptr` it was for 200000 creates and destroys, counting every reference, and the cellVoice savestate both ways. Not run with a game.
- The plain-data queues are `retro_deque` too: RSX's frame-time and recovered-command histories, `sys_usbd`'s pending events, the Skylander, Infinity, Dimensions and Kamen Rider figures' statuses and replies, and a P2P stream socket's backlog were `std::queue` and `std::deque`, which take a block from the heap and give one back every few dozen elements for as long as they are used. Each is made once for the most it is expected to hold; the ones that had no limit of their own are pushed with `retro_deque`'s new `push_back_growing`, which reserves twice as much only if that is ever reached, so nothing is dropped that was not before. The backlog is written to a savestate as the `std::deque` was, byte for byte. Against the `std::` containers built with the core's flags: RSX's frame-time step (60 times read, the oldest dropped, one added) 40 ns where it took 160, in 146 instructions and 2 calls where there were 345 and 10; a 32-byte reply queued and taken is the same time within the noise (7.3 ns against 7.6), in 107 and 21 instructions where there were 168 and 42, with no block taken every sixteen replies. `test_plain_queues` runs the Skylander portal's own code, the backlog's savestate against a `std::deque`'s, and the other patterns against the containers they were. The header's casts are C++'s when a C++ file includes it, so the warning is no longer turned off around its uses. Not run with a game or with one of these devices.
- The lv2 event queues and the scheduler's lists are libretro-common's `retro_deque` (new there: a double-ended queue of one type in one block, C89), in place of the ring and the vectors that had replaced their `std::deque`s. One container for both, with nothing of the C++ library in it: a queue holds exactly its size, the scheduler's deadlines are inserted in order and the earliest taken off the front in one step where a vector moved the rest down. Against `std::deque` built with the core's flags: an event sent and taken 4.2 ns where it took 5.7 (21 instructions and no call to send one, where there were 41 and a call into the allocator's path); a scheduler step with 4, 16 and 64 threads asleep 8.4, 14.1 and 40.0 ns where it took 11.9, 21.3 and 60.8, in the same order over two million steps; no allocation in either, where `std::deque` made 250000 in four million events. A savestate still has an event queue byte for byte as before, now written without a `std::deque` to copy into; `test_lv2_events` reads a queue back from one. The SPU's and the RSX's reads of an event queue go through the same calls.
- A thread going to sleep with a timeout, or waking from one, allocates nothing. The lv2 scheduler keeps the sleeping threads' deadlines in a list, the earliest first, and another list of threads about to sleep; both were `std::deque`, which takes a block from the heap and gives one back as its contents move along - every few dozen timed sleeps, under the scheduler's lock, all through play. A thread is in each list once at most, so room for as many as there can be threads (100 PPU and 1535 SPU) is set aside when the core is loaded - 26 KB for the deadlines, 13 KB for the other - and the lists never ask for more. The order of the deadlines, and of threads with the same deadline, is as it was. The library tests fail if a `std::deque` comes back into the scheduler or the event queues.
- Sending and receiving an lv2 event allocates nothing. Every event queue kept its events in a `std::deque`, which takes a block from the heap for every sixteen events sent and gives one back for every sixteen received, all through play: games send events constantly, and cellAudio alone sends each of its queues one every 5.3 ms. A queue holds 127 events at most, a number it is given when it is made, so the events are now a ring of that many made with the queue. 100000 events through a queue allocate nothing; a `std::deque` under the same traffic allocates 6259 times (counted in a program of its own, not in the old core). A savestate has the queue byte for byte as it had it. `test_lv2_events` checks the order round the ring, the queue's limit, the allocations, draining, and the savestate's bytes against a `std::deque` of the same events.
- cellAudio's mixer four samples at a time on ARM as well (NEON), and the same number on every machine. A multiply and an add in the mixer stay two operations: GCC fuses them into one on ARM for the build's C standard unless told otherwise, which made a sample's last bit depend on the compiler and on whether a frame went through the vector path. `test_audio_mix` is built for ARM64 and run under emulation in CI (`make -C rpcs3/libretro/tests check-arm64`): the 3240 blocks against the code replaced, none differing; without the instruction to keep them apart 1192 differ there.
- cellAudio's mixer takes a third to two thirds of the time. Every 5.3 ms each open port's block of 256 frames is added into the block that goes on to the frontend; the loop looked at the port's volume setting before every frame (it steps for 13 ms after a cellAudioSetPortLevel) and swapped, scaled and added one sample at a time, with the audio mutex held throughout. The setting cannot change while the mutex is held, so it is read once a block: frames are still mixed one at a time while the volume is on its way, and once it is there - nearly always - four samples at a time (SSE2). A stereo port into stereo takes 126 ns a block where it took 406, a 7.1 port into 7.1 463 where it took 1391, a 7.1 port down to stereo 607 where it took 941 (against the old loops in C, in the same test). It is C89 (`cellAudio_mix.c`). Every sample is the same number, bit for bit: `test_audio_mix` runs 3240 blocks - ports of one, two and eight channels into two, six and eight, each downmix, volumes standing, rising, falling, arriving and not, samples at odd addresses, infinities and denormals among them - against the code replaced, and none differs.
- An emulated controller's poll allocates nothing. The USB controllers, the arcade board and the PS Move's stand-ins hand the configuration a callback for each poll of the pad; it was taken as a `std::function`, made anew every poll, and a callback that holds on to more than two things - the arcade board's, the guns', the PS Move's - was put on the heap for it and freed again: an allocation a poll, mid-game. The pad was passed as a `std::shared_ptr` of its own as well, its count written up and down by every thread polling it. The callback is called as what it is and the pad is borrowed. A poll of the pressed buttons takes 123 ns where it took 148, of all sixteen 211 where it took 250, with a callback small enough not to have been allocated; the others save the allocation too. `test_pad_buttons` counts what 1000 polls with a callback holding six things allocate: 1000 before, none now.
- Reading and writing files through the frontend no longer counts bytes. Every read and write added its length to one counter shared by all threads, and every open to another - statistics nothing ever read. With several threads on files of their own (the SPU workers, the caches, the game's readers) that one counter was the thing they all waited on one another for: the core's own part of a read at an offset took 17 to 20 ns with four threads on four files, and takes 2. The counters are gone, with 340 lines nothing called: a second file class with a `FILE*` fallback and helpers for stat, mkdir, rename and remove that `fs::` never used. `test_vfs_positional` times the four threads (VFS_BENCH=1); the library tests fail if such a counter comes back.
- A retro_run asks the frontend about the light guns only while something reads them: 119 calls into the frontend a run where there were 155. The guns are the PS3's mice, which few games set up; the 36 calls a run (nine a port) were made for every game. They start with the run after a game or an emulated device (the arcade board, a USB gun) sets the mice up, and the mice start from where the guns are then; the arcade shooter mode reads them regardless. Finding the mouse handler each run no longer asks for its type (a `dynamic_cast`). `test_input_poll` counts the calls with and without the mice and checks that a gun no longer read is no gun; `test_mouse_handoff` that nothing is asked before the mice exist and that the mice start at the guns' position, not at a gun that was never read.
- A pad nobody is touching costs next to nothing on either side. Each retro_run handed every pad's buttons and sticks over to the game's threads again - some sixty atomic stores a pad - whether or not anything had changed, and every cellPadGetData read all of it back and went through each button's switch to find that nothing was new. The frontend's thread now hands over only what differs from what it handed over last, and counts those; cellPadGetData works a hand-over into the port's data once and, while the count stands, reads nothing. cellPadGetData with nothing new takes 15 ns where it took 76 to 90, and the game's threads no longer have the pad's cache lines taken from them sixty times a second. What the game gets is the same: a buffer cleared with a button still held has the button in the next data (the hand-over is worked in again), a new pad object does not take the old one's count, and a hand-over that changes nothing the game sees is still worked in once. `test_pad_guest` checks each of those, and that 100 runs of an untouched pad hand over nothing; it fails with the clearing left out, and with the unseen hand-over not kept.
- Reading a pad each retro_run takes a third of the time: 51 ns a pad where it took 142 to 177 (the same machine, minutes apart). Two things went. RPCS3 pushes a stick's circle out toward a square, as a DualShock 3 reports it, with an arctangent, a square root, two sines, a cosine and three powers for each stick of each pad each frame; a stick has 256 by 256 positions, so that is now a table (128 KB), filled once at start from RPCS3's own function and read with one load a stick. And each of a pad's seventeen buttons went through two nested switches to find the frontend's button it stands for; which button is which is worked out when the pad is bound. Both are in the C89 `libretro_pad_values.c`. `test_pad_handoff` runs all 65536 positions of both sticks, with the buttons and pressures changing along, against the code replaced (kept in the test): no button and no stick differs. With the table filled the wrong way round it fails.
- The frontend reads the game's audio from cellAudio's own ring: the copy into a buffer of the core's on the way is gone. Each retro_run copied a frame's worth out of cellAudio's ring (under a compare-and-swap of two counters, copied again if it lost the race) and handed the copy over. retro_run now borrows it where it lies - a pointer into the ring - hands that to the frontend, and gives it back; floats for a frontend that takes none are converted straight from the ring. The ring is libretro-common's lock-free single-writer, single-reader one (`retro_spsc`) behind `libretro_audio_ring.c` (C89), its memory mapped twice back to back so that what is waiting is one piece even across the wrap. The old ring was one byte longer than a whole number of frames: after its first lap every sample lay at an odd address and a frame straddled its end, so it could not have been lent. A flush (the emulator pausing) is a mark the writer leaves and the reader skips to; what the reader has borrowed stays whole until it gives it back. When the ring has less than is due it is copied out and the rest is silence, as before; a platform that cannot map the ring twice copies at the wrap. The RSX audio path keeps its write callback. Handing over a run of 800 stereo frames takes 12 ns where pulling a copy first takes about 60 (against a frontend that does next to nothing with it). `test_audio_ring` (also under ThreadSanitizer) checks order across the wrap for 8-, 24- and 4-byte frames, the room, the flush, and a writer flushing against a reader on another thread; `test_audio_frontend` checks that all 200 of 200 runs hand the frontend memory inside the ring, the samples in order, silence when short, and nothing from before a flush after it; `test_audio_handover` runs the lending path against the old code for nine more cases.
- The game's audio goes to the frontend in one piece a retro_run. A run's worth (800 frames at 60 runs a second) was pulled from cellAudio and handed over 512 frames at a time: two pulls and two calls into the frontend every run, and the speaker layout worked out again for each. It is one pull and one call at any rate from 47 runs a second up, and the layout's speakers are worked out when the audio is opened. This part of the core is now C89 (`libretro_audio_out.c`: the pacing, the silence while nothing plays, the conversion to s16 for a frontend that takes no floats); `LibretroAudioBackend` keeps only what RPCS3's audio interface needs of it. The same samples, the same amount every run. `test_audio_handover` runs it against the code it replaces, kept there as the reference, on the same stream for fourteen cases (floats, s16, the classic stereo callback, 5.1, 7.1, NTSC, 50 and 30 runs a second, silence, short pulls): identical output, half the calls. Against a game's side and a frontend that do next to nothing, a run takes 47 ns where it took 53 with floats handed over, 254 where it took 263 converting to s16; the calls saved cost more in the core, where each pull goes through cellAudio's ring and each call through the frontend. The core's own copy of the multi-channel audio declarations is gone: libretro-common's `libretro.h` has them.
- retro_run no longer waits for the game to tell it of a pad connected or disconnected. The event goes to sys_io's queue for the game's handler thread, and sending it took sys_io's mutex - which a game's thread holds through cellPadEnd, cellKbEnd or cellMouseEnd while it waits for that handler thread to end, and through the calls that start it. The frontend's thread waited with it. The queue is now behind a gate of one atomic word: a send goes through if sys_io is started and is dropped if not, as before, without waiting; the game's thread stopping sys_io closes the gate once no send is under way, and only then destroys the queue. The mutex stays between the game's own threads, as a mutex of the PS3's would. `test_sys_io_gate` sends while a thread holds the mutex (the send did not return before), and stops and starts 200000 times against a thread sending: no send is ever under way at a stop (547 were, with a stop that does not wait for them).
- The PS3 mice (the light guns) take no lock. cellMouse, cellGem, the arcade board and the emulated guns each took the mouse handler's lock to read a mouse, and whoever held it replayed what retro_run had left on a ring per gun. A mouse is now a record read whole and changed whole (`retro_cas_record`), with the data waiting for cellMouseGetData in it; retro_run writes the guns' moves, presses and wheel turns to a log that is read without being emptied, and whichever thread reads a mouse applies what the log has past what the mouse already had. Of several threads taking a mouse's data, each piece goes to one. Those that only want where the mouse is and what it holds (cellGem, the board, the guns) read a small record of their own, which takes the same events the same way. A mouse nothing read while the log went round catches up with where the gun is, as before. Reading a mouse takes 10 to 12 ns where taking the lock and looking at the rings took 18 to 21 (a stand-in for the old code, timed in the same run); cellMouseGetData with nothing waiting takes 6 ns and writes nothing. The mouse's lists are fixed arrays in the record, no longer `std::deque`s; the tablet and raw data lists, which nothing ever filled, are gone, and so is a host-clock timer nothing used. `test_mouse_handoff` has four threads take 20000 moves from one mouse while retro_run makes them (each taken once; 2 checks fail with a lost race not made again), checks cellMouseGetData's and cellMouseGetDataList's ways of taking, the catch-up, and that the mouse never moves back for two readers with the gun flat out, one of them away for longer than the log.
- cellGem's camera tracker hands its results over without a lock, the last lock of cellGem's own. What it finds in a frame - the four spheres, and how many pixels there are of each hue - was written and read under the tracker's lock, which cellGemIsTrackableHue and cellGemGetAllTrackableHues took on every call, with or without a real PS Move. The spheres are one record read whole (a sphere's position always goes with its radius) and each hue's count a word of its own. `test_gem_shared` has three threads read the spheres while they are published 200000 times: none torn (24 of 1.3 million with the record written in place).
- cellGem (the PS Move) takes no lock for what it keeps. Its thirty calls, the Gem thread and the camera tracker shared one lock; what they share - the attributes, the four controllers, the flags and counters - is now one record read whole and changed whole (`retro_cas_record`): a call that reads copies it out, a call that changes it works on a copy and puts it back if the record is still the one it read, and is made again on the newer one if not. A check and the change that follows it are still one step (of four threads calibrating a controller at once, one starts it). Three things upstream's code did under the reader's side of the lock, or under none, are whole changes now: the sharpshooter's firing mode and the sphere's visibility written while reading a controller's state, and cellGemReset and cellGemPrepareVideoConvert, which took no lock at all. cellGemInit marks cellGem as starting until the record is set up, and cellGemEnd as ending until the memory is out of it. A read of the record takes 8 ns where the reader's side of the lock took 16 to 19; a controller's state read that changes nothing costs what the lock did (about 20 ns). The savestate is as it was. The tracker's results and the mouse handler keep their own locks for now. `test_gem_shared` clears a controller's 64 status flags a bit at a time from four threads among four other writers, 300 times over, and has four threads calibrate at once 2000 times; with a lost race not made again the flags are left set.
- An emulated controller reads its buttons from the pad's without a lock. The USB controllers, the arcade board and the PS Move's stand-ins look up, for every button of the pad on every poll, which of their own buttons are on it; that took the configuration's lock for each button - sixteen and more times a poll - and held it while the caller's code ran, so two threads polling waited on one another, and it went through two maps and a set. The mapping is now a small table sorted by pad button, copied whole once a poll (in atomics under a sequence count, written when a configuration is loaded) and searched in place: a poll of the pressed buttons takes 146 ns where it took 204, of all sixteen 253 ns where it took 470. It also holds the buttons' values and not the configuration's own objects, which a poll read while a load was writing them: a poll gets one whole mapping, where it could get the old mapping's buttons with the new one's values. `test_pad_buttons` checks every lookup, that a poll no longer waits for another thread's, and four threads polling while the mapping is made again 20000 times (5 polls of 40000 were mixed before; none now).
- The pad lock is gone. After cellPad, the rest that took `pad::g_pad_mutex` takes nothing: cellGem (the PS Move, 18 places), the emulated USB controllers (Buzz, the guitar, the turntable, the drawing tablet, the GunCon 3, the Top Shot guns, the arcade board), the native overlays' input and the pad thread. They read the pads through what retro_run hands over, which never needed it. Two things the lock had been covering in cellGem are done without it: a controller's external port (its status and device, asked for by several of the game's threads at once) is in atomics, and a DualShock 3 standing in for a PS Move no longer writes that device into the pad's PS Move data or keeps its button combinations in one set shared by every thread. The library tests fail if a merge of upstream's code brings the lock back.
- Version 2 of libretro's Vulkan context negotiation, with a frontend that has it (RetroArch does). With version 1 the frontend made the instance from the core's application info and handed the core a list of device extensions and features to merge into its own. With version 2 the core says what it wants of the instance and of the device, and the frontend's own wrappers create them with what the frontend needs added: its extensions where the GPU has them, its features in the pNext chain, queues of its own. RPCS3's instance extensions (MoltenVK's settings on Apple systems, the debug ones when asked for) can be asked for at all this way. A frontend with only version 1 is given version 1, as before. The core's Vulkan header is libretro-common's (it had a copy of its own). `test_vk_negotiation` is the frontend for both versions, on a software Vulkan driver in CI: the instance and the device each made once, by the frontend's wrapper with version 2 and never by the core, with the extension the frontend added; with the core made to call vkCreateDevice itself it fails.
- cellPad takes no lock. Every cellPad call took the pad lock, so the game's threads calling it took turns. What cellPad keeps of a port (what it last gave the game, the port's setting, what it reported of the device, a custom controller's registration and data) is now one record in the port's pad, read whole and changed whole with libretro-common's `retro_cas_record`: a call is one change of one port, and of two threads at the same port one goes first and the other works on what it left, as the lock had it. A custom (LDD) controller's port goes to exactly one of the threads registering. The savestate is as it was.
  - cellPadGetData with nothing new for the game - most calls - reads the port (4 ns) where it took and gave back the lock (9.5 ns), and writes nothing another thread reads; with something new it puts the port back with one compare-and-swap (28 ns), at most once a frame a pad.
  - With the motion sensors on, cellPadGetData reports every 10 ms with no change of input, as a DualShock 3 does. That was 10 ms of the host's clock: the reports kept the host's pace while fast-forwarding and went on through a pause. It is the PS3's clock, which follows retro_run.
  - cellPadSetActDirect no longer writes the motors' values and the time into the pad's plain fields, which only the standalone's device handlers read: two threads would have raced on them.
  `test_pad_guest` has four threads calling cellPadGetData while the buttons change 500 times (each change given to the game once), two threads changing a port's setting at once, eight threads registering custom controllers on six ports and four registering and unregistering 20000 times, and the sensor reports against both clocks. With a lost race in cellPadGetData not made again, 1087 changes are given for 500.
- A fresh tree builds without thousands of failed compiles: since the last change to the Makefile, `make` tried to build every dependency file it did not have yet from a source named after it.
- A retro_run asks the frontend only for the input something reads. Each run also read the mouse's movement and buttons, the pointer and 65 keyboard keys, and the motion sensors (switched on at start), into state nothing looked at: 78 of the 233 calls into the frontend a run, and six more for the sensors. They are gone: 155 calls a run, and the sensors are left off. Reading the frontend's input is now C89 (`libretro_input.c` in place of the C++): the same values in the same places for the pad and mouse handlers, which borrow them by pointer; 2754 bytes of code where there were 5497, and a poll takes 277 ns where it took 447 against a frontend that answers at once (the best of seven runs each). The line written to the log at each change of a pad's motors went with it. `test_input_poll` counts every call by device and checks each value, the arcade shooter mode, and that the frontend hears of the motors only when they change.
- A tree built before a source was converted from C++ to C builds on: `make` stopped at "No rule to make target" for the C++ file its dependency files still named.
- Files through the frontend take no lock where the frontend reads and writes at an offset (VFS v6, new in RetroArch). The frontend's handle had one position, so every read, write, seek and size on a file locked it and sought first, and the threads sharing a file (every SPU worker appends to the SPU cache) waited on one another. Reads and writes now carry their offset, the position and the size are numbers the core keeps, and an append takes its place at the end with one atomic add. An older frontend, or a platform where the frontend has none, gets the lock as before. `test_vfs_positional` reads at offsets and appends from four threads on one handle with a frontend of each kind, checks every byte and record, and that the v6 frontend's handle is never sought.
- The pad keeps the frontend's precision. Two losses a DualShock 3 does not have:
  - Every button was pressed or not: 255 or 0. A DualShock 3's buttons and triggers are pressure-sensitive, and a trigger or button the frontend reports as analog (RETRO_DEVICE_INDEX_ANALOG_BUTTON) now reaches the game as its pressure: half pressed is 128, where it was 255. A racing game's throttle on R2 was all or nothing.
  - A stick's 16 bits went through two halves of 255 steps, each halved again, truncating both times: pushed all the way left or up read 1, never 0, and the pad's value 0 could not be reached. They go to the pad's 8 bits in one step, rounded: 0 full left or up, 128 at rest, 255 full right or down.
  The conversion is a C89 module of its own (`libretro_pad_values.c`), which reads the frontend's answers where they are rather than from a buffer gathered first; `LibretroPadHandler::process` uses it in place of its own C++ and takes 110 ns a pad a frame where it took 114 (the best of seven runs each), while reading twelve pressures more. `test_pad_values` runs all 65536 stick positions and all 32767 pressures against exact rounding; `test_pad_handoff` checks a half-pressed R2, a button-only Cross and the stick's range through the handler - on the code before, R2 read 255, full left 1, and one of the 256 values was never reached.
- The RSX audio path's periods (`sys_rsxaudio`, for games that go around cellAudio) and the PS Move's update and time-outs (`cellGem`) go by the PS3's clock, which follows retro_run, as the rest of the game's time does: they stop while the frontend is paused and follow fast-forward. The RSX audio ring's timestamps are made from that clock directly, not from a host time converted. `test_guest_clock` checks at all three paces that such a timestamp is the timebase's value then, and that the clock stops through a pause. cellAudio's own periods stay on the host's clock, as they were while the movies were checked.
- Reading the frontend's buttons into the pads allocates nothing. Each retro_run made a map of the 24 buttons and stick directions for each pad and threw it away: 26 allocations a pad a frame. They are read into an array in place. `test_pad_handoff` counts the allocations of a thousand frames of input: 26000 before, none now.
- The files the core installs at start (the game patches, the settings database) are compared with what is on disk a piece at a time, not read whole into a buffer of their own first.
- Movies play again (Demon's Souls' title screen movie and the first of a new game played neither) with the PS3's clock following retro_run. Two faults in that clock:
  - Whenever retro_run came a little faster than the frame rate the core declares - a 60 Hz display with 59.94 declared is enough - the guest's clock was ahead of the host's, and a host time taken as the guest's came out as zero: the lag between the two was kept unsigned. Every timed wait of the game's with a deadline then ended at once, and VBlank and audio timestamps were wrong. The lag is signed; a deadline and the VBlank time read the guest's clock itself.
  - A sleep of the game's was waited out by the host's clock, so in a 4x fast-forward a 20 ms sleep took 81 ms of the game's time. Sleeps and lv2 timers wait at the pace retro_run comes.
  `test_guest_clock` now runs retro_run at the declared rate, a little faster and in fast-forward, and checks at each that a host time taken as the guest's is the guest's time then (all 240 were wrong before, by up to the whole uptime), that a deadline 50 ms away has 50 ms left, and that a 20 ms sleep takes 20 ms of the game's time. Not run with the game here: Demon's Souls' movies are the check.
- The LLVM built into the core builds where valgrind is not installed. Its Linux config said `valgrind/valgrind.h` was there, because the machine it was generated on had it, and the Makefile build stopped at `Valgrind.cpp` on GitHub's runners. The generator leaves it out of the Linux config now; the core does not run valgrind's client requests.
- A first build no longer deletes the embedded sources it made (`generated/libretro_patches.cpp` and the other three) at its end, as make does with intermediate files; the next build found them missing, made them again and fetched the patches again. `make check` fails when they are gone after a build.
- The PS3's clock follows retro_run. Each retro_run grants the game one frame of its time; inside the frame the timebase, the system time, lv2 timers and sleeps run smoothly at the host's rate, as on the console, but the clock stops at the frame's end until the next retro_run, and when retro_run comes faster than real time it is pulled forward to every frame asked for.
  - Pausing RetroArch now stops the game's clock. It used to keep running, and the game saw the whole pause pass at once when it resumed.
  - Fast-forward and slow motion now speed the game's time up and slow it down, where before only VBlank followed them.
  - The game's threads are not stopped at frame boundaries: the console does not stop them, and they wait on each other within a frame. Only their time is bounded.
  - The SPU decrementer, which the LLVM recompiler read off the host's TSC inline, reads the slaved clock.
  `test_guest_clock` checks the clock moves at most a frame through a 120 ms pause and goes on from there, has all ten frames of ten asked for at once, keeps sixty asked for at an uneven pace, moves inside a frame, and never goes backwards on four threads reading it 57 million times while retro_run grants; the same test with the clock left on the host's fails four ways. Not yet run with games.
- The OpenGL renderer runs with frontends that do not have RETRO_ENVIRONMENT_GET_HW_SHARED_CONTEXT_INTERFACE, as it did before: its contexts are then made through the window system behind the frontend's context - WGL on Windows, EGL or GLX elsewhere - the way the core made them until now, and handed out through the same interface (`libretro_gl_fallback.c`, C89). Every window system function is looked up at run time among the frontend's libraries, so the core still links none and needs no window system headers. `test_gl_fallback` makes the frontend's context on surfaceless EGL, checks the fallback finds EGL and makes contexts in its share group, that a texture drawn on one on another thread is seen by the frontend's context, and that with no context current it makes none. A RetroArch from before the call booted the core's OpenGL renderer on GLX and on EGL under Xvfb.
- A boot that fails closes the content. retro_run had nothing to run and returned without a frame, so RetroArch counted none and sat on no picture; it now hands a dupe over every run and, once the boot has failed (the reason said already), asks the frontend to close the content. Run in RetroArch with content that does not boot: it says why and goes back to the menu. `test_failed_boot` runs retro_run with no game and checks a frame each run and the close asked for.
- The Windows build compiles again. Without a Win32 surface the Vulkan headers no longer bring `windows.h`, which `VKDMA.cpp` (VirtualQuery) and `vkutils/shared.cpp` (DebugBreak) had relied on: they include it themselves. The Win32 full-screen-exclusive code of the window swapchain, which the core never makes, is left out of the core. Every Vulkan, OpenGL and core source compiles with MinGW.
- The core knows no window system and links no graphics library. OpenGL is libretro's alone.
  - Every OpenGL function, OpenGL 1.0's included, comes from the frontend's get_proc_address into one table, on every platform. GLEW is gone from the tree; the GL types come from Khronos' `glcorearb.h` and `glext.h`. Nothing links libGL, libOpenGL, libEGL or opengl32 any more.
  - The renderer's contexts, for its own thread and RPCS3's shader compiler threads, come from the frontend through RETRO_ENVIRONMENT_GET_HW_SHARED_CONTEXT_INTERFACE (RetroArch's, from this release on). Eight are made in context_reset and handed out and back without a lock. The core's own EGL, GLX and WGL context code, its X11 run-time loader (`libretro_x11_runtime.cpp`) and their locks are gone.
  - A frontend without that interface cannot run the OpenGL renderer yet: the core says so and does not boot. The renderer driven from retro_run for such a frontend is to come; Vulkan is unaffected.
  - The Vulkan code makes no window system's surface either (no Win32, Xlib, Wayland, Metal or Android surface extension or function): the frontend presents, or the headless swapchain hands frames over.
  - The core's private, older copy of `libretro.h` is gone: it uses libretro-common's, which is RetroArch's.
  `test_gl_contexts` checks the contexts are all made in context_reset on its thread, never one on two threads while twelve threads take and give them back, nothing allocated doing so, none to be had when all are taken, and all destroyed in context_destroy; and that without the interface the renderer says it cannot run. The CI fails on any GL or window system library or function the core needs from the loader. Run in RetroArch under Xvfb with llvmpipe on GLX and EGL, context_reset gets the contexts and boots; not yet run on real hardware.
- The emulator's waits wake on events instead of sleeping. Every change of the emulator's state now wakes whoever waits for it, and so does every retro_run.
  - Unloading, resetting, loading a state and the Vulkan context going away wait for the stop to finish on the state itself, untimed. They used to look every millisecond for up to 5 s of the host's clock.
  - The boot waits for the emulator to be past loading the same way, instead of looking every 100 ms for up to 30 s.
  - A savestate waits for the stop, or for its preparation giving up, the same way, instead of looking every millisecond.
  - RPCS3's own waits (`qt_events_aware_op`) look again at each change of state and at each retro_run instead of sleeping between looks. The second the game gets to answer an exit request from its own menu is counted in the frontend's frames.
  - The core stops the game at once (RPCS3's Kill) instead of asking it to exit first. That grace is a second of the host's clock, and nothing runs the game while the frontend is not calling retro_run.
  - A PKG installs on the loading thread: RetroArch shows nothing while retro_load_game runs, so the progress messages a second thread polled for every 50 ms were never seen.
  - RPCS3's Stop Watchdog thread, which looked every 5 ms to report a slow stop to a UI the core does not have, and a 30 ms sleep at the stop, are left out of the core.
  `test_emu_waits` checks a wait does not look again while nothing happens, looks once per retro_run, and wakes at a change of the emulator's state. `no_host_clock.sh`, run with the library tests, fails when the core's own code reads the host's clock or sleeps; the photo's file name, which is the date, is marked as not pacing.
- The Windows build links again. The OpenGL frames' header brought in GLEW's, which turned every GL call in `libretro_video.cpp` into a call through GLEW, and the Windows build has no GLEW: 176 undefined references to `__glewUseProgram` and the rest. On Windows the header now takes the GL types as RPCS3's OpenGL.h does there (`gl.h` and `glext.h`), so the calls stay RPCS3's own. A MinGW compile of `libretro_video.cpp` had 765 references to GLEW before, none after.
- No host clock and no sleep left in what retro_run drives.
  - The pause watchdog is gone. It read the host's clock on a thread of its own to tell when RetroArch had stopped calling retro_run, and paused the game then; nothing needs it now that VBlank comes only from retro_run, so a frontend that stops calling it stops the game where it stands.
  - The Vulkan renderer no longer waits for retro_run to take a frame, nor sleeps while the frontend is away: a finished frame is queued, the newest two kept, and the renderer goes on. The game is held by VBlank, not by the handoff.
  - RSX's VBlank thread waits on the count of VBlanks retro_run has asked for, untimed, instead of polling it every millisecond.
  - Rumble the game does not renew stops after 3 s of retro_runs at the core's frame rate, counted by retro_run, not 3 s of the host's clock.
  - The stall report, which logged when no frame had come for a few seconds of the host's clock, is gone with its frame counters.
  `test_vk_handoff` checks the renderer hands 100 frames over without retro_run running and the newest two come out; `test_pad_handoff` checks rumble stops 91 runs after the last request at 30 fps (3 s), lasts again when asked again, and goes on when renewed.
- RetroArch paces the game, and nothing in the core does.
  - VBlank comes only from retro_run, one per run (two for a 30 fps game of the VBlank list). RPCS3's own VBlank timer, which posted VBlank by the host's clock and was the default, is gone from the core, along with the Frame Pacing option.
  - A flip waits for the next VBlank as on a PS3 (RPCS3's PS3 Native limit), so a game that does not wait for the refresh itself is held to it by the emulated hardware. RPCS3's frame limiter, which slept the renderer by the host's clock, is gone, along with the Frame Limit option.
  - Each retro_run hands over exactly one frame's worth of audio (48000 over the frames a second the core declares, the fractions carried), and silence the same length while the game's audio is stopped. It used to hand over as much as had played in real time since the last run, read off the host's clock.
  `test_audio_frontend` runs retro_run back to back and checks exactly 800 samples a run at 60 fps, 800 or 801 at 59.94 adding up to the right total over 5005 runs, and silence of the same length while paused. cellAudio still mixes on the host's clock; driving it, and the PS3's other clocks, from retro_run is to come.
- The core's own code keeps to its 4 KiB stack frame, and the Makefile build enforces it. Three functions went past it:
  - the cellRec provider thread, with its two 2 KiB audio blocks on the stack (now made once when recording starts);
  - the music decoder's thread, through `std::random_device`, about 5 KiB, used only to seed a shuffle (now seeded from the clock);
  - the Patch Manager's `libretro_patches::collect`, which built two 2 KiB option definitions on the stack (now filled in place in their vector).
  The libretro code and the RPCS3 files reworked for the core (cellRec, media_utils, video_provider, VKLibretro, MouseHandler) now build with `-Werror=frame-larger-than=4096`. The old cellRec does not build with it.
- OpenGL: the renderer's frame textures are freed when the frontend tears down its context. The textures and depth buffers the renderer draws into (three of them since the shared-textures change, one before) were never deleted, so each content load and video driver restart left them behind. They belong to the share group, so they are now deleted from the frontend's context in `context_destroy`, once the renderer's contexts are gone, together with the frontend's own framebuffers over them and any fences left. The handoff then starts over as at first. `test_gl_frames` checks that the teardown leaves only the frontend's own texture; without the delete, the three frames' textures are still there.
- A dialog's interception of the pads is no longer undone by retro_run. A dialog (save data, a message, the overlays) sets "intercepted" and "ignore input" on the pads from the game's side. retro_run counts the pads and clears "ignore input" once no button is held and nothing intercepts. These were plain fields that retro_run wrote without the pad lock. retro_run could clear "ignore input" right after a dialog had set it again, so the button that closed one dialog reached the game while the next was open. The fields are retro_atomics now, with "intercepted" and "ignore input" in one word: the dialog sets both at once, and retro_run clears "ignore input" with a compare-and-swap that fails if the word changed since it looked. `test_pad_handoff` now also runs 20000 interceptions against retro_run. With the old plain write it loses "ignore input" hundreds of times; now it loses none.
- OpenGL: the renderer no longer draws into the texture the frontend is copying. It drew every frame into one texture shared with the frontend's context, and retro_run copied from that same texture, so a frame could reach the screen mixed with the next one drawn over it. There are now three textures, handed between the threads with one atomic exchange each and fenced on the GPU in both directions (`rpcs3/libretro/libretro_gl_frames.c`, C89, taking its GL functions from the caller). The renderer draws into one. retro_run copies the newest finished one once the GPU has drawn it. The renderer reuses a texture only once the GPU has finished the frontend's copy of it. Neither thread waits for the other. A texture is resized only when a frame of another size is drawn into it, and the frontend is told the size of the frame it was handed. A flip with nothing drawn hands over nothing, and the renderer's per-flip fence and frame counting are gone. `test_gl_frames` (in the library tests, on Mesa's software OpenGL through surfaceless EGL) runs a renderer and a frontend context on two threads and checks every frame shown is whole, newer than the last, and at the size it was drawn. With one texture, as before, it shows torn frames. Mesa's software rasteriser serialises GPU work by itself, so the fences are only exercised for real on a GPU.
- OpenGL: retro_run no longer waits on the CPU for the renderer's frame, and the renderer no longer checks and logs its framebuffer on every flip. Before blitting, retro_run used to wait up to 1 ms on the renderer's fence (`glClientWaitSync`), and past that blitted a frame the GPU had perhaps not finished. It now queues the wait on the GPU (`glWaitSync`), so the blit runs once the frame is drawn and retro_run never waits. On each of its several binds per flip, the renderer asked the driver for its framebuffer's status, which makes the driver validate it. It also logged a line for the first 120 binds and every 60th after. It now asks only when the framebuffer is a new one, and logs only when the framebuffer is not complete.
- The core's libretro code uses retro_atomic throughout. The pause watchdog, PKG installation, the VFS counters, the audio backend's flags, the OpenGL present fence and resource flags, and the presented Vulkan frame size used `std::atomic`. The frame size is now one word holding width and height, so a reader never gets the width of one picture and the height of another. The watchdog keeps its last-run time in milliseconds in a `size_t`, compared unsigned, so 32-bit builds work too. An unused `emu_mutex`, an unused `frame_ready` flag and an unused fence counter are gone. What is left of `atomic_t` there is RPCS3's own interfaces (its callbacks and the pad globals it declares). No behaviour changes; `make check` and the library tests pass as before.
- Software frames (a frontend without the hardware Vulkan context) go to retro_run without a lock or an allocation per frame. The renderer used to copy each readback into a new `std::vector`, 33 MB at 4K, and hand it over under a mutex retro_run also took, with the previous frame freed under that lock. It now copies straight from the mapped readback into a triple buffer (`rpcs3/libretro/libretro_sw_frames.c`, C89) and publishes it with one atomic exchange. retro_run takes the newest finished frame the same way, and it stays whole until the next take, since the frontend may show it again until then. A buffer grows only when a frame outgrows it, at a new resolution. The OpenGL build's and the no-OpenGL build's copies of the old handoff are both gone. `test_sw_frames` (in the library tests, also under ThreadSanitizer) checks that frames taken on one thread while another finishes 20000 are always whole, newer than the last, and unchanged until the next take. Publishing without the exchange makes it fail.
- The Makefile no longer regenerates its embedded sources on every build, so it no longer fetches the game patches from rpcs3.net each time. `embed` rewrites a source only when its contents change, so the source stayed older than `embed`, and make ran the step again on every build: the patches, the settings database, the vblank list and the UI icons. A stamp next to each source now records the run, and a deleted source is made again. `make check` fails if a build leaves any of them to be made again; the old Makefile does.
- Light guns reach the PS3 mice without retro_run taking the mouse lock or allocating. retro_run used to take RPCS3's mouse lock, which cellMouse, cellGem, the USIO board and the emulated guns hold on the game's threads, for every move, press and wheel turn, and each event grew a `std::deque`. Now it puts them on a lock-free ring per gun (libretro-common's `retro_spsc`, set aside when the handler is made). Whoever next reads the mice under the lock first replays them through RPCS3's own mouse code (`MouseHandlerBase::take_pending`), so the game gets the same events as before. If a ring fills while nothing reads the mice, the mouse catches up with where the gun points and what it holds once something does. `test_mouse_handoff` (in `make check`) checks the events the game reads, that retro_run finishes while the game holds the mouse lock, the catch-up after a full ring, and that the mouse only ever moves forward while one thread moves the gun and another reads. Leaving out the catch-up, or moving the mouse under the lock again, makes it fail.
- Input goes from the frontend to the game without the pad lock, and a race on a pad's status is fixed. Every retro_run took `pad::g_pad_mutex`, the lock the game's own threads hold in cellPad, to hand over the buttons and sticks, to count the connected pads and to read the rumble. A game thread holding it, or being switched out while holding it, made retro_run wait. Now:
  - Each pad's buttons and sticks are handed over in retro_atomics under a sequence count. retro_run writes them and never waits. cellPad, cellGem's emulated devices, the GameTablet and RPCS3's overlays copy them whole, and copy again if they caught a write in progress.
  - The rumble the game asks for is published as an atomic.
  - A pad's status (`m_port_status`) is a retro_atomic, changed with atomic or/and. retro_run used to set and clear its connected bit without the lock while the game changed the same field under it (registering or removing an LDD controller), so a change could be lost.
  - The buttons are no longer copied into a vector resized every run.
  `test_pad_handoff` (in `make check`) checks that what is handed over is what is read, including a copilot's buttons merged in. It also checks that the handover finishes while the game holds the pad lock, that 200000 reads during writes never see half of one handover, and that two threads changing the status lose nothing. Taking the lock again in the handover, or dropping the odd count while writing, makes it fail. The PS3 mice (light guns) still share RPCS3's mouse lock with cellMouse.
- The Vulkan images the frontend shows are shared between the renderer and retro_run without a lock. The libretro swapchain took a mutex on every acquire, release and image lookup. It also held that mutex while making new images on a resize, so a retro_run landing then waited for the whole resize. Now the images of one size form a set that the renderer's thread makes and publishes. Which images are held are retro_atomics in the set (`Emu/RSX/VK/vkutils/libretro_images.h`, C89). retro_run reads the published set and gives images back. A set replaced by a resize or restart goes on a lock-free stack, and retro_run frees it once the frontend is past it, as before; the old images stay alive exactly as long as they used to. Only a full teardown waits, on the renderer's thread, for a retro_run reading the set to finish. retro_run's list of images to give back is a fixed ring instead of a `std::deque`. `test_vk_images` (in the library tests, also under ThreadSanitizer) checks that no image is ever held twice between two threads and that every retired set is freed exactly once. Taking the retired sets with a load and a store instead of an exchange makes it fail.
- Frames go from the renderer to RetroArch without a lock or an allocation. The Vulkan handoff (`VKLibretro.cpp`) used to take a mutex several times every frame, on both the renderer's thread and retro_run, and its queue of finished images was a vector that allocated as images were queued and overtaken. With at most two frames ever waiting, the queue is now a single retro_atomic word holding the count and the two image indices, changed with a CAS. The frontend's running and stepping state are retro_atomics too. When the frontend has stopped, the renderer's thread waits for the next retro_run in 1 ms sleeps; retro_run never waits for the renderer, except in a step (Frame Advance, or the run after a pause), where it waits for the frame the game draws with the input it has just polled, as before. The OpenGL path lost a frame wait nothing called, along with the mutex and condition variable it took on every frame. `test_vk_handoff` (in `make check`) checks the order and overtaking, two threads with no frame taken out of order, no allocation, and stepping. The old code fails the allocation check.
- The game's audio goes to the frontend with no lock, no buffering of its own and no copies. retro_run used to wait up to 2 ms on a mutex shared with cellAudio's thread. It also pulled cellAudio's ring 2048 frames at a time into a 500 ms ring of its own, copied that out again and converted it to s16, using about 128 KB of stack. Now it pulls exactly what is due straight from cellAudio's ring into the buffer it hands the frontend. A frontend that takes floats (multi-channel output with `batch_float`, as RetroArch has) gets the game's floats untouched. Otherwise they are converted with libretro-common's `convert_float_to_s16` (newly synced, SIMD). cellAudio's thread opens, closes and sets the callbacks under a retro_atomic flag. retro_run never waits for it: if the flag is held it hands over nothing that run, and the time is owed to the next. `test_audio_frontend` (in `make check`) checks the floats arrive bit for bit, the s16 conversion, and that no run takes a millisecond while cellAudio's thread churns. The old code fails the float check.
- CI checks that the per-game data the core carries reaches the games. This covers RPCS3's game patches, which the patch engine reads from the embedded patch.yml, and the vblank-unlock list used by VBlank Frequency "From List". `test_game_patches` (in `make check`, linked as the core is) reads patch.yml through the patch engine. It checks that every executable and patch a plain scan of the file finds is read, with no error. It also checks that a sample of the games the patches name get their patches as core options (every 25th, since each lookup reads the whole file again). `test_vblank_list` (in the library tests) finds every title ID of vblank_unlock.txt in its block, again with Windows line ends. Its reader is now `rpcs3/libretro/vblank_list.c` (C89). Both lists were found to work; unlike the settings database, nothing was missing.
- The core calls itself ParaLLEl PS3 to the frontend, as its info file already did; it said RPCS3, so RetroArch's menu and title showed "RPCS3". RetroArch keeps a core's overrides, remaps and per-core save folders under that name, so those made under "RPCS3" are now looked for under "ParaLLEl PS3". The core's own files (system/rpcs3) stay where they are. `load_test` (in `make check`) checks the name.
- Recording for games (cellRec) hands frames and audio between threads without locks or per-frame allocation. The RSX renderer, cellAudio, cellRec's own thread and the encoder used to share queues behind mutexes, with a new buffer for every frame. They now pass slots through libretro-common's lock-free `retro_spsc` rings (newly synced). A frame's buffer is swapped in and out of its slot, so after the first few frames no buffer is allocated. Audio goes in blocks of 256 samples into storage set aside when recording starts. A full queue drops the frame, as the old queues did when the encoder fell behind; only cellRec's flush at its close waits for room. Stopping a recording clears the queues without stopping either side. `video_provider`, which hands the renderer's frames and cellAudio's samples to whichever recorder is active, publishes the recorder through an atomic pointer. Replacing it waits on the PPU thread, never on the renderer or cellAudio. `retro_atomic.h` is synced for its C++ casts (RetroArch), which RPCS3's `-Werror=old-style-cast` needs. `test_video_sink` (in the library tests) runs a producer and a consumer through 20000 frames and blocks, also under ThreadSanitizer. It also checks that buffers are recycled, the drop and wait behaviour, the clear and the audio blocks.
- Per-game settings from RPCS3's database are applied again, so Demon's Souls draws its world instead of only its HUD on black. It needs Write Color Buffers, which its entry turns on. The lookup kept pointers to member names that rjson had already reused. Of the 2242 games in the database the core carries, only the first two were ever found; every other game ran on the user's settings alone. The lookup is now `rpcs3/libretro/config_database.c` (C89), which keeps only whether each name is the one it wants. `test_config_database` (in `check` and the library tests) looks up every entry against a second reader; the old lookup misses 2240 of them.
- The Makefile builds LLVM into the core from the 3rdparty/llvm submodule, with make and a compiler and nothing to install, where it needed an LLVM 21 or newer on the system and built a core without the recompilers when it found none (NNshi, on Windows). It builds TableGen for the machine running the build, generates what LLVM's build generates, compiles the libraries the core's components need - the JIT, the passes, the X86 or AArch64 target, the perf listener on Linux - into one archive and links it in; the submodule is fetched once when it is not there. The source lists, flags and TableGen runs, and the headers LLVM's configuration writes for Linux, Windows and macOS, come from LLVM's own build description through rpcs3/libretro/make/llvm/gen.py and are committed, so no CMake is involved. `SYSTEM_LLVM=1` takes the system's LLVM through llvm-config as before; `HAVE_LLVM=0` builds without it. Intel's VTune JIT listener is left out (its sources are not in the submodule). The first build compiles about 2,100 files more (55 minutes on two cores). `make check` now also has the core's JIT compile and run code with the LLVM it was built with (test_llvm_jit). CI builds the core this way, with the LLVM objects kept between runs. Built and checked on Linux x86_64; for Windows, LLVM's system-specific code (all of Support, IR, MC) cross-compiles with MinGW-w64, but a Windows core has not been linked here; macOS is not built yet.
- A core built without LLVM (the Makefile builds one when it finds no usable llvm-config) boots games instead of freezing on "Scanning PPU Modules": the PPU and SPU decoders default to the LLVM recompilers, which such a build does not have, and the game's main thread stopped with "LLVM is not available in this build" at its first PPU module (NNshi, Demon's Souls on Windows). The PPU interpreter and the SPU ASMJIT recompiler (the dynamic interpreter on ARM) take their place, at every configuration the emulator loads and at the core's options. An emulation thread that stops on an error now says so on screen ("The emulation stopped: ...") instead of leaving the last picture. The Makefile's warning says what a build without LLVM means. CI builds and checks the Makefile build without LLVM too.
- Recording for games (cellRec: a game recording its own play, or a clip of it, to an MP4) goes through libretro-common instead of FFmpeg, and FFmpeg is gone from the core: its submodule, its CMake target and finder, the Makefile's download and `SYSTEM_FFMPEG`, and the CI steps that built it for Android and Windows. Video is encoded by libretro-common's MPEG-4 Part 2, H.264 and motion JPEG encoders, audio by its AAC-LC encoder or as u-law or big-endian float PCM, all at the bit rates cellRec asks for, and written by its MP4 muxer straight to the file through filestream. The game's frames, or the renderer's, are scaled to the recording's size by libretro-common's scaler (after a box average when they are at least twice its size) and converted to YUV 4:2:0 by its new RGB conversion. Everything is allocated when a recording starts, except the scaler's tables, made again when the input size changes. Different from before: no B-frames (H.264 is Constrained Baseline, for Main Profile too); motion JPEG steers its quality toward the bit rate frame by frame; u-law recordings are written (FFmpeg's u-law encoder takes only 16-bit samples; the old code asked it for floats and stopped with an error); the first frame shows from the start of the file; a recording holds up to four hours. cellSearch now lists a motion JPEG MP4 as an unknown codec, as it did with FFmpeg, not as MPEG-4. CI records each video codec with each audio format from each kind of input (larger RGBA and BGRA frames, ARGB and YUV frames at the recording's size), has FFmpeg read the files back, and checks the codecs it names, each frame's time (a skipped frame leaves a gap), every frame against the picture (42 to 50 dB PSNR), and the audio against what went in, at no delay (35 to 47 dB SNR; float PCM exact). Not tried with a game yet.
- Music and videos (cellMusicDecode, cellSearch): WMA tracks play from their first sample to their last, at the length the file states. libretro-common's WMA decoder now drops the encoder's priming frame and plays the last frame's tail; standalone RPCS3, through FFmpeg, drops a frame of the audio at the start (46 ms at 44.1 kHz). CI plays WMA1 and WMA2 at four rates against the signal they were encoded from. AVI and WMV videos are listed again, read by libretro-common's new AVI and ASF header reader: their codecs, length and bit rates as ffprobe gives them (CI checks five such files, and test_media XVID and AVC in AVI and WMV2 in ASF). An AVI's MP3 stream lists no bit rate, its header having none (FFmpeg read one from the frames). Not tried with a game yet.
- Music for games (cellMusicDecode: a game playing the user's music, and cellSearch and the media list overlay listing it) goes through libretro-common instead of FFmpeg: a track is found by its bytes, not its extension, and decoded by audio_transfer - MP3, AAC (MP4/M4A and ADTS), WMA, ATRAC3 and ATRAC3plus (.oma, .at3), WAVE, FLAC, Vorbis, Opus, AC-3 - folded to stereo and resampled to 48 kHz with libretro-common's sinc resampler; its title, artist, album, genre, track, disc and date are read by rtags; videos are listed by libretro-common's MP4, Matroska, MPEG program stream, AVI and ASF readers. The file is mapped where it lies, not opened through FFmpeg's I/O. CI plays files FFmpeg wrote - MP3 at 44.1 kHz, AAC, WMA2, FLAC 5.1, mono WAVE at 22.05 kHz, Vorbis at 32 kHz - and the sound is FFmpeg's, resampled as standalone RPCS3 resamples it: within 51 to 83 dB of it, mono at -3 dB on both sides and 5.1 folded as before; the tags are ffprobe's, and the codecs and lengths of the files and of six videos (MPEG-4 and AVC in MP4, AVC in Matroska, MPEG-1 and MPEG-2 program streams with MPEG audio or AC-3) are what cellSearch had from FFmpeg. Different from before: an MP3's length no longer counts its encoder padding (FFmpeg's estimate did); Vorbis, Opus and FLAC in Ogg have their tags listed (FFmpeg gave cellSearch none); the year of a date such as "2004-05-01" is listed, where only a bare year was. Not tried with a game yet.
- Video decoding for games (cellVdec: the movies and cutscenes in AVC, MPEG-2 and MPEG-4/DivX) goes through libretro-common's rh264, rmpeg1_video and rmpeg4_video instead of FFmpeg's decoders, and the pictures are converted for the game by libretro-common's YUV conversion instead of swscale. CI decodes streams FFmpeg encoded, with B pictures, and every picture is FFmpeg's - the same bytes in AVC, within one level in MPEG-2 and MPEG-4 - in display order, with the time stamps and user data of the access unit it came from and its picture type; B-skip and PB-skip drop the pictures they did; RGBA and ARGB come out within 3 levels of swscale's, UYVY the same. RGB is converted in the colour matrix the game asks for (BT.601 or BT.709), where swscale always used BT.601. MPEG-4's frame rate comes from the time between pictures, where FFmpeg's often fell back to 30. The decoded pictures go into buffers kept in a lock-free pool and reused, where a frame was allocated for every picture, and the alpha plane for RGBA output is gone. AVC decodes on one thread, about 80 pictures a second at 1080p on CI's machine. Not tried with a game yet.
- ATRAC3plus audio for games (cellAtracXdec: the music and voices of many PS3 games, and movie sound) is decoded by libretro-common's ratrac3p instead of FFmpeg's decoder. The sound is what it was: CI decodes streams of every channel configuration, mono to 7.1, with a different signal in each channel, and compares them with what the core's FFmpeg (8.1.1) path gave - within 7 millionths of full scale as floats, one step at 16 bits - with every channel in the place it was. A frame is decoded from where it lies in the game's memory, without copying it into the decoder's work memory first, and the decoder is made once for a stream and reset when the game starts it again, where FFmpeg's was set up again at every new sequence. Not tried with a game yet.
- Video post-processing for games (cellVpost: a decoded picture to RGBA for the screen) goes through libretro-common's YUV conversion and scaler instead of FFmpeg's swscale. The picture is converted in the colour matrix (BT.601 or BT.709) and range (full or broadcast) the game asks for, where swscale was always given BT.601 broadcast range. Scaled down, the picture is filtered with a windowed sinc instead of swscale's bilinear, which skipped samples: five times closer to a careful scaling of the same picture on CI's; scaled up it is bilinear, a little closer than before. The alpha the game asks for is put straight into each pixel, where a plane of it was allocated and filled for every picture, and a picture the size of the last takes no memory; one the size of the output is converted straight into the game's buffer. CI compares the conversion with what swscale wrote from the same picture (within 3 levels at one size, in both matrices) and the scaled pictures with a careful scaling of it, and counts the allocations of a repeated picture: none. Not tried with a game yet.
- The RSX blit engine's scaled and converting copies done in software (NV3089 with a scale, or between R5G6B5 and A8R8G8B8: some games' 2D blits, video and HUD layers) go through libretro-common's scaler instead of FFmpeg's swscale, the first of the core's uses of FFmpeg to move. Nearest-sample scaling picks the same samples as before; the "smooth" (first order hold) blits are now true bilinear, where swscale's fast bilinear stepped between columns. A8R8G8B8 to R5G6B5 takes each channel's top bits, where swscale went through YUV and shared colour between neighbouring pixels, which changed a noisy image's colours by up to half their range. The scaler and its buffers are kept from one copy to the next, so a copy the size of the last no longer sets up a new swscale context and allocates: it takes no memory. libretro-common is synced with RetroArch's. CI compares the copies with what swscale wrote from the same pixels (R5G6B5 to A8R8G8B8 the same bytes, nearest upscales the same colours, bilinear within 2 levels), checks every nearest pick and a short source slice, and counts the allocations of a repeated copy: none. Not tried with a game yet.
- JPEG headers for games (cellJpgDecReadHeader) are read right: the module walked a JPEG's segments by lengths read wrong (the high byte times 255, and from the second segment on the marker's own code in place of the length's low byte) and wanted a JFIF segment first, so it refused nearly every JPEG with CELL_JPGDEC_ERROR_HEADER - any with a quantization table before the frame header, which is how encoders write them, and every Exif file - and games could not decode them. Standalone RPCS3 has the same bug. The header is now found by the segments' real lengths, after any application segments (JFIF, Exif, Adobe), from any kind of frame (baseline, progressive, and the rest). CI reads the header of every test JPEG, with an Exif segment longer than 255 bytes, fill bytes and a progressive frame, and every file cut short before its frame header; the old walk refused all 16 test files. The component count and colour space reported stay as they were. Not tried with a game yet.
- Building the core needs no Vulkan or GLEW installed, with the Makefile or CMake, as libretro's other Vulkan cores build: Khronos' Vulkan headers (1.4.350), volk and GLEW (2.3.1) are in the tree (3rdparty/Vulkan-Headers, 3rdparty/volk, 3rdparty/glew). The core no longer links the Vulkan loader: volk loads Vulkan's functions when the core starts, from RetroArch's own `vkGetInstanceProcAddr` when RetroArch's context is Vulkan, else from the system's loader (vulkan-1.dll, libvulkan.so.1, MoltenVK) opened at run time - so the Windows core no longer imports vulkan-1.dll, the Linux core no longer needs libvulkan.so.1, and the macOS core no longer links MoltenVK. GLEW is compiled into the core on Linux and not used on Windows, where the OpenGL renderer loads its functions itself. The Makefile's checks for installed Vulkan and GLEW and its message about what to install are gone, as is the unused MoltenVK project. CI checks that the Linux and macOS cores need no Vulkan library and the Windows core imports no vulkan-1.dll. Not tried with a game yet.
- Makefile: a missing Vulkan or GLEW header or library stops `make` at once, saying which and what to install (in an MSYS2 shell, the pacman command for its packages), where the build ran until the first file that includes it (`vulkan/vulkan.h: No such file or directory`). On Windows, vulkan-1 is linked dynamically: MinGW has it as an import library only, which the core's static link passed over, so the link would have failed once the headers were there; a shared LLVM is linked the same way. The C files among RPCS3's sources are compiled with C flags, not C++'s (eight warnings each), and `ASMJIT_STATIC` is defined once (it warned "redefined" in every file using the JIT). The core's own warnings in libretro_core.cpp and libretro_input.cpp are fixed. CI checks that a missing header and a missing library each stop make with the message.
- JPEG decoding for games (cellJpgDec) writes each row straight into the game's buffer, in the format it asked for, instead of decoding the whole picture into memory of its own and copying it over, and takes its decoder from a pool of eight that finished decodes give back, instead of making one, with its planes and its picture, at every decode. Once the decoders have decoded an image of a size, decoding another no bigger takes no memory; the pool is lock-free and emptied when the emulation ends. Reading the header no longer copies the whole file into new memory first: a JPEG in the game's memory is read where it lies, a file into the pool's memory. A file cut short past its header is decoded as far as it goes, as standalone RPCS3's stb_image decodes it, where it was refused; a broken file leaves the rows decoded before the break in the game's buffer. CI decodes JPEGs in each layout and colour space games ask for and compares every byte with what the module wrote before, and counts the decoder's allocations: none after the first round, where the old way took five an image. Not tried with a game yet.
- Makefile: `make clean` run a second time no longer prints `make: [Makefile:...: clean] Error 1 (ignored)`. The folders it searches for objects include the generated sources' (rpcs3/libretro/generated/), which the first clean removed, and `find` failed on it; only folders that are there are searched now. CI cleans twice after the build and checks neither prints an error.
- Makefile: `make clean`, and the clean-up when the toolchain changes, delete only the build's own objects. They searched the whole tree for `*.o` and `*.d`, which in a checkout with the LLVM submodule found the files of those names that are test data in clang's tree (the fake toolchains in clang/test/Driver/Inputs) and deleted them; `git -C 3rdparty/llvm/llvm checkout -- .` puts them back. `make clean` no longer counts as a change of toolchain.
- The core is built as `parallel_ps3_libretro` (.so, .dll, .dylib) instead of `rpcs3_libretro`, with its own core info file, `parallel_ps3_libretro.info` (in libretro-super's dist/info, and its copy here in rpcs3/libretro/); `rpcs3_libretro.info` is left as it is. The buildbot's CORENAME, the CI scripts, the workflows' artifacts and the Makefile follow. The crash report is written as `parallel_ps3_libretro_crash.log`. The core still calls itself RPCS3 to the frontend (library_name), so the folders RetroArch keeps for it (configuration overrides, remaps, saves and states sorted by core) and the system/rpcs3/ directory stay where they are.
- CI checks in-memory save states: `make check` builds a program linked as the core is and has RPCS3's own serializer write a state into memory the way Save State does, compressed with zstd, and read it back the way Load State does, from the core's buffer through `make_savestate_reader`; a second state goes into the same buffer, which keeps its memory. The Makefile build workflow runs it.
- Makefile: an LLVM is used only when it is built for the system the core is for. In an MSYS2 shell, `/usr/bin/llvm-config` is MSYS's own LLVM, for its POSIX layer; the Makefile took it, and its headers (MSYS's `/usr/include`) against MinGW's broke the build at the first file (`conflicting declaration 'typedef struct _mbstate_t mbstate_t'`). It now looks for MinGW's under `$MINGW_PREFIX` first, and an `llvm-config` whose host is not the core's system - Windows (MinGW), Linux or macOS - is passed over with a message, or stops the build when HAVE_LLVM=1 asks for one.
- Windows (CMake build): the core no longer exports asmjit's and RtMidi's classes along with its libretro functions. Both are built into the core, not DLLs of their own, but asmjit's API was declared dllexport (its CMake variable ASMJIT_STATIC never reached the compiler) and RtMidi was built with RTMIDI_EXPORT; ASMJIT_STATIC is now defined for asmjit and the code that uses it, and RTMIDI_EXPORT is dropped, as the Makefile build has them. Seen on the Makefile's Windows build, where this took some 230 exports off the DLL; not tried on the CMake Windows build yet.
- Makefile, Windows (`platform=win`, MinGW-w64): the core it builds now loads. It needed MinGW's libstdc++-6.dll beside it (the link switched back to shared libraries before the C++ runtime came in), and exported some 230 of asmjit's and RtMidi's functions along with its own; it is now linked statically and exports its libretro functions and the NVIDIA/AMD GPU-selection flags only, and it links GLEW as the CMake build does on MinGW. RtMidi has its MIDI backend on Windows (WinMM) and macOS (CoreMIDI), as RtMidi's own build has by default, for Rock Band 3's MIDI instruments. Checked by building it with MinGW-w64 GCC 13 on Linux and loading it under Wine: it needs Windows' own DLLs, vulkan-1 and opengl32, nothing else. `libretro_vfs.cpp` includes `<sys/stat.h>` on Windows, which GCC's MinGW headers do not bring in by themselves.
- The core builds without CMake too: `make` at the top of the tree, as libretro's other cores build (Makefile, with the source lists in Makefile.common).
